Classification of cohomogeneity-one actions on symmetric spaces of noncompact type Thanks: The first author has been supported by Grant PID2022-138988NB-I00 funded by MICIU/AEI/10.13039/ 501100011033 and by ERDF, EU, and by ED431C 2023/31 (Xunta de Galicia).
Víctor Sanmartín-López, Ivan Solonenko

Abstract
. We complete the classification of isometric cohomogeneity-one actions on all symmetric spaces of noncompact type up to orbit equivalence.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
53C35, 57S20

1.
Introduction

A proper isometric action of a Lie group
G
on a connected Riemannian manifold
M
is said to be of
cohomogeneity one
if it has an orbit of codimension one. Cohomogeneity-one actions have been in the limelight because they can be used to construct Riemannian metrics with special properties: for instance, Einstein metrics, metrics with special holonomy, or metrics of positive sectional curvature (
[
BB82
,
Böh98
,
BS89
,
GWZ08
]
). One usually starts with a cohomogeneity-one action
G\curvearrowright M
and dilates the existing metric
g
along the orbits of
G
to produce a new
G
-invariant metric, which might retain some properties of
g
or gain new ones. Since most geometric structures on manifolds are governed by systems of partial differential equations, one can take advantage of the following principle: if a system of PDEs is invariant under a cohomogeneity-one action, it can be reduced to ODEs. For example, this can be applied to the Ricci flow, since it preserves isometries and hence the property of a metric of being invariant under a cohomogeneity-one action (see, e.g.,
[
BK19
,
BK23
]
). Cohomogeneity-one actions are also an important object of study in submanifold geometry, since their principal orbits are homogeneous hypersurfaces and thus are isoparametric and have constant principal curvatures. Besides, cohomogeneity-one actions are one of the chief examples of hyperpolar (and hence polar) actions. Originally, these attracted interest due to their applications to topology: in
[
Bot56
,
BS58
]
, Bott and Samelson studied so-called variationally complete actions on symmetric spaces, which enabled them to apply Morse theory to the spaces of loops and paths on the ambient space; and in
[
Con71
]
, Conlon showed that hyperpolar actions are variationally complete.

Due to their prominence, it is natural to ask, what are all possible cohomogeneity-one actions on a given Riemannian manifold? This becomes a classification problem when we set a suitable geometric notion of equivalence: two actions are called
orbit-equivalent
if there is an ambient isometry identifying their orbits. This problem is essentially equivalent to classifying homogeneous hypersurfaces up to isometric congruence. In this paper, by
classification
we mean providing a complete and explicit list of actions up to orbit-equivalence—but we do allow some actions in the resulting list to be orbit-equivalent to each other. Figuring out exactly which ones are is referred to as the
problem of congruence
; we do not pursue it here, although we will make occasional remarks about it along the way.

The existence of at least one cohomogeneity-one action (from now on,
C1-action
) implies a large isometry group, so it is sensible to first pose the classification problem for homogeneous spaces and in particular their most well-studied subclass of symmetric spaces. Historically, the first two symmetric spaces where the problem was solved were the Euclidean space
\mathbb{E}^{n}
and the real hyperbolic space
\mathbb{R}H^{n}
. In them, it follows from the classification of isoparametric hypersurfaces—which all turn out to be homogeneous—by Somigliana, Levi-Civita, and Segre for
\mathbb{E}^{n}
(
[
Som19
,
LC37
,
Seg38
]
) and by Cartan for
\mathbb{R}H^{n}
(
[
Car38
]
). The same approach does not work as smoothly for the sphere: famously, there are plenty of inhomogeneous isoparametric hypersurfaces in
\mathbb{S}^{n}
, but more importantly, the classification of isoparametric hypersurfaces in spheres still appears to be unfinished (see
[
Sif17
]
). In
[
HL71
]
, Hsiang and Lawson proved that every C1-action on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
is orbit-equivalent to the action induced by the isotropy representation of a symmetric space of rank 2 and dimension
n+1
. C1-actions on the other simply connected compact symmetric spaces of rank one—that is, on the projective spaces over the real division algebras
\mathbb{C},\mathbb{H}
, and
\mathbb{O}
—were classified by Takagi (
[
Tak73
]
), D’Atri (
[
D’A79
]
), and Iwata (
[
Iwa78
,
Iwa81
]
). In
[
Kol02
]
, Kollross classified C1-actions on all irreducible symmetric spaces of compact type. His proof relied on Dynkin’s classification of maximal subgroups of semisimple Lie groups (
[
Dyn57a
,
Dyn57b
]
). In particular, Kollross showed that the moduli space of C1-actions on any such space is finite. On reducible symmetric spaces of compact type, the classification problem is still open.

On the other hand, the story of C1-actions on symmetric spaces of
noncompact
type has been much more intricate and convoluted. The reason for this is that, informally speaking, noncompact semisimple Lie groups have “way more” subgroups than compact ones. Excluding Cartan’s classification for
\mathbb{R}H^{n}
, the story essentially begins with
[
BB01
]
, where Berndt and Brück constructed a plethora of new examples of C1-actions on symmetric spaces of noncompact type and rank one. In particular, they obtained a one-parameter family of mutually orbit-non-equivalent C1-actions on the complex hyperbolic space
\mathbb{C}H^{n}
, thus showing that the moduli space of such actions can be not only infinite but even nondiscrete. Berndt and Brück’s main tool was a novel method that allows one to construct C1-actions on a given space by solving a certain problem in the representation theory of reductive groups. They went on to solve that problem for
\mathbb{C}H^{n}
and the Cayley hyperbolic plane
\mathbb{O}H^{2}
but left the case of the quaternionic hyperbolic space
\mathbb{H}H^{n}
open as it led to a pretty elaborate problem in quaternion algebra.

A C1-action on a symmetric space of noncompact type can have either one or zero singular orbits. In
[
BT03
]
, Berndt and Tamaru classified C1-actions without singular orbits on all irreducible symmetric spaces of noncompact type. They discovered that there are two distinct types of such actions: a continuous family parametrized by the projectivization of a maximal flat in
M
, and a discrete family parametrized by the simple roots of the restricted root system of
M
. They also solved the problem of congruence for these actions. In a later article
[
BDRT10
]
with Díaz-Ramos, they generalized that result and classified homogeneous hyperpolar foliations on all symmetric spaces of noncompact type. In particular, that extends the classification in
[
BT03
]
to reducible spaces. In
[
Sol21
]
, the second author also solved the problem of congruence in the reducible case. Berndt and Tamaru made another advancement in
[
BT04
]
: they classified C1-actions with a totally geodesic singular orbit on all irreducible symmetric spaces of noncompact type. They showed that such an orbit is almost always a reflective submanifold and then used the classification of those obtained by Leung in
[
Leu75
,
Leu79
]
. Later in
[
BT07
]
, Berndt and Tamaru proved that, in rank one, every C1-action with a non-totally-geodesic singular orbit arises via the construction method introduced by Berndt and Brück. As a consequence, that completed the classification for
\mathbb{C}H^{n}
and
\mathbb{O}H^{2}
. The aforementioned quaternion algebra problem was solved much later in
[
DRDVRV21
]
, which concluded the classification for
\mathbb{H}H^{n}
and thus all rank-one spaces.

The next big development occurred in
[
BT13
]
. In that paper, Berndt and Tamaru generalized the method for constructing C1-actions from
[
BB01
]
to spaces of arbitrary rank—the resulting procedure was given the name
nilpotent construction
. The gist of this method is as follows: given a space
M
, one constructs a series of representations of certain reductive subgroups of the isometry group
I(M)
; in each of those representations, one has to look for subspaces satisfying certain properties—each such subspace gives rise to a C1-action on
M
. We will refer to the problem of finding all such subspaces as the
nilpotent construction problem
. Berndt and Tamaru also invented another procedure—called the
canonical extension
—allowing one to construct actions on spaces of higher rank from those on their certain totally geodesic submanifolds called boundary components. Since a boundary component is itself a symmetric space of noncompact type, one gets an inductive procedure for extending C1-actions into higher rank. The main result of
[
BT13
]
asserts that, on any (possibly reducible) symmetric space of noncompact type, if a C1-action has a non-totally-geodesic singular orbit, it must arise via either the canonical extension or the nilpotent construction. One of the big underpinnings of that result was Mostow’s description of maximal subalgebras of real semisimple Lie algebras in
[
Mos61
]
. By that point, there remained only two obstacles on the path to full classification. The first of them was understanding C1-actions with a totally geodesic singular orbit on
reducible
spaces. This problem was bypassed in
[
DRDVO23
]
by means of Dynkin’s structural result
[
Dyn57b
, Th. 15.1]
(see also
[
Kol02
, Th. 2.1]
) on maximal subalgebras of semisimple Lie algebras—which largely complements the one by Mostow.

To complete the classification, one is left to solve the nilpotent construction problem. To this day, this has only been done in rank one, on some spaces of rank two (
[
BT13
,
BDV15
,
Sol23
]
), as well as on
\SL(n,\mathbb{R})/\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{SO}(n),\,n\geq 3
(
[
DRDVO23
]
). The aim of the current article is to resolve this problem for all symmetric spaces of noncompact type and thus conclude the classification—see the
Main theorem
below.

We now briefly introduce the necessary context and lay out our main result. (For more details, see Sections
2
and
3
.) Let
M=G/K
be a symmetric space of noncompact type, where
G=I^{0}(M)
is the identity component of the isometry group of
M
and
K
is the isotropy group of some point
o\in M
. The isometry Lie algebra
\mathfrak{{g}}
splits into a Cartan decomposition
\mathfrak{{g}}=\mathfrak{{k}}\oplus\mathfrak{{p}}
, where
\mathfrak{{k}}=\Lie(K)
. If we write
\uptheta
for the corresponding Cartan involution and
B
for the Killing form of
\mathfrak{{g}}
, then we can define an inner product on
\mathfrak{{g}}
by the formula
B_{\uptheta}(X,Y)=-B(X,\uptheta Y)
. Pick a maximal abelian subspace
\mathfrak{{a}}
in
\mathfrak{{p}}
. This induces the restricted root space decomposition
\mathfrak{{g}}=\mathfrak{{g}}_{0}\oplus\bigoplus_{\upalpha\in\Upsigma}\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}
with respect to the restricted root system
\Upsigma\subset\mathfrak{{a}}^{*}
. We have
\mathfrak{{g}}_{0}=\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{{a}}
, where
\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}
is the centralizer of
\mathfrak{{a}}
in
\mathfrak{{k}}
. Fix a choice of simple roots
\Uplambda\subset\Upsigma
; this yields a nilpotent subalgebra
\mathfrak{{n}}=\bigoplus_{\upalpha\in\Upsigma^{+}}\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}
of
\mathfrak{{g}}
and an Iwasawa decomposition
\mathfrak{{g}}=\mathfrak{{k}}\oplus\mathfrak{{a}}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}
.

Take a one-dimensional subspace
\ell\subseteq\mathfrak{{a}}
and consider
\mathfrak{{h}}_{\ell}=(\mathfrak{{a}}\ominus\ell)\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}
, where the first summand stands for the orthogonal complement of
\ell
in
\mathfrak{{a}}
. This is a Lie subalgebra of
\mathfrak{{g}}
, and its corresponding Lie subgroup
H_{\ell}
acts on
M
with cohomogeneity one and no singular orbits—hence its orbits induce a Riemannian foliation. Similarly, take a simple root
\upalpha_{i}\in\Uplambda
and any one-dimensional subspace
\ell_{\upalpha_{i}}\subseteq\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha_{i}}
. Then
\mathfrak{{h}}_{\upalpha_{i}}=\mathfrak{{a}}\oplus(\mathfrak{{n}}\ominus\ell_{\upalpha_{i}})
is a Lie subalgebra of
\mathfrak{{g}}
whose corresponding Lie subgroup
H_{\upalpha_{i}}
also acts on
M
with cohomogeneity one and no singular orbits. A different choice of
\ell_{\upalpha_{i}}
in
\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha_{i}}
leads to an orbit-equivalent action.

Suppose for a moment that
M=M_{1}\times M_{2}
, where
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are homothetic symmetric spaces of noncompact type and rank one, and write
\mathfrak{{g}}_{i}
for their isometry Lie algebras. Pick an isomorphism
\upvarphi\colon\mathfrak{{g}}_{1}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 6.3941pt\hskip-6.3941pt\hbox{$\xrightarrow{\phantom{\sim}}$}\hskip-6.3941pt\hskip-4.76384pt\raisebox{0.64444pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\sim\,$}}\hskip-4.76384pt\hskip 6.3941pt}}\mathfrak{{g}}_{2}
and consider the diagonal subalgebra
\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upvarphi}=\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{X+\upvarphi(X)\mid X\in\mathfrak{{g}}_{1}}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}\subset\mathfrak{{g}}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{{g}}_{2}=\mathfrak{{g}}
. Then the corresponding connected Lie subgroup
G_{\upvarphi}\subset G_{1}\times G_{2}=G
(here
G_{i}=I^{0}(M_{i})
) acts on
M
with cohomogeneity one and a totally geodesic singular orbit—we call this a
diagonal C1-action
on
M
.

Coming back to a general
M
, take any subset
\Upphi\subseteq\Uplambda
of simple roots. We can define a subspace
\mathfrak{{a}}_{\Upphi}=\bigcap_{\upalpha\in\Upphi}\Ker(\upalpha)
of
\mathfrak{{a}}
together with its centralizer
\mathfrak{{l}}_{\Upphi}=Z_{\mathfrak{{g}}}(\mathfrak{{a}}_{\Upphi})
and the subalgebra
\mathfrak{{m}}_{\Upphi}=\mathfrak{{l}}_{\Upphi}\ominus\mathfrak{{a}}_{\Upphi}
. The
o
-orbit of the connected Lie subgroup corresponding to
\mathfrak{{m}}_{\Upphi}
is called a
boundary component
of
M
and denoted by
B_{\Upphi}
. This is a totally geodesic submanifold and a symmetric space of noncompact type in its own right. Its restricted root system
\Upsigma_{\Upphi}
can be realized as the root subsystem of
\Upsigma
generated by
\Upphi
. Note that
\Upsigma_{\Upphi}
inherits a choice
\Upphi
of simple roots. This allows us to define a subalgebra
\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi}=\bigoplus_{\uplambda\in\Upsigma^{+}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to8.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.84 -2.08 L 0 10.03}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\hskip-1.0pt}{\hbox{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to8.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.84 -2.08 L 0 10.03}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\hskip-1.0pt}{\hbox{\hbox to2.95pt{\vbox to5.45pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.72499pt\lower-0.22499pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.45pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.77 0 L -0.69 6.92}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\hskip 1.0pt}{\hbox{\hbox to1.9pt{\vbox to3.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.08 0 L 0 4.15}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\Upsigma_{\Upphi}^{+}}\mathfrak{{g}}_{\uplambda}
. The isometry Lie algebra
\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\Upphi}
of
B_{\Upphi}
can be realized as the subalgebra of
\mathfrak{{g}}
generated by
\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}
as
\upalpha
runs through
\Upsigma_{\Upphi}
. The connected Lie subgroup of
G
corresponding to
\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\Upphi}
is denoted by
G^{\prime}_{\Upphi}
and is a finite covering of
I^{0}(B_{\Upphi})
. Let
H_{\Upphi}\subseteq G^{\prime}_{\Upphi}
be a Lie subgroup with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{{h}}_{\Upphi}\subseteq\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\Upphi}
. We can then define a subalgebra
\mathfrak{{h}}_{\Upphi}^{\Uplambda}=\mathfrak{{h}}_{\Upphi}\oplus\mathfrak{{a}}_{\Upphi}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi}
of
\mathfrak{{g}}
, whose corresponding connected Lie subgroup
H_{\Upphi}^{\Uplambda}\subseteq G
acts on
M
with the same cohomogeneity as that of
H_{\Upphi}\curvearrowright B_{\Upphi}
. This procedure is called the
canonical extension
.

Write
\Uplambda=\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{\upalpha_{1},\ldots,\upalpha_{r}}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
, let
H^{1},\ldots,H^{r}
be the dual basis for
\mathfrak{{a}}
, and denote
H^{\Upphi}=\sum_{\upalpha_{i}\in\Upphi}H^{i}
. The subalgebra
\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi}
is graded:
\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi}=\bigoplus_{\upnu\geq 1}\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi}^{\upnu}
, where
\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi}^{\upnu}
is the eigenspace of
\ad(H^{\Upphi})
in
\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi}
with eigenvalue
\upnu
. Define
L_{\Upphi}=Z_{G}(\mathfrak{{a}}_{\Upphi})
and
K_{\Upphi}=K\cap L_{\Upphi}
. The orbit
L_{\Upphi}\cdot o
contains
B_{\Upphi}
and is denoted by
F_{\Upphi}
. The adjoint representation of
K_{\Upphi}
on
\mathfrak{{g}}
is orthogonal and preserves the grading on
\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi}
. For every subspace
\mathfrak{{w}}\subseteq\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi}^{1}
, the sum
\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi,\mathfrak{{w}}}=\mathfrak{{w}}\oplus\bigoplus_{\upnu\geq 2}\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi}^{\upnu}
is a subalgebra, and its corresponding Lie subgroup is denoted by
N_{\Upphi,\mathfrak{{w}}}
. Such a subspace
\mathfrak{{w}}
is called
admissible
if
N_{L_{\Upphi}}(\mathfrak{{w}})
acts transitively on
F_{\Upphi}
and
protohomogeneous
if
N_{K_{\Upphi}}(\mathfrak{{w}})
acts transitively on the unit sphere in
\mathfrak{{v}}=\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi}^{1}\ominus\mathfrak{{w}}
(see Remark
3.4
). If
\mathfrak{{w}}
satisfies these conditions and has
\codim_{\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi}^{1}}(\mathfrak{{w}})\geq 2
, then the subgroup
N^{0}_{L_{\Upphi}}(\mathfrak{{w}})N_{\Upphi,\mathfrak{{w}}}
acts on
M
with cohomogeneity one and has a singular orbit of codimension equal to
\codim_{\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi}^{1}}(\mathfrak{{w}})
. This is called the
nilpotent construction
, and the problem of finding all such subspaces
\mathfrak{{w}}
is called the
nilpotent construction problem
. Note that if two admissible and protohomogeneous subspaces
\mathfrak{{w}},\mathfrak{{w}}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi}^{1}
differ by an element of
K_{\Upphi}
, they give rise to orbit-equivalent actions. For reasons to be explained in Section
3
(see Theorem
3.5
), one only has to consider this problem when
\Upphi
is of the form
\Uplambda\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to8.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.84 -2.08 L 0 10.03}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.84 -2.08 L 0 10.03}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\hskip-1.0pt}{\hbox{\hbox to2.95pt{\vbox to5.45pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.72499pt\lower-0.22499pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.45pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.77 0 L -0.69 6.92}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\hskip 1.0pt}{\hbox{\hbox to1.9pt{\vbox to3.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.08 0 L 0 4.15}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{\upalpha_{j}}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
for some simple root
\upalpha_{j}
. In that case, it is customary to replace
\Upphi
with
j
in subscripts and superscripts—e.g.,
\mathfrak{{a}}_{j}
instead of
\mathfrak{{a}}_{\Upphi}
,
\mathfrak{{n}}_{j,\mathfrak{{w}}}
instead of
\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi,\mathfrak{{w}}}
, and so on. The nilpotent construction problem has only been solved for a handful of spaces, and only on five of them it has given rise to new C1-actions not obtainable via other methods described above. Let us go through each of them briefly.

For rank-one spaces, we have
\Uplambda=\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{\upalpha_{1}}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
and
\mathfrak{{n}}_{1}^{1}=\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha_{1}}
, and every subspace of
\mathfrak{{n}}_{1}^{1}
is trivially admissible. In this case, it is more convenient to describe
\mathfrak{{w}}
in terms of its orthogonal complement
\mathfrak{{v}}
. If
M=\mathbb{C}H^{n+1}
, we have
\mathfrak{{n}}_{1}^{1}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{n},\,K_{1}\simeq\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{U}(n)
, and the representation of
K_{1}
on
\mathfrak{{n}}_{1}^{1}
is the tautological representation of the unitary group. It was shown in
[
BB01
]
that
\mathfrak{{w}}\subseteq\mathfrak{{n}}_{1}^{1}
is protohomogeneous if and only if for every nonzero
v\in\mathfrak{{v}}
, the angle between
Jv
(here
J
is the complex structure on
\mathfrak{{n}}_{1}^{1}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{n}
) and
\mathfrak{{v}}
is a constant not depending on
v
; this angle is then called the
Kähler angle
of
\mathfrak{{v}}
. Such a subspace
\mathfrak{{v}}
—and thus the resulting C1-action—is determined by its Kähler angle and dimension up to congruence by
\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{U}(n)
. Disregarding those subspaces that lead to C1-actions with a totally geodesic singular orbit, we obtain from
[
BB01
]
that the moduli space of C1-actions on
\mathbb{C}H^{n+1}
coming from the nilpotent construction and no other method is given by
\bm{\mathcal{{M}}_{\mathbb{C}H^{n+1}}}=(0,\frac{\uppi}{2})\times\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{2,4,\ldots,2\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}\hskip 1.0pt\sqcup\hskip 1.0pt\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{\frac{\uppi}{2}}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}\times\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{2,3,\ldots,n}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
. If
M=\mathbb{H}H^{n+1}
, the representation
K_{1}\curvearrowright\mathfrak{{n}}_{1}^{1}
is equivalent to the standard representation
\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{Sp}(n)\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{Sp}(1)\curvearrowright\mathbb{H}^{n}
. In that same paper, Berndt and Brück devised a generalization of the notion of Kähler angle to real subspaces of
\mathbb{H}^{n}
, which they called the
quaternion-Kähler angle
. This time, this is a
triple
of angles
(\upvarphi_{1},\upvarphi_{2},\upvarphi_{3})\in[0,\frac{\uppi}{2}]^{3}
. As was shown in
[
DRDVRV21
]
, for every protohomogeneous subspace
\mathfrak{{w}}
of
\mathbb{H}^{n}
,
\mathfrak{{v}}
has constant quaternion-Kähler angle and is
almost
determined by it together with its dimension up to
\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{Sp}(n)\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{Sp}(1)
: for certain angles and dimensions, there might be two noncongruent subspaces. Therefore, the moduli space
\bm{\mathcal{{M}}_{\mathbb{H}H^{n+1}}}
of C1-actions on
\mathbb{H}H^{n+1}
arising from the nilpotent construction but no other method is a certain subset of the disjoint union of a collection of cubes
[0,\frac{\uppi}{2}]^{3}
. That subset (as well as the corresponding subspaces) was given an explicit description in
[
DRDVRV21
]
(see Theorem A therein). If
M=\mathbb{O}H^{2}
, the representation
K_{1}\curvearrowright\mathfrak{{n}}_{1}^{1}
is equivalent to the spin representation
\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{Spin}(7)\curvearrowright\mathbb{R}^{8}
. The corresponding protohomogeneous subspaces were classified in
[
BB01
,
BT07
]
, and the resulting moduli space can be described as
\bm{\mathcal{{M}}_{\mathbb{O}H^{2}}}=\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{2,3,6,7}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}\hskip 1.0pt\sqcup\hskip 1.0pt[0,1]\times\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{4}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
. For each of these rank-one spaces, the C1-action corresponding to
\mathfrak{{w}}
is given on the level of Lie algebras by
\mathfrak{{h}}_{1,\mathfrak{{w}}}=N_{\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}}(\mathfrak{{w}})\oplus\mathfrak{{a}}\oplus\mathfrak{{w}}\oplus\mathfrak{{g}}_{2\upalpha_{1}}
. Finally, consider the rank-two spaces
\mathrm{{G}}_{2}^{2}/\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{SO}(4)
and
\mathrm{{G}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})/\mathrm{{G}}_{2}
. Let us write
\Uplambda=\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{\upalpha_{1},\upalpha_{2}}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
, where
\upalpha_{2}
is the short simple root. As shown in
[
BT13
]
, for either space, the nilpotent construction yields only one new C1-action, which corresponds to the choice
j=2
and
\mathfrak{{w}}=\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{0}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
; the corresponding subgroup
H_{2,0}
has Lie algebra
\mathfrak{{h}}_{2,0}=\mathfrak{{l}}_{2}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}_{2}^{2}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}_{2}^{3}
.

Coming back to the general case one more time, suppose
M=\prod_{i}M_{i}
is the de Rham decomposition of
M
. The group
G
decomposes as
G=\prod_{i}G_{i}
, where
G_{i}=I^{0}(M_{i})
. An isometric C1-action
H\curvearrowright M
is called
decomposable
if it is orbit-equivalent to the action of a subgroup of
G
of the form
H_{i}\times\prod_{l\neq i}G_{l}
for some
i
, where
H_{i}\subset G_{i}
. In this case,
H_{i}
acts on the de Rham factor
M_{i}
also with cohomogeneity one. We are now in a position to formulate the main result of the article.

Main theorem
.

Let
M=G/K
be a symmetric space of noncompact type and
H
a connected Lie group acting on
M
properly and isometrically. Then
H
acts with cohomogeneity one if and only if its action is orbit-equivalent to one of the following:

(1)

The action of
H_{\ell}
for some one-dimensional linear subspace
\ell\subseteq\mathfrak{{a}}
.

(2)

The action of
H_{\upalpha_{i}}
for some simple root
\upalpha_{i}\in\Uplambda
.

(3)

The canonical extension of a C1-action with a totally geodesic singular orbit on an irreducible boundary component
B_{\Upphi}
of
M
.

(4)

The canonical extension of a diagonal C1-action on a reducible rank-2 boundary component
B_{\Upphi}
of
M
whose de Rham factors are homothetic.

(5)

A decomposable action
H_{i}\times\prod_{l\neq i}G_{l}\curvearrowright M_{i}\times\prod_{l\neq i}M_{l}=M
, where the de Rham factor
M_{i}
and the action
H_{i}\curvearrowright M_{i}
are one of the following:

(a)

M_{i}
is isometric to

•

\Gr^{*}(k,\mathbb{C}^{2k+n})\;(k\geq 1,n\geq 2)
,

•

\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{SO}(2n+1,\mathbb{H})/\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{U}(2n+1)\;(n\geq 2)
, or

•

\mathrm{{E}}_{6}^{-14}/\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{Spin}(10)\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{U}(1)
,

and
H_{i}\curvearrowright M_{i}
is the canonical extension of an action on the boundary component
\mathbb{C}H^{n+1}

(
resp.,
\mathbb{C}H^{3}
or
\mathbb{C}H^{5}
)
of
M_{i}
belonging to
\mathcal{{M}}_{\mathbb{C}H^{n+1}}

(
resp.,
\mathcal{{M}}_{\mathbb{C}H^{3}}
or
\mathcal{{M}}_{\mathbb{C}H^{5}}
)
.

(b)

M_{i}\simeq\Gr^{*}(k,\mathbb{H}^{2k+n})\;(k,n\geq 1)
and
H_{i}\curvearrowright M_{i}
is the canonical extension of an action on the boundary component
\mathbb{H}H^{n+1}
of
M_{i}
belonging to
\mathcal{{M}}_{\mathbb{H}H^{n+1}}
.

(c)

M_{i}\simeq\mathbb{O}H^{2}
and
H_{i}\curvearrowright M_{i}
is an action belonging to
\mathcal{{M}}_{\mathbb{O}H^{2}}
.

(d)

M_{i}\simeq\mathrm{{G}}_{2}^{2}/\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{SO}(4)
and
H_{i}=H_{2,0}
.

(e)

M_{i}\simeq\mathrm{{G}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})/\mathrm{{G}}_{2}
and
H_{i}=H_{2,0}
.

Notice that the actions in (
) and (
) are also decomposable (see
[
DRDVO23
, Th. C]
). It is worth pointing out that this theorem does not give a complete description of the moduli space of C1-actions yet, as one still needs to solve the problem of congruence for the actions described in (
)-(
). We will make some occasional remarks about that in Section
3
(see also
[
DRDVO23
, Rem. 1.1]
), but we will not attempt to resolve the problem of congruence in general, since that would go beyond the scope of the paper.

Let us briefly lay out our strategy. In order to prove the
Main theorem
, we will have to resolve the nilpotent construction problem. As our first step, we will tackle the admissibility condition on the subspace
\mathfrak{{w}}
—which has to do with
transitive
actions on symmetric spaces. In the compact irreducible case, such actions were explicitly classified by Onishchik in
[
Oni62
]
. There is little hope of getting a similar result in the noncompact case, as the list would be infinite for most spaces. As a substitute, we obtain a general structural result asserting that any Lie group acting transitively and isometrically on a symmetric space of noncompact type contains a skeleton of a very specific form.

Theorem A
.
1
1

1

In the special cases of
\mathbb{R}H^{n}
and
\mathbb{C}H^{n}
, a similar result was obtained in
[
CLGS09
, Th. 3.1, 4.1]

Let
M=G/K
be a symmetric space of noncompact type and
H\subseteq G
a Lie subgroup acting on
M
transitively. Then there exist an Iwasawa decomposition
\mathfrak{{g}}=\mathfrak{{k}}\oplus\mathfrak{{a}}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}
and a maximal abelian subspace
\mathfrak{{t}}_{0}\subseteq\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}
such that the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{{h}}
of
H
contains a solvable subalgebra of the form
V\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}
, where
V\subseteq\mathfrak{{t}}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{{a}}
projects surjectively onto
\mathfrak{{a}}
.

Let us write
A
and
N
for the connected Lie subgroups of
G
corresponding to
\mathfrak{{a}}
and
\mathfrak{{n}}
, respectively. We have
G=KAN
, which is also called an Iwasawa decomposition. Informally speaking, Theorem
A
says that every transitive group of isometries of
M
contains
N
and “almost contains”
A
for some Iwasawa decomposition of
G
. In regard to the nilpotent construction, this theorem will let us show that the subspace
\mathfrak{{w}}
can always be assumed to respect the restricted root space decomposition of
\mathfrak{{g}}
, which will significantly simplify the problem. Together with the protohomogeneity condition—or rather its Lie-algebraic reformulation—this will let us establish deep connections between the subspace
\mathfrak{{w}}
and root system
\Upsigma
of
M
. For our next step, we observe that we should only care about actions that do not arise via any nontrivial canonical extension. We reinterpret this assumption in terms of how
\mathfrak{{w}}
interacts with the root spaces corresponding to the simple roots—which leads to very strong restrictions on
\Upsigma
, prohibits most choices of
\upalpha_{j}
, and rules out many spaces altogether solely based on their root systems. We then proceed to carry out a careful analysis of the singular orbit of
H_{j,\mathfrak{{w}}}
and its extrinsic geometry within the framework of the solvable model of
M
(once again, see Section
2
for definitions and details). This will give us a much better understanding of whether the singular orbit is totally geodesic—and if it is, such an action can also be disregarded. Having done that, we will be able to rule out most of the remaining spaces simply by looking at their root multiplicities. At that stage, we will only be left to deal with spaces whose root systems are of type
\mathrm{{B}}_{r}
or
\mathrm{{BC}}_{r}
. For these, we will have to resort to a more brute-force approach. We will examine the shape operators of the singular orbit using the Lie bracket relations between the root spaces of
\mathfrak{{g}}
—and deduce that this orbit is bound to be totally geodesic, provided
r>1
. Eventually, all these considerations will let us conclude that the nilpotent construction produces no actions other than the known examples or their canonical extensions, which will prove the
Main theorem
.

In our investigation of the nilpotent construction problem, we obtain two incidental results of independent interest. One of them is Theorem
A
, which we have already introduced above. The other side result concerns certain common classes of submanifolds of
M
and gives them a useful uniform description—also in terms of an Iwasawa decomposition.

Theorem B
.

Let
M=G/K
be a symmetric space of noncompact type and
S\subseteq M
either of the following:

(1)

a complete connected totally geodesic submanifold,

(2)

a singular orbit of a proper isometric C1-action.

Then there exist an Iwasawa decomposition
G=KAN
and a connected Lie subgroup
H\subseteq AN
of the form
H=(H\cap A)\ltimes(H\cap N)
such that
S=H\cdot o
.

Since
AN
acts on
M
simply transitively, we can think of
M
as a solvable Lie group with a left-invariant metric. From this viewpoint, Theorem
B
asserts that every submanifold of
M
of type (
) or (
) can be realized as a Lie subgroup—and is thus a solvmanifold. This makes studying extrinsic and intrinsic geometry of the submanifold considerably easier (see Subsection
2.3
). We expect that part (
) of the theorem might aid the ongoing efforts to classify totally geodesic submanifolds in symmetric spaces (see
[
KRV23
]
and further references therein). Note that submanifolds in (
) and (
) are examples of homogeneous minimal submanifolds (for (
), this is shown in
[
BB01
, Prop. 3]
).

The article is organized as follows. Section
2
consists of preliminaries necessary for understanding everything that follows. In Section
3
, we talk in depth about the known types and methods of construction of C1-actions on symmetric spaces of noncompact type and discuss the current state of their classification. In Section
4
, we deal with the admissibility condition in the nilpotent construction and prove Theorem
A
. This is the backbone of the whole paper that essentially renders the nilpotent construction solvable. Section
5
is dedicated to the proof of Theorem
B
. Finally, in Section
6
, we resolve the nilpotent construction problem and prove the
Main theorem
.

Acknowledgments.
The authors are indebted to Miguel Domínguez-Vázquez for his countless comments and suggestions, as well as to Tomás Otero for his fruitful feedback on Section
4
. The second author is particularly grateful to Miguel for organizing his visit to Santiago de Compostela in May 2024, which largely kick-started the project that led to this paper. I am also thankful to Jürgen Berndt for introducing me to the study of C1-actions and guiding me through it over the years of my PhD. Finally, we would like to thank Hiroshi Tamaru, Alberto Rodríguez-Vázquez, and José Carlos Díaz-Ramos for sharing their insights and valuable advice.

2.
Preliminaries

This section serves as a collection of preliminaries required to understand the rest of the article. We begin with a brief refresher on some aspects of the theory of symmetric spaces of noncompact type in Subsection
2.1
. After that, in Subsection
2.2
, we give a quick overview of the theory of parabolic subgroups of real semisimple Lie groups. Lastly, in Subsection
2.3
, we talk about a certain type of homogeneous submanifolds in symmetric spaces of noncompact type, whose extrinsic geometry such as shape operators can be studied effectively by means of the Iwasawa and restricted root space decompositions.

2.1.
Symmetric spaces of noncompact type

Let
M
be a symmetric space of noncompact type represented by a Riemannian symmetric pair
(G,K)
with
G
a connected noncompact semisimple Lie group and
K\subset G
a maximal compact subgroup. We will usually write this simply as “let
M=G/K
be a symmetric space of noncompact type”.

Remark 2.1
.

By representing
M
as
G/K
, we have a distinguished base point
eK\in M
, whose isotropy group is
K
. However, in certain circumstances, it might be beneficial to change the base point to a new one, say
o\in M
, that is better positioned. In that case, if there is no confusion, we will sometimes commit a slight abuse of notation and reuse the letter
K
for the isotropy group of the new point
o
. This is equivalent to replacing
K
with a conjugate subgroup but keeping the letter.

The
ineffectiveness kernel
of
(G,K)
is the subgroup
I
of elements of
G
that act on
M
trivially. Note that this is a closed normal subgroup. When unambiguous, we will refer to its Lie algebra
\mathfrak{{i}}
, which is an ideal of
\mathfrak{{g}}=\Lie(G)
, as the
ineffectiveness kernel
as well.

Agreement
.

From now on, whenever we have a symmetric space
M
of noncompact type and write it as
M=G/K
, we assume by default that
(G,K)
is an almost effective Riemannian symmetric pair. This means that the ineffectiveness kernel
I\subset G
is finite. It follows a posteriori that
K
is compact and
I
coincides with the center of
G
. We refer to
[
Sol24
, Sect. 2.4.1]
for details.

Fix a base point
o\in M
. The subgroup
K=G_{o}
is the fixed-point set of a unique involutive automorphism
\Uptheta
of
G
. The corresponding automorphism
\uptheta
of
\mathfrak{{g}}
is a Cartan involution, and we will denote its corresponding Cartan decomposition by
\mathfrak{{g}}=\mathfrak{{k}}\oplus\mathfrak{{p}}
. Here
\mathfrak{{k}}=\Ker(\Id_{\mathfrak{{g}}}\hskip-1.0pt-\hskip 1.5pt\uptheta)=\Lie(K)
and
\mathfrak{{p}}=\Ker(\Id_{\mathfrak{{g}}}\hskip-1.0pt+\hskip 1.5pt\uptheta)
. The reductive complement
\mathfrak{{p}}
gets naturally identified with
T_{o}M
by sending
X\in\mathfrak{{p}}
to the value of its corresponding Killing vector field
\widehat{{X}}
at
o
. Having fixed
\uptheta
, we have a
K
-invariant inner product on
\mathfrak{{g}}
given by
B_{\uptheta}(X,Y)=-B(X,\uptheta Y)
, where
B
is the Killing form. Throughout the article, we will use
B_{\uptheta}
as a default inner product on
\mathfrak{{g}}
. We will also denote it by
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{-}\hskip 1.0pt|\hskip 1.5pt{-}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt_{B_{\uptheta}}
or even
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{-}\hskip 1.0pt|\hskip 1.5pt{-}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt
if there is no ground for confusion. Given two subspaces
U\subseteq V\subseteq\mathfrak{{g}}
,
V\ominus U
stands for the orthogonal complement of
U
inside
V
. The summands
\mathfrak{{k}}
and
\mathfrak{{p}}
are orthogonal with respect to both
B_{\uptheta}
and
B
;
B_{\uptheta}
coincides with
B
on
\mathfrak{{p}}\times\mathfrak{{p}}
and equals
-B
on
\mathfrak{{k}}\times\mathfrak{{k}}
. Being a
K
-invariant inner product on
\mathfrak{{p}}\cong T_{o}M
,
{\left.\kern-1.2ptB_{\uptheta}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathfrak{{p}}\times\mathfrak{{p}}}}
can be extended to a
G
-invariant Riemannian metric
g_{B}
on
M
. This is called the
Killing metric
; it is symmetric, does not depend on the choice of a base point, and can be obtained by rescaling the original metric
g
along each de Rham factor of
M
—in fact, any
G
-invariant metric on
M
can be obtained in this way (and is thus also symmetric). The Killing metric enjoys the following property: if two de Rham factors of
M
are homothetic, then they are isometric with respect to
g_{B}
. In general, a
G
-invariant metric on
M
satisfying this property is said to be
almost Killing
. Almost Killing metrics have the largest isometry group (all equal to
I(M,g_{B})
), of which
I(M,g)
is an open subgroup; in particular, we always have
I^{0}(M,g)=I^{0}(M,g_{B})
. It can be shown that
I(M,g_{B})
is naturally isomorphic to
\Aut(\mathfrak{{g}})
(see
[
Sol22
, Sect. 3]
for details).

Pick a maximal abelian subspace
\mathfrak{{a}}
in
\mathfrak{{p}}
. Given
\upalpha\in\mathfrak{{a}}^{*}
, we let

\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}=\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{X\in\mathfrak{{g}}\mid[H,X]=\upalpha(H)X\;\text{for every}\;H\in\mathfrak{{a}}}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\},

and we also put
\Upsigma=\left\{\upalpha\in\mathfrak{{a}}^{*}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to8.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.84 -2.08 L 0 10.03}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\hskip-1.0pt}{\hbox{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to8.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.84 -2.08 L 0 10.03}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\hskip-1.0pt}{\hbox{\hbox to2.95pt{\vbox to5.45pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.72499pt\lower-0.22499pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.45pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.77 0 L -0.69 6.92}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\hskip 1.0pt}{\hbox{\hbox to1.9pt{\vbox to3.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.08 0 L 0 4.15}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\{0\}\mid\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}\neq\{0\}\right\}
. This set is called the restricted root system of
\mathfrak{{g}}
(or
M
) and its elements are called restricted roots. These induce a restricted root space decomposition
\mathfrak{{g}}=\mathfrak{{g}}_{0}\oplus\bigoplus_{\upalpha\in\Upsigma}\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}
. The summands of this decomposition are all pairwise orthogonal with respect to
B_{\uptheta}
, and we can also decompose
\mathfrak{{g}}_{0}=\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{{a}}
, where
\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}=Z_{\mathfrak{{k}}}(\mathfrak{{a}})
. The dimension of
\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}
is called the
multiplicity
of the root
\upalpha
. It is customary to denote
\Upsigma_{0}=\Upsigma\cup\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{0}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
. For any
\upalpha,\upbeta\in\Upsigma_{0},\,\upalpha\neq-\upbeta
, we have
[\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha},\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upbeta}]=\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha+\upbeta}
. This implies that the adjoint representation of
\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}
on
\mathfrak{{g}}
preserves each root space
\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}
. We can restrict
B_{\uptheta}
to an inner product on
\mathfrak{{a}}
and use it to identify
\mathfrak{{a}}
with
\mathfrak{{a}}^{*}
in a standard way, which in turn induces an inner product on
\mathfrak{{a}}^{*}
. With respect to this inner product,
\Upsigma
becomes a root system in
\mathfrak{{a}}^{*}
. We can pull each root
\upalpha\in\Upsigma
back under the isomorphism
\mathfrak{{a}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 6.3941pt\hskip-6.3941pt\hbox{$\xrightarrow{\phantom{\sim}}$}\hskip-6.3941pt\hskip-4.76384pt\raisebox{0.64444pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\sim\,$}}\hskip-4.76384pt\hskip 6.3941pt}}\mathfrak{{a}}^{*}
and define a vector
H_{\upalpha}\in\mathfrak{{a}}
; in other words,
H_{\upalpha}
is determined by
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{H_{\upalpha}}\hskip 1.0pt|\hskip 1.5pt{H}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt=\upalpha(H)=\vcentcolon\langle\hskip 1.0pt{\upalpha}\hskip 0.5pt,{H}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt
for every
H\in\mathfrak{{a}}
.

We make a choice of positive roots
\Upsigma^{+}\subset\Upsigma
and write
\Uplambda=\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{\upalpha_{1},\ldots,\upalpha_{r}}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
for the corresponding set of simple roots. This allows us to define a nilpotent subalgebra
\mathfrak{{n}}=\bigoplus_{\upalpha\in\Upsigma^{+}}\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}
, which leads to a vector space decomposition
\mathfrak{{g}}=\mathfrak{{k}}\oplus\mathfrak{{a}}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}
, also known as an Iwasawa decomposition. We will write
\Upsigma^{-}
for the set
-\Upsigma^{+}
of negative roots and
\DD
for the Dynkin diagram of
\Upsigma
. Any root
\upalpha\in\Upsigma^{+}
can be written as
\upalpha=\sum_{i=1}^{r}n_{i}\upalpha_{i}
with
n_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, and the positive integer
\height(\upalpha)=\sum_{i=1}^{r}n_{i}
is called the
height
of
\upalpha
. Since
\upalpha_{1},\ldots,\upalpha_{r}
form a basis for
\mathfrak{{a}}^{*}
, we can introduce the dual basis
H^{1},\ldots,H^{r}
for
\mathfrak{{a}}
; these vectors are determined by
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{H^{i}}\hskip 1.0pt|\hskip 1.5pt{H_{\upalpha_{j}}}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt=\langle\hskip 1.0pt{H^{i}}\hskip 0.5pt,{\upalpha_{j}}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt=\updelta_{ij}
. By definition, if
\upalpha=\sum_{i=1}^{r}n_{i}\upalpha_{i}
, then
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{H^{i}}\hskip 0.5pt,{\upalpha}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt=n_{i}
. Given a root
\upalpha\in\Upsigma
, we denote
\mathfrak{{k_{\upalpha}}}=\mathfrak{{k}}\cap(\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}\oplus\mathfrak{{g}}_{-\upalpha})
and
\mathfrak{{p_{\upalpha}}}=\mathfrak{{p}}\cap(\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}\oplus\mathfrak{{g}}_{-\upalpha})
. This leads to decompositions
\mathfrak{{k}}=\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}\oplus\bigoplus_{\upalpha\in\Upsigma^{+}}\mathfrak{{k}}_{\upalpha}
and
\mathfrak{{p}}=\mathfrak{{a}}\oplus\bigoplus_{\upalpha\in\Upsigma^{+}}\mathfrak{{p}}_{\upalpha}
. Notice that we have
\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}\oplus\mathfrak{{g}}_{-\upalpha}=\mathfrak{{k}}_{\upalpha}\oplus\mathfrak{{p}}_{\upalpha}
for every
\upalpha\in\Upsigma
.

We write
A
and
N
for the connected Lie subgroups of
G
corresponding to
\mathfrak{{a}}
and
\mathfrak{{n}}
, respectively. These subgroups are closed and simply connected. The multiplication in
G
yields a diffeomorphism
K\times A\times N\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 6.3941pt\hskip-6.3941pt\hbox{$\xrightarrow{\phantom{\sim}}$}\hskip-6.3941pt\hskip-4.76384pt\raisebox{0.64444pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\sim\,$}}\hskip-4.76384pt\hskip 6.3941pt}}G
, which is also called an Iwasawa decomposition. The Lie group
A
normalizes
N
, so the semidirect product
A\ltimes N
is a solvable subgroup of
G
. (We will normally shorten
A\ltimes N
to just
AN
.) The action
AN\curvearrowright M
is simply transitive and the metric pulled back from
M
to
AN
along the diffeomorphism
AN\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 6.3941pt\hskip-6.3941pt\hbox{$\xrightarrow{\phantom{\sim}}$}\hskip-6.3941pt\hskip-4.76384pt\raisebox{0.64444pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\sim\,$}}\hskip-4.76384pt\hskip 6.3941pt}}M,\,an\mapsto an\cdot o,
is left-invariant, hence
M
can be thought of as a simply connected solvable Lie group with a left-invariant metric—we call this the
solvable model
of
M
. The orbit
A\cdot o
is a maximal flat in
M
and it is isometric to the Euclidean space
\mathbb{E}^{r}
, where
r=\dim(\mathfrak{{a}})
is the rank of
M
.

2.2.
Parabolic subalgebras and subgroups

The theory of parabolic subgroups of real semisimple Lie groups plays a pivotal role in the geometry of symmetric spaces of noncompact type because it yields a number of important decomposition results for the space itself, as well as for its isometry group. Among other things, these decompositions can be used to construct various homogeneous objects—like submanifolds or foliations—on the ambient space. They also underpin the two methods of constructing C1-actions with a non-totally-geodesic singular orbit. Here we lay out a brief exposition of the theory and refer to
[
BT13
,
Sol23
,
Sol24
]
for more details. For a deeper introduction, see
[
Kna02
, Ch.VII, Sect. 7]
and
[
BCO16
, Sect. 13.2]
.

Let
M=G/K
be a symmetric space of noncompact type with a fixed choice of
o\in M,\mathfrak{{a}}\subset\mathfrak{{p}},
and
\Upsigma^{+}\subset\Upsigma
as above. Fix any subset
\Upphi\subseteq\Uplambda
.

•

We write
\Upsigma_{\Upphi}
for the root subsystem of
\Upsigma
generated by
\Upphi
. It inherits a choice of positive roots
\Upsigma_{\Upphi}^{+}=\Upsigma_{\Upphi}\cap\Upsigma^{+}
.

•

and
\mathfrak{{a}}^{\Upphi}=\mathfrak{{a}}\ominus\mathfrak{{a}}_{\Upphi}=\bigoplus_{\upalpha_{i}\in\Upphi}\mathbb{R}H_{\upalpha_{i}}
.

The subset
\Updelta_{\Upphi}=\Upsigma^{+}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to8.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.08 0 L 0 4.15}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\Upsigma_{\Upphi}^{+}
can be split into parts:
\Updelta_{\Upphi}=\bigsqcup_{\upnu\geq 1}\Updelta_{\Upphi}^{\upnu}
, where
\Updelta_{\Upphi}^{\upnu}
consists of the roots
\uplambda\in\Updelta_{\Upphi}
with
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{H^{\Upphi}}\hskip 0.5pt,{\uplambda}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt=\upnu
, and
H^{\Upphi}=\sum_{\upalpha_{j}\in\Uplambda\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to8.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.84 -2.08 L 0 10.03}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.08 0 L 0 4.15}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\Upphi}H^{j}
.

•

Let
\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi}=\bigoplus_{\uplambda\in\Updelta_{\Upphi}}\mathfrak{{g}}_{\uplambda}
. This nilpotent subalgebra is graded:
\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi}=\bigoplus_{\upnu\geq 1}\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi}^{\upnu}
, where
\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi}^{\upnu}=\bigoplus_{\uplambda\in\Updelta_{\Upphi}^{\upnu}}\mathfrak{{g}}_{\uplambda}
is the eigenspace of
\ad(H^{\Upphi})
in
\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi}
with eigenvalue
\upnu
.

•

Consider
\mathfrak{{l}}_{\Upphi}=\mathfrak{{g}}_{0}\oplus\bigoplus_{\upalpha\in\Upsigma_{\Upphi}}\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}
. This reductive subalgebra of
\mathfrak{{g}}
centralizes
\mathfrak{{a}}_{\Upphi}
, normalizes
\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi}
, and preserves the grading on
\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi}
. In fact, one has
\mathfrak{{l}}_{\Upphi}=Z_{\mathfrak{{g}}}(\mathfrak{{a}}_{\Upphi})
.

•

\mathfrak{{q}}_{\Upphi}=\mathfrak{{l}}_{\Upphi}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi}
is a parabolic subalgebra of
\mathfrak{{g}}
, and this semidirect sum is called a Chevalley decomposition. Every parabolic subalgebra of
\mathfrak{{g}}
is inner-conjugate to
\mathfrak{{q}}_{\Upphi}
for some choice of
\Upphi
. One can show that
\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi}
is the nilradical of
\mathfrak{{q}}_{\Upphi}
.

•

Going further, let
\mathfrak{{m}}_{\Upphi}=\mathfrak{{l}}_{\Upphi}\ominus\mathfrak{{a}}_{\Upphi}=\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{{a}}^{\Upphi}\oplus\bigoplus_{\upalpha\in\Upsigma_{\Upphi}}\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}\subseteq\mathfrak{{l}}_{\Upphi}
. This is also a reductive subalgebra of
\mathfrak{{g}}
, and we have a direct sum decomposition
\mathfrak{{l}}_{\Upphi}=\mathfrak{{m}}_{\Upphi}\oplus\mathfrak{{a}}_{\Upphi}
. The induced splitting
\mathfrak{{q}}_{\Upphi}=(\mathfrak{{m}}_{\Upphi}\oplus\mathfrak{{a}}_{\Upphi})\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi}
is called a Langlands decomposition. We will normally omit the parentheses and simply write
\mathfrak{{m}}_{\Upphi}\oplus\mathfrak{{a}}_{\Upphi}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi}
.

•

The
\mathfrak{{k}}
- and
\mathfrak{{p}}
-parts of
\mathfrak{{m}}_{\Upphi}
are
\mathfrak{{k}}_{\Upphi}=\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}\oplus\bigoplus_{\upalpha\in\Upsigma_{\Upphi}^{+}}\mathfrak{{k}}_{\upalpha}
and
\mathfrak{{b}}_{\Upphi}=\mathfrak{{a}}^{\Upphi}\oplus\bigoplus_{\upalpha\in\Upsigma_{\Upphi}^{+}}\mathfrak{{p}}_{\upalpha}
, respectively.

•

Being reductive,
\mathfrak{{m}}_{\Upphi}
is the direct sum of its center
\mathfrak{{z}}_{\Upphi}\subseteq\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}
and its semisimple part
\mathfrak{{g}}_{\Upphi}=[\mathfrak{{m}}_{\Upphi},\mathfrak{{m}}_{\Upphi}]=[\mathfrak{{l}}_{\Upphi},\mathfrak{{l}}_{\Upphi}]
.

•

\mathfrak{{g}}_{\Upphi}
splits as the direct sum of its compact and noncompact semisimple parts. The latter can be described as
\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\Upphi}=[\mathfrak{{b}}_{\Upphi},\mathfrak{{b}}_{\Upphi}]\oplus\mathfrak{{b}}_{\Upphi}
, or alternatively, as the subalgebra of
\mathfrak{{g}}
generated by
\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha},\,\upalpha\in\Upsigma_{\Upphi}
. The compact semisimple part of
\mathfrak{{g}}_{\Upphi}
is given by
Z_{\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}}(\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\Upphi})\ominus\mathfrak{{z}}_{\Upphi}
. The sum of the center of
\mathfrak{{m}}_{\Upphi}
and its compact semisimple part can thus be described as
Z_{\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}}(\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\Upphi})=Z_{\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}}(\mathfrak{{b}}_{\Upphi})
.

•

\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\Upphi}
comes with an Iwasawa decomposition:
\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\Upphi}=\mathfrak{{k}}^{\Upphi}\oplus\mathfrak{{a}}^{\Upphi}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}^{\Upphi}
, where
\mathfrak{{k}}^{\Upphi}=\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\Upphi}\cap\mathfrak{{k}}_{\Upphi}=\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\Upphi}\cap\mathfrak{{k}}
and
\mathfrak{{n}}^{\Upphi}=\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\Upphi}\cap\mathfrak{{n}}=\bigoplus_{\upalpha\in\Upsigma_{\Upphi}^{+}}\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}
.

•

We denote the
\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}
-part of
\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\Upphi}
by
\mathfrak{{k}}^{\Upphi}_{0}
. Note that

\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}=Z_{\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}}(\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\Upphi})\oplus\mathfrak{{k}}^{\Upphi}_{0}=\mathfrak{{z}}_{\Upphi}\oplus(Z_{\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}}(\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\Upphi})\ominus\mathfrak{{z}}_{\Upphi})\oplus\mathfrak{{k}}^{\Upphi}_{0}.

This is a direct sum of Lie algebras, and the summands are pairwise orthogonal with respect to
B_{\uptheta}
(or
B
).

•

The connected (closed) Lie subgroups of
G
corresponding to
\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\Upphi},\mathfrak{{a}}_{\Upphi},
and
\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi}
are denoted by
G^{\prime}_{\Upphi},A_{\Upphi},
and
N_{\Upphi}
, respectively. Furthermore, we define:

\hskip 27.98602ptL_{\Upphi}=Z_{G}(\mathfrak{{a}}_{\Upphi}),\quad Q_{\Upphi}=L_{\Upphi}\ltimes N_{\Upphi}=N_{G}(\mathfrak{{q}}_{\Upphi}),\quad K_{\Upphi}=K\cap L_{\Upphi},\quad M_{\Upphi}=K_{\Upphi}G^{\prime}_{\Upphi}.

These are closed subgroups of
G
with Lie algebras
\mathfrak{{l}}_{\Upphi},\mathfrak{{q}}_{\Upphi},\mathfrak{{k}}_{\Upphi},
and
\mathfrak{{m}}_{\Upphi}
, respectively. We have a direct product decomposition
L_{\Upphi}=M_{\Upphi}\times A_{\Upphi}
. The decompositions
Q_{\Upphi}=L_{\Upphi}\ltimes N_{\Upphi}
and
Q_{\Upphi}=(M_{\Upphi}\times A_{\Upphi})\ltimes N_{\Upphi}=\vcentcolon M_{\Upphi}\times A_{\Upphi}\ltimes N_{\Upphi}
are also called Chevalley and Langlands decompositions of the parabolic subgroup
Q_{\Upphi}
, respectively. The adjoint representation of
L_{\Upphi}
on
\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi}
respects its grading. If we write
K^{\Upphi},A^{\Upphi},
and
N^{\Upphi}
for the connected Lie subgroups of
G^{\prime}_{\Upphi}
with Lie algebras
\mathfrak{{k}}^{\Upphi},\mathfrak{{a}}^{\Upphi},
and
\mathfrak{{n}}^{\Upphi}
, respectively, then we have a global Iwasawa decomposition
G^{\prime}_{\Upphi}=K^{\Upphi}A^{\Upphi}N^{\Upphi}
.

•

The orbit
B_{\Upphi}=G^{\prime}_{\Upphi}\hskip-1.2pt\cdot o=M_{\Upphi}\hskip-1.2pt\cdot o
is a
boundary component
of
M
; it is a totally geodesic submanifold (corresponding to the Lie triple system
\mathfrak{{b}}_{\Upphi}
) and itself a symmetric space of noncompact type and rank
r_{\Upphi}=|\Upphi|
. We can think of its Dynkin diagram
\DD_{\Upphi}
as the subdiagram of
\DD
spanned by the nodes corresponding to the roots in
\Upphi
. This space is represented by the almost effective Riemannian symmetric pair
(G^{\prime}_{\Upphi},K^{\Upphi})
, as well as by the Riemannian symmetric pair
(M^{0}_{\Upphi},K^{0}_{\Upphi})
. Note that the latter has ineffectiveness kernel
Z_{\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}}(\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\Upphi})
, which is compact.

•

The Langlands decomposition of
Q_{\Upphi}
induces—via the transitive action
Q_{\Upphi}\curvearrowright M
—a horospherical decomposition
B_{\Upphi}\times A_{\Upphi}\times N_{\Upphi}\cong M
(just a diffeomorphism). We can summarize this with the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\Upphi}\times A_{\Upphi}\ltimes N_{\Upphi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{\Upphi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{\Upphi}\times A_{\Upphi}\times N_{\Upphi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{\Upphi}/K_{\Upphi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

With respect to the Langlands and horospherical decompositions, the action of
Q_{\Upphi}
on
M
can be written as:

\displaystyle M_{\Upphi}\times A_{\Upphi}\ltimes N_{\Upphi}\curvearrowright B_{\Upphi}\times A_{\Upphi}\times N_{\Upphi},

(1)

\displaystyle(m,a,n)\cdot(m^{\prime}\cdot o,a^{\prime},n^{\prime})=((mm^{\prime})\cdot o,aa^{\prime},(m^{\prime}a^{\prime})^{-1}n(m^{\prime}a^{\prime})n^{\prime}).

•

The submanifold
F_{\Upphi}=L_{\Upphi}\cdot o\cong B_{\Upphi}\times A_{\Upphi}\subseteq M
is also totally geodesic and is a symmetric space of rank
r
with an
(r-r_{\Upphi})
-dimensional flat factor; its splitting into the product of
B_{\Upphi}
and
A_{\Upphi}\cong A_{\Upphi}\cdot o
is a Riemannian product decomposition. For lack of a better word, we call
F_{\Upphi}
an
extended boundary component
. It corresponds to the Lie triple system
\mathfrak{{f}}_{\Upphi}=\mathfrak{{b}}_{\Upphi}\oplus\mathfrak{{a}}_{\Upphi}
.

We will be mostly interested in the situations where
\Upphi
is of the form
\Uplambda\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to8.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.84 -2.08 L 0 10.03}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\hskip-1.0pt}{\hbox{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to8.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.84 -2.08 L 0 10.03}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\hskip-1.0pt}{\hbox{\hbox to2.95pt{\vbox to5.45pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.72499pt\lower-0.22499pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\hskip 1.0pt}{\hbox{\hbox to1.9pt{\vbox to3.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.08 0 L 0 4.15}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{\upalpha_{j}}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
for some simple root
\upalpha_{j}
. In that case, in all of the objects defined above, we simply replace the subscript or superscript
\Upphi
with
j
; we also write
\Uplambda_{j}=\Uplambda\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to8.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.84 -2.08 L 0 10.03}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\hskip-1.0pt}{\hbox{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to8.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.84 -2.08 L 0 10.03}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\hskip-1.0pt}{\hbox{\hbox to2.95pt{\vbox to5.45pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.72499pt\lower-0.22499pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.45pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.77 0 L -0.69 6.92}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\hskip 1.0pt}{\hbox{\hbox to1.9pt{\vbox to3.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.08 0 L 0 4.15}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{\upalpha_{j}}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
. We then have
H^{\Upphi}=H^{j}
, and for each
\upalpha\in\Upsigma^{+}
,
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{\upalpha}\hskip 0.5pt,{H^{j}}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt
gives the coefficient corresponding to
\upalpha_{j}
in the expression of
\upalpha
with respect to the simple system
\Uplambda
. Hence, the set
\Updelta_{j}^{1}
can be described as

(2)

\Updelta_{j}^{1}=\Bigl\{\upalpha_{j}+\sum_{i\neq j}n_{i}\upalpha_{i}\in\Upsigma^{+}\mid n_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\Bigr\}.

In Section
6
we will need to determine explicitly the set
\Updelta_{j}^{1}
for some particular choices of
\upalpha_{j}
. In order to do so, we will make use of the following generalization of the classical notion of a root string. Let
\Upphi\subseteq\Uplambda
once again be an arbitrary subset of simple roots, and let
\uplambda\in\Upsigma
be any root. We define the
\Upphi
-string of
\uplambda
as the set of all the elements of
\Upsigma_{0}
of the form
\uplambda+\sum_{\upalpha_{i}\in\Upphi}n_{i}\upalpha_{i}
with
n_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
. This notion of string was introduced by the first author in
[
SL24
]
, where he also calculated all possible
\Upphi
-strings for all choices of
\Upsigma,\Upphi,
and
\uplambda
. The base idea of that computation is simple: if
\uplambda
is the root of minimum height in its
\Upphi
-string, then
\Upphi\cup\{\uplambda\}
is a simple system for the root subsystem
\Upsigma_{\Upphi,\uplambda}=\vspan_{\mathbb{Z}}(\Upphi\cup\{\uplambda\})\cap\Upsigma
of
\Upsigma
. Note that for such
\uplambda
, every root
\uplambda+\sum_{\upalpha_{i}\in\Upphi}n_{i}\upalpha_{i}
in the
\Upphi
-string has all
n_{i}
nonnegative (see
[
Kna02
, Prop. 2.49]
). The argument is then based on calculating explicitly the
\Upphi
-string of
\uplambda
for any possible pair
(\Upsigma,\Upsigma_{\Upphi,\uplambda})
. Under this new perspective,
\Updelta_{j}^{1}
is nothing but the
\Uplambda_{j}
