-string of
\upalpha_{j}
, as one can see from (
2
) and the fact that
\upalpha_{j}
has minimum height in its
\Uplambda_{j}
-string. Hence, for any root system
\Upsigma
and any choice of
j
, we have an explicit description of
\Updelta_{j}^{1}
. We will make great use of that in Section
6
.

2.3.
The extrinsic geometry of singular orbits

As we have explained, any symmetric space
M
of noncompact type can be regarded as a solvable Lie group
AN
endowed with a certain left-invariant metric. One of the key underpinnings of this work is the fact that the singular orbit of a nilpotent construction action
2
2

2

Recall from Theorem
B
that this is in fact true for any C1-action. We will show this in Section
5
.
on
M
can always be realized as a Lie subgroup of
AN
. This property singles out a very special class of submanifolds of
M
, whose geometry is tied particularly closely to the structure of the isometry Lie algebra
\mathfrak{{g}}
. In the following lines, we introduce a few tools that will help us study the extrinsic geometry of such submanifolds.

Let
M=G/K
be a symmetric space of noncompact type. In this subsection, we assume that the metric on
M
is Killing (see Remark
3.6
). As usual, we fix a point
o\in M
, a maximal abelian subspace
\mathfrak{{a}}\subset\mathfrak{{p}}
, and a set of positive roots
\Upsigma^{+}\subset\Upsigma
. These choices give rise to an Iwasawa decomposition
G=KAN
and an identification
AN\cong M
. The isomorphism
\mathfrak{{a}}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 6.3941pt\hskip-6.3941pt\hbox{$\xrightarrow{\phantom{\sim}}$}\hskip-6.3941pt\hskip-4.76384pt\raisebox{0.64444pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\sim\,$}}\hskip-4.76384pt\hskip 6.3941pt}}T_{o}M\cong\mathfrak{{p}}
is given simply by the projection onto
\mathfrak{{p}}
along
\mathfrak{{k}}
. As follows from the proof of
[
Tam11
, Prop. 4.4]
, the induced left-invariant metric on
AN
is related to the inner product on
\mathfrak{{g}}
via the equation

(3)

\langle\hskip 1.0pt{X}\hskip 1.0pt|\hskip 1.5pt{Y}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt_{AN}=\langle\hskip 1.0pt{X_{\mathfrak{{a}}}}\hskip 1.0pt|\hskip 1.5pt{Y_{\mathfrak{{a}}}}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt_{B_{\uptheta}}+\frac{1}{2}\langle\hskip 1.0pt{X_{\mathfrak{{n}}}}\hskip 1.0pt|\hskip 1.5pt{Y_{\mathfrak{{n}}}}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt_{B_{\uptheta}}\quad\text{for any $X$, $Y\in\mathfrak{{a}}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}$},

where
\mathfrak{{a}}
and
\mathfrak{{n}}
in the subscript denote the
\mathfrak{{a}}
- and
\mathfrak{{n}}
-components of a vector, respectively. Since
\mathfrak{{a}}
and
\mathfrak{{n}}
are mutually orthogonal with respect to both inner products, we see from (
3
) that
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{X}\hskip 1.0pt|\hskip 1.5pt{Y}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt_{AN}=\langle\hskip 1.0pt{X}\hskip 1.0pt|\hskip 1.5pt{Y}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt_{B_{\uptheta}}
whenever
X
or
Y
lies in
\mathfrak{{a}}
; similarly,
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{X}\hskip 1.0pt|\hskip 1.5pt{Y}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt_{AN}=\frac{1}{2}\langle\hskip 1.0pt{X}\hskip 1.0pt|\hskip 1.5pt{Y}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt_{B_{\uptheta}}
if
X
or
Y
lies in
\mathfrak{{n}}
. The Levi-Civita connection on
AN
also admits a neat expression. First, we need the following simple equality:

(4)

\langle\hskip 1.0pt{\ad(X)Y}\hskip 1.0pt|\hskip 1.5pt{Z}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt_{B_{\uptheta}}=-\langle\hskip 1.0pt{Y}\hskip 1.0pt|\hskip 1.5pt{\ad(\uptheta X)Z}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt_{B_{\uptheta}}\quad\text{for any $X$, $Y$, $Z\in\mathfrak{{g}}$}.

Combining this with the Koszul formula and (
3
) leads to:

(5)

\langle\hskip 1.0pt{\nabla_{X}Y}\hskip 1.0pt|\hskip 1.5pt{Z}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt_{AN}=\frac{1}{4}\langle\hskip 1.0pt{[X,Y]+[\uptheta X,Y]-[X,\uptheta Y]}\hskip 1.0pt|\hskip 1.5pt{Z}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt_{B_{\uptheta}}\quad\text{for any $X$, $Y$, $Z\in\mathfrak{{a}}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}$}.

Now, let
H
be a connected Lie subgroup of
AN
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{{h}}
. Under the identification between
M
and
AN
, the orbit
H\cdot o
corresponds to
H
, the tangent space
T_{o}(H\cdot o)
becomes
\mathfrak{{h}}
, and the normal space
N_{o}(H\cdot o)
becomes
\mathfrak{{v}}=(\mathfrak{{a}}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}})\ominus\mathfrak{{h}}
, where the orthogonal complement is taken with respect to
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{-}\hskip 1.0pt|\hskip 1.5pt{-}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt_{AN}
. We would like to have a handy description of the shape operators of
H\cdot o
. Since this is a homogeneous submanifold, it suffices to examine its shape operators only at
o
.

Lemma 2.2
.

Given
\upxi\in\mathfrak{{v}}
, the shape operator
\mathcal{{A}}_{\upxi}
of
H\cdot o
at
o
is given by the following formula:

(6)

\langle\hskip 1.0pt{\mathcal{{A}}_{\upxi}X}\hskip 1.0pt|\hskip 1.5pt{Y}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt_{AN}=\frac{1}{4}\langle\hskip 1.0pt{[\upxi,X]-[\uptheta\upxi,X]}\hskip 1.0pt|\hskip 1.5pt{Y}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt_{B_{\uptheta}}\quad\text{for any $X,Y\in\mathfrak{{h}}$}.

Proof.

By definition,
\mathcal{{A}}_{\upxi}X=-(\nabla_{X}\upxi)^{\top}
, where
\top
denotes the orthogonal projection to
\mathfrak{{h}}
along
\mathfrak{{v}}
. Given
Y\in\mathfrak{{h}}
, we use (
5
) to compute:

\langle\hskip 1.0pt{\mathcal{{A}}_{\upxi}X}\hskip 1.0pt|\hskip 1.5pt{Y}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt_{AN}=-\langle\hskip 1.0pt{(\nabla_{X}\upxi)^{\top}}\hskip 1.0pt|\hskip 1.5pt{Y}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt_{AN}=-\langle\hskip 1.0pt{\nabla_{X}\upxi}\hskip 1.0pt|\hskip 1.5pt{Y}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt_{AN}=\frac{1}{4}\langle\hskip 1.0pt{[\upxi,X]-[\uptheta X,\upxi]-[\uptheta\upxi,X]}\hskip 1.0pt|\hskip 1.5pt{Y}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt_{B_{\uptheta}}.

By comparing this to (
6
), it only remains to show that
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{[\uptheta X,\upxi]}\hskip 1.0pt|\hskip 1.5pt{Y}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt_{B_{\uptheta}}=0
. We calculate:

\langle\hskip 1.0pt{[\uptheta X,\upxi]}\hskip 1.0pt|\hskip 1.5pt{Y}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt_{B_{\uptheta}}=-\langle\hskip 1.0pt{\upxi}\hskip 1.0pt|\hskip 1.5pt{[X,Y]}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt_{B_{\uptheta}}=-\langle\hskip 1.0pt{\upxi}\hskip 1.0pt|\hskip 1.5pt{[X,Y]}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt_{AN}=0.

Here the first equality follows from (
4
), the second from the fact that
[X,Y]\in\mathfrak{{n}}
, and the last one from
[X,Y]\in\mathfrak{{h}}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Formula (
6
) can be simplified still further provided we know some additional information about
\mathfrak{{h}}
: for example, if
\mathfrak{{h}}=(\mathfrak{{h}}\cap\mathfrak{{a}})\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}(\mathfrak{{h}}\cap\mathfrak{{n}})
(which is often the case, see Theorem
B
) or if
\mathfrak{{h}}
contains nonzero vectors lying in a single root space. We will see a particular instance of that in the context of the nilpotent construction in Section
6
(see Proposition
6.17
). As a preparation for that, we need the following two results, which provide an insight into how different root spaces behave with respect to the Lie bracket.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
\upalpha\in\Sigma^{+}
, and let
X,Y\in\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}
be orthogonal. Then:

(1)

[\uptheta X,X]=2||X||^{2}_{AN}H_{\upalpha}=||X||^{2}_{B_{\uptheta}}H_{\upalpha}
.

(2)

[\uptheta X,Y]\in\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}
.

For a proof, see
[
BSL23
, Lem. 2.3]
. Note that we do not need to specify whether
X
and
Y
are orthogonal with respect to
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{-}\hskip 1.0pt|\hskip 1.5pt{-}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt_{AN}
or
B_{\uptheta}
, as those are proportional on
\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}
.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
\upalpha,\upbeta\in\Upsigma
be non-proportional roots and suppose that the
\upbeta
-string of
\upalpha
has the form
\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{\upalpha,\upalpha+\upbeta,\ldots,\upalpha+m\upbeta}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
for some positive integer
m
. Then for any nonzero
X\in\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upbeta}
and any
1\leq k\leq m
, the operator
\ad(X)^{k}\colon\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}\to\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha+k\upbeta}
is injective.

Proof.

By choosing a different system of simple roots if necessary, we may assume without loss of generality that both
\upalpha
and
\upbeta
are positive. It follows from
[
Kna02
, Prop. 2.48]
that
m\in\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{1,2,3}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
. The case of
k=1
(and any
m
) was done in
[
BSL23
, Lem. 2.4(i)]
, and the case
k=m=2
was shown in Proposition 4.2(iv) of the same paper. Therefore, we only need to deal with
3
3

3

This can only occur when the irreducible component of
\Upsigma
containing
\upalpha
and
\upbeta
is isomorphic to
\mathrm{{G}}_{2}
.

m=3,\,k=2,3
. Take a nonzero vector
Y\in\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}
. By combining
[
BSL23
, Lem. 2.4]
with
[
Kna02
, Prop. 2.48(g)]
, we obtain that
\ad(\uptheta X)\circ\ad(X)^{3}(Y)
is a nonzero multiple of
\ad(X)^{2}(Y)
, and
\ad(\uptheta X)\circ\ad(X)^{2}(Y)
is a nonzero multiple of
\ad(X)(Y)
. But we already know that this latter vector is nonzero. Consequently,
\ad(X)^{2}(Y)
must be nonzero, and hence so does
\ad(X)^{3}(Y)
. This concludes the proof.
∎

In Section
6
, we will constantly use another important property of singular orbits of C1-actions: their principal curvatures, counted with multiplicities, do not depend on the normal unit direction. More rigorously, given such an orbit
S\subset M
and two normal unit vectors
\upxi,\upnu\in NS
(possibly at different points of
S
), the spectra of the shape operators
\mathcal{{A}}_{\upxi}
and
\mathcal{{A}}_{\upnu}
coincide. This is a simple consequence of the facts that
S
is a homogeneous submanifold and that the slice representation of the underlying C1-action at any point of
S
is transitive on the unit sphere in the normal space to
S
at that point. Submanifolds with this noteworthy geometric property were investigated and called
CPC submanifolds
in
[
BSL23
]
. In fact, one of the goals of that article was to show that singular orbits of C1-actions are far from being the only class of submanifolds that enjoy the CPC property.

3.
Cohomogeneity-one actions

In this section, we discuss what is known about cohomogeneity-one actions on symmetric spaces of noncompact type and review the current progress in their classification. We begin by describing the four known types and two methods of construction of such actions.

3.1.
Types of cohomogeneity-one actions

Let
M=G/K
be a symmetric space of noncompact type, and let
H
be a connected Lie group acting on
M
properly, isometrically, and with cohomogeneity one. As was shown in
[
BB01
]
, there can only be two scenarios:

(1)

H
has no singular orbits in
M
and its orbits form a Riemannian foliation by hypersurfaces. We call that a homogeneous codimension-one foliation. In that case, every orbit is principal.

(2)

H
has precisely one singular orbit
S
, all the other orbits are principal, and they can be described as the equidistant tubes of various radii around
S
. In that case,
S
is diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{k}
for some
k
, while each principal orbit is diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{S}^{n-k-1}
, where
n=\dim(M)
.

Two isometric actions
H_{1}\curvearrowright M
and
H_{2}\curvearrowright M
are called
orbit-equivalent
if there exists an isometry
\upvarphi\in I(M)
that identifies their orbits: for every
p\in M,\,\upvarphi(H_{1}\cdot p)=H_{2}\cdot\upvarphi(p)
. Similarly, two submanifolds
S_{1},S_{2}\subseteq M
are called
congruent
if there exists
\uppsi\in I(M)
such that
\uppsi(S_{1})=S_{2}
. We also need the notion of
strong
orbit equivalence (resp.,
strong
congruence): this is when the above isometry can be chosen in
I^{0}(M)
. The problem of distinguishing between orbit-equivalent actions or congruent submanifolds is generally referred to as
the problem of congruence
.

From now on, we fix a symmetric space
M=G/K
of noncompact type, a base point
o\in M
, a maximal abelian subspace
\mathfrak{{a}}\subset\mathfrak{{p}}
, and a choice of positive roots
\Upsigma^{+}\subset\Upsigma
.

Let
\ell\subseteq\mathfrak{{a}}
be a one-dimensional subspace. The connected Lie subgroup
H_{\ell}
of
G
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{{h}}_{\ell}=(\mathfrak{{a}}\ominus\ell)\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}
is closed, and its action on
M
has cohomogeneity one and no singular orbits. Moreover, all of its orbits are isometrically congruent to each other. The orbit foliation of
H_{\ell}
is denoted by
\mathcal{{F}}_{\ell}
and is said to be of
horospherical type
. This name was coined in
[
DRDVO23
]
and comes from the fact that for some choices of
\ell
, the leaves of
\mathcal{{F}}_{\ell}
are horospheres. Consider the group
\Aut^{\mathrm{w}}(\DD)
of symmetries of the Dynkin diagram that preserve the simple root multiplicities. It permutes the basis
\Uplambda
of
\mathfrak{{a}}^{*}
and thus acts on
\mathfrak{{a}}^{*}
and
\mathfrak{{a}}
. Provided the metric on
M
is almost Killing, two such actions by
H_{\ell}
and
H_{\ell^{\prime}}
are orbit-equivalent if and only if the lines
\ell,\ell^{\prime}\subseteq\mathfrak{{a}}
differ by an element of
\Aut^{\mathrm{w}}(\DD)
. For more details as well as a more general statement on congruence, see
[
BT03
,
Sol21
]
.

Let
\upalpha_{i}\in\Uplambda
be any simple root and
\ell_{\upalpha_{i}}\subseteq\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha_{i}}
a one-dimensional subspace. The connected Lie subgroup
H_{\upalpha_{i}}
of
G
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{{h}}_{\upalpha_{i}}=\mathfrak{{a}}\oplus(\mathfrak{{n}}\ominus\ell_{\upalpha_{i}})
is closed and its action on
M
has cohomogeneity one and no singular orbits. The resulting foliation is denoted by
\mathcal{{F}}_{\upalpha_{i}}
and is said to be of
solvable type
(also coined in
[
DRDVO23
]
). This foliation has a unique minimal leaf—the one passing through
o
. Moreover, for every
t>0
, the two leaves at distance
t
from the minimal one are congruent to each other and to no other leaf. Were we to choose another line
\ell^{\prime}_{\upalpha_{i}}
in
\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha_{i}}
, the resulting action would be strongly orbit-equivalent to that for
\ell_{\upalpha_{i}}
. When the metric on
M
is almost Killing, two such actions by
H_{\upalpha_{i}}
and
H_{\upalpha_{j}}
are orbit-equivalent if and only if the simple roots
\upalpha_{i}
and
\upalpha_{j}
differ by an element of
\Aut^{\mathrm{w}}(\DD)
. Once again, see
[
BT03
,
Sol21
]
for details and a general version of the congruence criterion.

The next two types of C1-actions are those with a totally geodesic orbit. The only symmetric space of noncompact type that admits a totally geodesic hypersurface is
\mathbb{R}H^{n}
, and that hypersurface (the hyperbolic hyperplane
\mathbb{R}H^{n-1}\subset\mathbb{R}H^{n}
) is a leaf of the (unique up to congruence) foliation of solvable type on
\mathbb{R}H^{n}
. This means that we need only consider C1-actions with a totally geodesic
singular
orbit. According to (
) above, such an action is fully determined by its singular orbit up to orbit equivalence. In
[
BT04
]
, Berndt and Tamaru classified such actions for
M
irreducible and discovered that they are intimately related to reflective submanifolds. A submanifold of a Riemannian manifold is called
reflective
if it can be realized as a connected component of the set of fixed points of an involutive isometry of the ambient space. Such submanifolds are automatically totally geodesic. In
[
Leu75
,
Leu79
]
, Leung classified reflective submanifolds in all irreducible symmetric spaces of compact type up to congruence. Later on, it was shown in
[
BT04
]
that if a C1-action on
M
has a totally geodesic singular orbit, that orbit has to be reflective—apart from 5 exceptions, all mysteriously related to the group
\mathrm{{G}}_{2}
(see Theorem 4.2 therein). Using Leung’s classification and the duality between symmetric spaces of compact and noncompact type, Berndt and Tamaru calculated exactly which reflective submanifolds arise in this way. In
[
Leu79
]
, Leung showed that if two reflective submanifolds in a compact irreducible symmetric space are isometric, then they are congruent, albeit not necessarily strongly congruent. That result also carries over to the noncompact type via the duality. But if the singular orbits of two C1-actions are congruent, then the actions themselves are orbit-equivalent.
So a C1-action with a totally geodesic singular orbit is determined up to orbit equivalence by the isometry class of that orbit. For the complete list of these orbits, see
[
BT04
, Th. 3.1, 3.3]
.

The second type of C1-actions with a totally geodesic singular orbit was studied in
[
DRDVO23
]
and occurs on reducible spaces—but of very specific form. Suppose
M=M_{1}\times M_{2}
is the product of two homothetic rank-one spaces; this means that
M_{i}
is a hyperbolic space over
\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C},
or
\mathbb{H}
, or the Cayley hyperbolic plane
\mathbb{O}H^{2}
, but the radii of
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
can be different. We can write
\mathfrak{{g}}=\mathfrak{{g}}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{{g}}_{2}
, where
\mathfrak{{g}}_{i}
is the isometry Lie algebra of
M_{i}
. Fix an isomorphism
\upvarphi\colon\mathfrak{{g}}_{1}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 6.3941pt\hskip-6.3941pt\hbox{$\xrightarrow{\phantom{\sim}}$}\hskip-6.3941pt\hskip-4.76384pt\raisebox{0.64444pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\sim\,$}}\hskip-4.76384pt\hskip 6.3941pt}}\mathfrak{{g}}_{2}
and consider the diagonal subalgebra
\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upvarphi}=\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{X+\upvarphi(X)\mid X\in\mathfrak{{g}}_{1}}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
. The corresponding connected Lie subgroup
G_{\upvarphi}
of
G=G_{1}\times G_{2}
(here we take
G=I^{0}(M)
and
G_{i}=I^{0}(M_{i})
) acts on
M
with cohomogeneity one and has a totally geodesic (even reflective) singular orbit homothetic to
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
. We call this a
diagonal C1-action
on
M
. Choosing another isomorphism
\uppsi\colon\mathfrak{{g}}_{1}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 6.3941pt\hskip-6.3941pt\hbox{$\xrightarrow{\phantom{\sim}}$}\hskip-6.3941pt\hskip-4.76384pt\raisebox{0.64444pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\sim\,$}}\hskip-4.76384pt\hskip 6.3941pt}}\mathfrak{{g}}_{2}
results in a strongly orbit-equivalent action provided the isomorphism
\uppsi^{-1}\circ\upvarphi
of
\mathfrak{{g}}_{1}
is inner. The group
\Aut(\mathfrak{{g}}_{1})
has two connected components if
M_{i}\simeq\mathbb{R}H^{n}
or
\mathbb{C}H^{n}
and only one if
M_{i}\simeq\mathbb{H}H^{n}
or
\mathbb{O}H^{2}
(see
[
Gün10
]
). Consequently, this construction yields one strong-orbit-equivalence class of C1-actions if
M_{i}\simeq\mathbb{H}H^{n}
or
\mathbb{O}H^{2}
and at most two if
M_{i}\simeq\mathbb{R}H^{n}
or
\mathbb{C}H^{n}
.

Now, we describe a general construction, introduced in
[
BT13
]
and called the
canonical extension
, that allows to construct new C1-actions on higher-rank symmetric spaces from those on their boundary components. We come back to the general case of an arbitrary
M=G/K
and fix a choice of
o,\,\mathfrak{{a}},
and
\Upsigma^{+}
. Pick a subset
\Upphi\subset\Uplambda
. Suppose
H_{\Upphi}
is a connected Lie group acting properly and isometrically on the boundary component
B_{\Upphi}
. Factoring out the ineffectiveness kernel, we may assume it is a subgroup of
I^{0}(B_{\Upphi})
and then lift it along
G^{\prime}_{\Upphi}\twoheadrightarrow I^{0}(B_{\Upphi})
to a connected Lie subgroup of
G^{\prime}_{\Upphi}\subset M_{\Upphi}
(still denoted by the same symbol). Consider the subgroup

H_{\Upphi}^{\Uplambda}=H_{\Upphi}\times A_{\Upphi}\ltimes N_{\Upphi}\subset M_{\Upphi}\times A_{\Upphi}\ltimes N_{\Upphi}=Q_{\Upphi}.

This group acts on
M
properly and with the same cohomogeneity as that of
H_{\Upphi}\curvearrowright B_{\Upphi}
. Moreover, with respect to the horospherical decomposition, its orbits can be described as
(H_{\Upphi}\cdot p)\times A_{\Upphi}\times N_{\Upphi}
, where
p\in B_{\Upphi}
. In particular, if
H_{\Upphi}\cdot p
is a singular orbit of
H_{\Upphi}
in
B_{\Upphi}
, then
H_{\Upphi}^{\Uplambda}\cdot p
is that of
H_{\Upphi}^{\Uplambda}
in
M
. The Lie algebra of
H_{\Upphi}^{\Uplambda}
can be described as
\mathfrak{{h}}_{\Upphi}^{\Uplambda}=\mathfrak{{h}}_{\Upphi}\oplus\mathfrak{{a}}_{\Upphi}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi}
, where
\mathfrak{{h}}_{\Upphi}=\Lie(H_{\Upphi})\subseteq\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\Upphi}
. The action
H_{\Upphi}^{\Uplambda}\curvearrowright M
(as well as the group
H_{\Upphi}^{\Uplambda}
and the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{{h}}_{\Upphi}^{\Uplambda}
) is called the
canonical extension
of
H_{\Upphi}\curvearrowright B_{\Upphi}
(resp., of
H_{\Upphi}
and
\mathfrak{{h}}_{\Upphi}
). If two actions on
B_{\Upphi}
are
strongly
orbit-equivalent, then their canonical extensions are (strongly) orbit-equivalent. However, non-strongly orbit-equivalent actions on
B_{\Upphi}
can lead to orbit-non-equivalent actions on
M
(see the example on p. 139 in
[
BT13
]
). This subtlety is important when one wants to study the problem of congruence for C1-actions (see
[
DRDVO23
, Rem. 1.1]
). It is also worth mentioning that the canonical extension method is transitive. Given two subsets
\Upphi\subset\Uppsi\subset\Uplambda
, we can regard
B_{\Upphi}
as a boundary component of
B_{\Uppsi}
. In particular, given an isometric action on
B_{\Upphi}
, we could first canonically extend it to
B_{\Uppsi}
, and then extend the result to
M
, but that would be orbit-equivalent to the canonical extension of the original action directly to
M
. In other words, given a Lie subalgebra
\mathfrak{{h}}_{\Upphi}\subseteq\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\Upphi}
, we have
(\mathfrak{{h}}_{\Upphi}^{\Uppsi})^{\Uplambda}=\mathfrak{{h}}_{\Upphi}^{\Uplambda}
(see
[
DRDVO23
, Lem. 4.2]
for a proof). For more details on the canonical extension method, see
[
BT13
]
.

Finally, we have reached the central point of this paper: the
nilpotent construction
. In its current form, this method was also invented in
[
BT13
]
, although its simpler version for rank-one spaces goes all the way back to
[
BB01
]
. One more time, fix a subset
\Upphi\subset\Uplambda
. Let
\mathfrak{{w}}\subseteq\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi}^{1}
be any subspace. We introduce the following notation:

\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi,\mathfrak{{w}}}=\mathfrak{{w}}\oplus\bigoplus_{\upnu\geq 2}\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi}^{\upnu},\quad\mathfrak{{h}}_{\Upphi,\mathfrak{{w}}}=N_{\mathfrak{{l}}_{\Upphi}}(\mathfrak{{w}})\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi,\mathfrak{{w}}}\subseteq\mathfrak{{l}}_{\Upphi}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi}=\mathfrak{{q}}_{\Upphi};

and we write
N_{\Upphi,\mathfrak{{w}}}
and
H_{\Upphi,\mathfrak{{w}}}
for the corresponding connected Lie subgroups of
G
. These are both closed subgroups. Note that
H_{\Upphi,\mathfrak{{w}}}=N^{0}_{L_{\Upphi}}(\mathfrak{{w}})\ltimes N_{\Upphi,\mathfrak{{w}}}\subseteq L_{\Upphi}\ltimes N_{\Upphi}=Q_{\Upphi}
. The following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

F_{\Upphi}\subseteq H_{\Upphi,\mathfrak{{w}}}\hskip-1.2pt\cdot o
.

(2)

N_{L_{\Upphi}}(\mathfrak{{w}})
acts transitively on
F_{\Upphi}
.

(3)

The image of the projection of
N_{\mathfrak{{l}}_{\Upphi}}(\mathfrak{{w}})
to
\mathfrak{{p}}
along
\mathfrak{{k}}
equals
\mathfrak{{b}}_{\Upphi}\oplus\mathfrak{{a}}_{\Upphi}
.

In case
\Upphi=\Uplambda_{j}
for some
\upalpha_{j}\in\Uplambda
, we can say a little more. First of all, since
\mathfrak{{a}}_{j}=\mathbb{R}H^{j}
and
\ad(H^{j})
acts on
\mathfrak{{n}}_{j}^{1}
as the identity, we have
N_{\mathfrak{{l}}_{j}}(\mathfrak{{w}})=N_{\mathfrak{{m}}_{j}}(\mathfrak{{w}})\oplus\mathfrak{{a}}_{j}
and
N_{L_{j}}(\mathfrak{{w}})=N_{M_{j}}(\mathfrak{{w}})\times A_{j}
. What is more, the above conditions are also equivalent to:

(iv)

N_{M_{\Upphi}}(\mathfrak{{w}})
acts transitively on
B_{\Upphi}
.

(v)

The image of the projection of
N_{\mathfrak{{m}}_{\Upphi}}(\mathfrak{{w}})
to
\mathfrak{{p}}
along
\mathfrak{{k}}
equals
\mathfrak{{b}}_{\Upphi}
.

Let us come back to the case of a general subset
\Upphi
. We will constantly make use of the following fact, which was stated in its infinitesimal version in
[
BT13
]
:

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{{w}}\subseteq\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi}^{1}
be any subspace, and consider
\mathfrak{{v}}=\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi}^{1}\ominus\mathfrak{{w}}
. Then one has:

(1)

\Uptheta N_{L_{\Upphi}}(\mathfrak{{w}})=N_{L_{\Upphi}}(\mathfrak{{v}})
and
\uptheta N_{\mathfrak{{l}}_{\Upphi}}(\mathfrak{{w}})=N_{\mathfrak{{l}}_{\Upphi}}(\mathfrak{{v}})
.

(2)

\Uptheta N_{M_{\Upphi}}(\mathfrak{{w}})=N_{M_{\Upphi}}(\mathfrak{{v}})
and
\uptheta N_{\mathfrak{{m}}_{\Upphi}}(\mathfrak{{w}})=N_{\mathfrak{{m}}_{\Upphi}}(\mathfrak{{v}})
.

(3)

N_{K_{\Upphi}}(\mathfrak{{w}})=N_{K_{\Upphi}}(\mathfrak{{v}})
and
N_{\mathfrak{{k}}_{\Upphi}}(\mathfrak{{w}})=N_{\mathfrak{{k}}_{\Upphi}}(\mathfrak{{v}})
.

Proof.

First of all, we need only prove the first statement in each of the three parts, since the second one is its infinitesimal version. Part (
) will follow from (
) because
K_{\Upphi}=L_{\Upphi}\cap K
is
\Uptheta
-stable. (Alternatively, it follows from the fact that the representation of
K_{\Upphi}
on
\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi}^{1}
is orthogonal.) The proof for (
) is essentially the same as for (
), so we only deal with (
). First, by considering the covering
\uppi\colon G\twoheadrightarrow G/Z(G)\cong I^{0}(M)
, it is easy to see that it suffices to prove the statement for
G/Z(G)
. In other words, we may assume that
G\cong I^{0}(M)
and thus the Riemannian symmetric pair
(G,K)
is effective. In this case,
\Uptheta
can be described as the conjugation
C_{s_{o}}
, where
s_{o}\in I(M)
is the geodesic symmetry at the base point
o\in M
. Given
g\in N_{L_{\Upphi}}(\mathfrak{{w}})
, we need to show that
\Ad(\Uptheta(g))=\Ad(s_{o}gs_{o})=\uptheta\Ad(g)\uptheta
preserves
\mathfrak{{v}}
. Pick any
Y\in\mathfrak{{v}}
and
X\in\mathfrak{{w}}
. We want to show that
\uptheta\hskip-1.0pt\Ad(g)\uptheta(Y)
is still orthogonal to
X
. We compute:

\langle\hskip 1.0pt{X}\hskip 1.0pt|\hskip 1.5pt{\uptheta\Ad(g)\uptheta(Y)}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt=-B(X,\Ad(g)\uptheta(Y))=-B(\Ad(g^{-1})X,\uptheta Y)=\langle\hskip 1.0pt{\Ad(g^{-1})X}\hskip 1.0pt|\hskip 1.5pt{Y}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt=0,

since
\Ad(g^{-1})X\in\mathfrak{{w}}
. We deduce that
\Uptheta N_{L_{\Upphi}}(\mathfrak{{w}})\subseteq N_{L_{\Upphi}}(\mathfrak{{v}})
. Applying
\Uptheta
to each side and switching the roles of
\mathfrak{{w}}
and
\mathfrak{{v}}
gives the reverse inclusion.
∎

We embrace the notation from the lemma and denote the orthogonal complement
\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi}^{1}\ominus\mathfrak{{w}}
by
\mathfrak{{v}}
.

Definition 3.2
.

A subspace
\mathfrak{{w}}\subseteq\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi}^{1}
is called

(1)

admissible
if
N_{L_{\Upphi}}(\mathfrak{{w}})
acts transitively on
F_{\Upphi}
, and

(2)

protohomogeneous
if
N_{K_{\Upphi}}(\mathfrak{{w}})
acts transitively on the unit sphere in
\mathfrak{{v}}
.

The main merit of this construction and these definitions is the following result of Berndt and Tamaru from
[
BT13
]
:

Theorem 3.3
.

Let
M=G/K
be a symmetric space of noncompact type with a fixed choice of
o,\mathfrak{{a}},\Upsigma^{+},
and
\Upphi
.

(1)

Suppose
\mathfrak{{w}}\subset\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi}^{1}
is an admissible and protohomogeneous subspace of
\codim_{\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi}^{1}}(\mathfrak{{w}})\geq 2
. Then the subgroup
H_{\Upphi,\mathfrak{{w}}}
acts on
M
with cohomogeneity one and has
F_{\Upphi}\times N_{\Upphi,\mathfrak{{w}}}\subset F_{\Upphi}\times N_{\Upphi}\cong M
as its orbit through
o
. That orbit has codimension equal to
\codim_{\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi}^{1}}(\mathfrak{{w}})
and is thus singular.

(2)

Let
\mathfrak{{w}}\subset\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi}^{1}
and
k\in K_{\Upphi}
. Then
\mathfrak{{w}}^{\prime}=\Ad(k)(\mathfrak{{w}})
is admissible
(
resp., protohomogeneous
)
if and only if
\mathfrak{{w}}
is. Moreover, the actions of
H_{\Upphi,\mathfrak{{w}}}
and
H_{\Upphi,\mathfrak{{w}}^{\prime}}
are orbit-equivalent by means of
k
.

For any subspace
\mathfrak{{w}}
as in Theorem
3.3
(
), the action of
H_{\Upphi,\mathfrak{{w}}}
is called a
nilpotent construction action
. Note that, under the projection
\mathfrak{{a}}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 6.3941pt\hskip-6.3941pt\hbox{$\xrightarrow{\phantom{\sim}}$}\hskip-6.3941pt\hskip-4.76384pt\raisebox{0.64444pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\sim\,$}}\hskip-4.76384pt\hskip 6.3941pt}}\mathfrak{{p}}\cong T_{o}M
along
\mathfrak{{k}}
,
\mathfrak{{v}}
gets identified with the normal space to the orbit
H_{\Upphi,\mathfrak{{w}}}\cdot o
at
o
. For more details on the nilpotent construction, see
[
BT13
,
BDV15
]
.

Remark 3.4
.

A word of caution is in order. Our notation and definitions of admissibility and protohomogeneity are slightly (but inconsequentially) different from those used in other papers on the subject, such as
[
BT13
,
DRDVRV21
,
Sol23
,
DRDVO23
]
. In these papers, the perspective was not on
\mathfrak{{w}}
but on its orthogonal complement
\mathfrak{{v}}
. More precisely, in a situation where a subspace
\mathfrak{{w}}\subset\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi}^{1}
is admissible or protohomogeneous according to Definition
3.2
, the authors of the aforementioned papers say instead that
\mathfrak{{v}}
is admissible or protohomogeneous, respectively. (Note that the terms protohomogeneous and admissible were only coined in
[
DRDVRV21
]
and
[
Sol23
]
.) They also denote the subgroups
N_{\Upphi,\mathfrak{{w}}}
and
H_{\Upphi,\mathfrak{{w}}}
by
N_{\Upphi,\mathfrak{{v}}}
and
H_{\Upphi,\mathfrak{{v}}}
. This is of course only a matter of notation and it has no implications for the nilpotent construction—but one should be careful not to confuse the two notational agreements. We will see in Subsection
4.2
how our perspective proves beneficial in certain circumstances.

3.2.
The current state of classification

Now we formulate a result that sums up what is currently known about C1-actions on symmetric spaces of noncompact type and their classification. In the present form, this result was established in
[
DRDVO23
]
, although that paper is largely underpinned by
[
BT13
]
.

Theorem 3.5
.

Let
M=G/K
be a symmetric space of noncompact type and rank
r
. Let
H
be a connected Lie group acting on
M
properly and isometrically. Then
H
acts with cohomogeneity one if and only if its action is orbit-equivalent to one of the following:

(1)

The action of
H_{\ell}
for some one-dimensional linear subspace
\ell\subseteq\mathfrak{{a}}
.

(2)

The action of
H_{\upalpha_{i}}
for some simple root
\upalpha_{i}\in\Uplambda
.

(3)

The canonical extension of a C1-action with a totally geodesic singular orbit on an irreducible boundary component
B_{\Upphi}
of
M
.

(4)

The canonical extension of a diagonal C1-action on a reducible rank-2 boundary component
B_{\Upphi}
of
M
whose de Rham factors are homothetic.

(5)

The action of
H_{j,\mathfrak{{w}}}
for some
j\in\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{1,\ldots,r}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
, where
\mathfrak{{w}}\subset\mathfrak{{n}}_{j}^{1}
is an admissible and protohomogeneous subspace of
\codim_{\mathfrak{{n}}_{j}^{1}}(\mathfrak{{w}})\geq 2
.

From our discussion in Subsection
3.1
, it is evident that part (
) stands out among the others: it does not produce an explicit list of actions but rather reduces the classification to a problem in representation theory. We also see that the nilpotent construction is only relevant when
\Upphi=\Uplambda_{j}
for some
j
. For this reason, for the rest of the article, whenever we say
nilpotent construction
, we tacitly assume that
\Upphi
is of this form, unless otherwise specified. Note that actions of type (
) in Theorem
3.5
are decomposable (see
[
DRDVO23
, Th. C]
). Consequently, when dealing with the nilpotent construction problem, we may always assume that
M
is irreducible.

Remark 3.6
(The choice of a metric).

Suppose
M=G/K
is a symmetric space of noncompact type and
H
is a connected Lie group acting on
M
isometrically. Rescaling the existing Riemannian metric along the de Rham factors of
M
may change the full isometry group
I(M)
but not its identity component
I^{0}(M)
(see
[
Sol24
, Prop. 2.1.60]
). This implies that after any such rescaling, the action
H\curvearrowright M
would still be isometric. Therefore, when classifying C1-actions—and in particular dealing with the nilpotent construction—we may assume that
M
is equipped with the Killing metric
g_{B}
. (See also
[
DRDVO23
, Rem. 2.5]
.) This simplifies certain formulas and is particularly useful when one wants to work with the solvable model of
M
. In this article, we adopt this assumption in Subsection
2.3
and Section
6
. Note that one cannot, however, make this assumption in general when dealing with the problem of congruence—since it affects the full isometry group.

4.
The admissibility condition

This section serves as the backbone of the article: here, we will prove a structural result that will significantly simplify the nilpotent construction problem and essentially render it solvable. Our line of argument is going to be broken into three steps. First, we prove Theorem
A
—a general result on groups that act transitively and isometrically on symmetric spaces of noncompact type. We then use that result to show that one of the groups involved in the nilpotent construction can be assumed to look particularly nice with respect to an Iwasawa decomposition. Finally, we show how that puts serious restrictions on the subspace
\mathfrak{{w}}\subset\mathfrak{{n}}_{j}^{1}
and forces it to be positioned nicely with respect to the restricted root space decomposition.

4.1.
Transitive groups of isometries of symmetric spaces of noncompact type

In
[
Oni62
]
, Onishchik obtained (among other things) a classification of transitive isometric actions on irreducible symmetric spaces of compact type (see also
[
Kol17
, Prop. 21]
). There is no analogous result for symmetric spaces of noncompact type. The core reason for this lies in the fact that the isometry group in the noncompact case is itself noncompact and thus its subgroups are generally more complicated (for starters, they are not necessarily reductive). Our first goal in this section is to obtain a general result that will give a uniform description of transitive actions on symmetric spaces of noncompact type.

First of all, we recall the classification of maximal solvable subalgebras—also known as
Borel subalgebras
—obtained by Mostow in
[
Mos61
]
. Let
\mathfrak{{g}}
be a real semisimple Lie algebra and
\mathfrak{{c}}\subset\mathfrak{{g}}
a Cartan subalgebra (for a background on Cartan subalgebras in real semisimple Lie algebras, see
[
Kna02
, Ch.VI, Sect. 6]
). There exists a Cartan involution
\uptheta
that preserves
\mathfrak{{c}}
; we write
\mathfrak{{g}}=\mathfrak{{k}}\oplus\mathfrak{{p}}
for the corresponding Cartan decomposition and thus have
\mathfrak{{c}}=\tilde{\mathfrak{{t}}}\oplus\tilde{\mathfrak{{a}}}
, where
\tilde{\mathfrak{{t}}}=\mathfrak{{c}}\cap\mathfrak{{k}}
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{{a}}}=\mathfrak{{c}}\cap\mathfrak{{p}}
. Note that
\mathfrak{{c}}
is abelian and consists of semisimple elements, and its subspaces
\tilde{\mathfrak{{t}}}
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{{a}}}
can be described invariantly as:

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathfrak{{t}}}

\displaystyle=\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{X\in\mathfrak{{c}}\mid\text{all eigenvalues of $\ad_{\mathfrak{{g}}}(X)$ are purely imaginary}}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\},

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathfrak{{a}}}

\displaystyle=\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{X\in\mathfrak{{c}}\mid\text{all eigenvalues of $\ad_{\mathfrak{{g}}}(X)$ are real}}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}.

In particular, for every
X\in\tilde{\mathfrak{{a}}}
,
\ad(X)
is diagonalizable as an operator on
\mathfrak{{g}}
. The dimensions of
\tilde{\mathfrak{{t}}}
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{{a}}}
are called the compact and noncompact dimensions of
\mathfrak{{c}}
, respectively. Pick a maximal abelian subspace
\mathfrak{{a}}\subset\mathfrak{{p}}
containing
\tilde{\mathfrak{{a}}}
, write
\Upsigma\subset\mathfrak{{a}}^{*}
for the corresponding restricted root system, and let
\mathfrak{{g}}=\mathfrak{{g}}_{0}\oplus\bigoplus_{\upalpha\in\Upsigma}\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}
stand for the induced restricted root space decomposition.

Example 4.1
.

Pick a maximal abelian subspace
\mathfrak{{t}}_{0}
in
\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}
. Then
\mathfrak{{t}}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{{a}}
is a Cartan subalgebra of
\mathfrak{{g}}
. It is said to be maximally noncompact since it has the largest possible noncompact dimension. All maximally noncompact Cartan subalgebras are congruent via
\Inn(\mathfrak{{g}})
.

The following was shown in
[
DRLN23
]
:

Proposition 4.2
.

There exists a choice of simple roots
\Uplambda
for
\Upsigma
and a subset
\Upphi\subseteq\Uplambda
such that
\tilde{\mathfrak{{a}}}=\mathfrak{{a}}_{\Upphi}
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{{t}}}
is a maximal abelian subspace of
\mathfrak{{k}}_{\Upphi}
.

In the context of this proposition, maximally noncompact Cartan subalgebras correspond precisely to
\Upphi=\varnothing
. In
[
Mos61
, Sect. 4]
, Mostow showed that every Borel subalgebra of
\mathfrak{{g}}
contains a Cartan subalgebra. From that, he was able to get a complete description of all Borel subalgebras. Here we give a reformulation of Mostow’s result obtained in
[
DRLN23
, Th. 2.2]
, which is slightly better suited for our purposes:

Theorem 4.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{{g}}
be a real semisimple Lie algebra and
\mathfrak{{b}}\subset\mathfrak{{g}}
a Borel subalgebra. Then there exists a choice of a Cartan decomposition
\mathfrak{{g}}=\mathfrak{{k}}\oplus\mathfrak{{p}}
, a maximal abelian subspace
\mathfrak{{a}}\subset\mathfrak{{p}}
, a set of simple roots
\Uplambda\subset\Upsigma
, a subset
\Upphi\subseteq\Uplambda
, and a maximal abelian subspace
\tilde{\mathfrak{{t}}}\subseteq\mathfrak{{k}}_{\Upphi}
such that
\mathfrak{{b}}=\tilde{\mathfrak{{t}}}\oplus\mathfrak{{a}}_{\Upphi}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}_{\Upphi}
.

We call a subspace
W
of
\mathfrak{{g}}

Iwasawa-adapted
if
W=(W\cap\mathfrak{{k}})\oplus(W\cap\mathfrak{{a}})\oplus(W\cap\mathfrak{{n}})
. We see from Theorem
4.3
that
every Borel subalgebra is Iwasawa-adapted with respect to some Iwasawa decomposition
.

Example 4.4
.

If
\mathfrak{{c}}=\mathfrak{{t}}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{{a}}
is a maximally noncompact Cartan subalgebra, then every Borel subalgebra containing it is of the form
\mathfrak{{t}}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{{a}}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}
for some choice of
\Upsigma^{+}\subset\Upsigma
. Such a Borel subalgebra is also said to be maximally noncompact.

Using Mostow’s classification, we can obtain a general structural result on groups acting transitively by isometries on symmetric spaces of noncompact type. First, we need the following

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
M
be a symmetric space of noncompact type. Suppose
H
is a Lie group acting on
M
transitively and isometrically. Then there exists a connected solvable Lie subgroup
S
of
H
that still acts transitively on
M
.

Proof.

Let
G
stand for
I^{0}(M)
and write
\upvarphi\colon\mathfrak{{h}}\to\mathfrak{{g}}=\Lie(G)
for the homomorphism arising from the action. Pick a Levi decomposition
\mathfrak{{h}}=\mathfrak{{l}}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathrm{{rad}}(\mathfrak{{h}})
and an Iwasawa decomposition
\mathfrak{{l}}=\mathfrak{{k}}\oplus\mathfrak{{a}}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}
, and write
\mathfrak{{l}}=\mathfrak{{k}}\oplus\mathfrak{{p}}
for the corresponding Cartan decomposition. Consider the restriction
\uppsi\colon\mathfrak{{l}}\to\mathfrak{{g}}
of
\upvarphi
. Since both
\mathfrak{{l}}
and
\mathfrak{{g}}
are semisimple, there exists a Cartan decomposition
\mathfrak{{g}}=\overline{\mathfrak{{k}}}\oplus\overline{\mathfrak{{p}}}
such that
\uppsi(\mathfrak{{k}})\subseteq\overline{\mathfrak{{k}}}
(see
[
Oni04
, Ch. 6, Cor. 1]
). The subalgebra
\overline{\mathfrak{{k}}}\subset\mathfrak{{g}}
is the isotropy Lie algebra of some point
o\in M
. Consider the subalgebra
\mathfrak{{s}}=(\mathfrak{{a}}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{{n}})\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathrm{{rad}}(\mathfrak{{h}})
of
\mathfrak{{h}}
; it is solvable as a semidirect sum of two solvable Lie algebras. Since
\upvarphi(\mathfrak{{k}})\subseteq\overline{\mathfrak{{k}}}
,
\upvarphi(\mathfrak{{s}})
has the same projection in
\mathfrak{{g}}/\overline{\mathfrak{{k}}}\cong T_{o}M
as
\upvarphi(\mathfrak{{h}})
, which is the whole
T_{o}M
—by the transitivity of
H\curvearrowright M
. In other words, the
o
-orbit of the connected Lie subgroup
S\subseteq H
corresponding to
\mathfrak{{s}}
is open. This orbit is then a complete totally geodesic submanifold of
M
and thus coincides with the whole
M
.
This completes the proof.
∎

In the proof, we have used some ideas from
[
BDRT10
, Prop. 2.2]
4
4

4

We would like to point out a slight issue in the proof of that proposition: the authors do not show that the subgroup
K
is compact. However, this is rectifiable—e.g., by using an argument similar to ours.
. We are now ready to prove the main result of this subsection
5
5

5

Compare this to
[
BDRT10
, Prop. 5.1]
.
.

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
M
be a symmetric space of noncompact type represented by a Riemannian symmetric pair
(G,K)
with compact ineffectiveness kernel
\mathfrak{{i}}\triangleleft\mathfrak{{g}}

(
note that
G
is necessarily reductive
)
. Write
\overline{\mathfrak{{g}}}\trianglelefteq\mathfrak{{g}}
for the
(
unique
)
ideal complementary to
\mathfrak{{i}}
. Suppose
H\subseteq G
is a Lie subgroup that acts transitively on
M
. Then there exist

(1)

an Iwasawa decomposition
\overline{\mathfrak{{g}}}=\mathfrak{{k}}\oplus\mathfrak{{a}}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}
,

(2)

a maximal abelian subspace
\mathfrak{{t}}_{0}\subseteq\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}
, and

(3)

a maximal abelian subspace
\mathfrak{{t}}\subseteq\mathfrak{{i}}
,

such that
\mathfrak{{h}}=\Lie(H)
contains a subalgebra of the form
V\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}
, where
V\subseteq\mathfrak{{t}}\oplus\mathfrak{{t}}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{{a}}
is a subspace that projects surjectively onto
\mathfrak{{a}}
.

The need to formulate this proposition in such a general and technical form will become evident when we apply it to the nilpotent construction in Proposition
4.14
. Note that Theorem
A
is a special case of Proposition
4.6
when the pair
(G,K)
is almost effective.

Proof of Proposition
4.6
.

By Lemma
4.5
, there exists a solvable subalgebra
\mathfrak{{s}}\subseteq\mathfrak{{h}}
whose corresponding connected Lie subgroup still acts transitively on
M
. Let
\mathfrak{{b}}\subset\mathfrak{{g}}
be a maximal solvable Lie subalgebra containing
\mathfrak{{s}}
. Since
\mathfrak{{g}}=\mathfrak{{i}}\oplus\overline{\mathfrak{{g}}}
is a direct sum decomposition,
\mathfrak{{b}}
must necessarily be of the form
\mathfrak{{t}}\oplus\overline{\mathfrak{{b}}}
, where
\mathfrak{{t}}
is maximal solvable in
\mathfrak{{i}}
and
\overline{\mathfrak{{b}}}
is one in
\overline{\mathfrak{{g}}}
—otherwise,
\pr_{\mathfrak{{i}}}(\mathfrak{{b}})\oplus\pr_{\overline{\mathfrak{{g}}}}(\mathfrak{{b}})
would be a larger solvable subalgebra. We claim that
\mathfrak{{t}}
is abelian. Indeed, being a subalgebra of the compact Lie algebra
\mathfrak{{i}}
,
\mathfrak{{t}}
is itself compact, hence reductive. But since it is also solvable, it must be abelian. Observe that
\overline{\mathfrak{{g}}}\cong\mathfrak{{g}}/\mathfrak{{i}}
is naturally isomorphic to the isometry Lie algebra of
M
and hence is semisimple (
[
Hel01
, Ch.V, Th. 4.1]
). According to Theorem
4.3
, we may assume that
\overline{\mathfrak{{b}}}
is Iwasawa-adapted to some Iwasawa decomposition
\overline{\mathfrak{{g}}}=\mathfrak{{k}}\oplus\mathfrak{{a}}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}
. Let us denote the underlying Cartan decomposition by
\overline{\mathfrak{{g}}}=\mathfrak{{k}}\oplus\mathfrak{{p}}
. In order for its corresponding Lie subgroup to act transitively,
\mathfrak{{s}}
has to project surjectively onto
\mathfrak{{a}}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}\cong\mathfrak{{p}}
along
\mathfrak{{i}}\oplus\mathfrak{{k}}
. This is only possible when
\overline{\mathfrak{{b}}}\cap\mathfrak{{a}}=\mathfrak{{a}}
and
\overline{\mathfrak{{b}}}\cap\mathfrak{{n}}=\mathfrak{{n}}
, which means that
\overline{\mathfrak{{b}}}
is maximally noncompact, i.e.,
\overline{\mathfrak{{b}}}=\mathfrak{{t}}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{{a}}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}
for some maximal abelian subspace
\mathfrak{{t}}_{0}\subseteq\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}
(see Examples
4.1
and
4.4
).

So far, we have obtained a subalgebra
\mathfrak{{s}}\subseteq\mathfrak{{t}}\oplus\mathfrak{{t}}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{{a}}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}
of
\mathfrak{{h}}
that projects surjectively onto
\mathfrak{{a}}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}
along
\mathfrak{{t}}\oplus\mathfrak{{t}}_{0}
. What is left to show is that
\mathfrak{{s}}
actually contains
\mathfrak{{n}}
. Write
\Upsigma
for the restricted root system in
\mathfrak{{a}}^{*}
and take any
\upalpha\in\Upsigma^{+}
and
Z\in\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}
. Pick
H\in\mathfrak{{a}}
such that
[H,Z]=Z
. There exist
X_{1},X_{2}\in\mathfrak{{t}}
and
Y_{1},Y_{2}\in\mathfrak{{t}}_{0}
such that
X_{1}+Y_{1}+H
and
X_{2}+Y_{2}+Z
lie in
\mathfrak{{s}}
. As
\mathfrak{{t}}\oplus\mathfrak{{t}}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{{a}}
is abelian and
\mathfrak{{i}}
commutes with
\overline{\mathfrak{{g}}}
, we have:

\mathfrak{{s}}\ni[X_{1}+Y_{1}+H,X_{2}+Y_{2}+Z]=[Y_{1},Z]+Z.

The adjoint representation of
\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}
(and thus
\mathfrak{{t}}_{0}
) on
\mathfrak{{g}}
preserves each root space and is orthogonal. This means that
[Y_{1},Z]
lies in
\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}
and is orthogonal to
Z
. We deduce that
\mathfrak{{s}}\cap\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}
enjoys the following property: for every
Z\in\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}
,
\mathfrak{{s}}\cap\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}
contains a vector whose orthogonal projection in
\mathbb{R}Z
is
Z
. It is clear that
\mathfrak{{s}}\cap\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}
must then coincide with
\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}
. We see that
\mathfrak{{s}}
contains all positive root spaces and thus the whole
\mathfrak{{n}}
. This concludes the proof.
∎

4.2.
The redundancy

Our next goal is to apply Proposition
4.6
to the admissibility condition in the nilpotent construction, since it has to do with a certain action on a boundary component being transitive. More precisely, applying that proposition would relate the normalizer
N_{\mathfrak{{m}}_{j}}(\mathfrak{{w}})
to some Iwasawa decomposition of the subalgebra
\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{j}\subset\mathfrak{{g}}
. We have to overcome the following practical difficulty:

An Iwasawa decomposition of
\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{j}
arising from Proposition
4.6
may not agree with the existing ambient Iwasawa decomposition of
\mathfrak{{g}}
.

We will deal with this using a rather technical argument—but in order to make it neat, we are going to embrace a more conceptual moduli-space-like approach. Observe that some of the choices we make in the nilpotent construction—like the point
o
or the maximal flat
\mathfrak{{a}}
—turn out to be redundant in the end. Out of all the initial data we fix, what really ends up being used in the nilpotent construction are the Lie algebras
\mathfrak{{m}}_{j},\mathfrak{{a}}_{j},
and
\mathfrak{{n}}_{j}
and the subspace
\mathfrak{{w}}
(and, by implication, the parabolic subalgebra
\mathfrak{{q}}_{j}
as well as the boundary component
B_{j}
). The grading on
\mathfrak{{n}}_{j}
can be recovered from the representation of
\mathfrak{{a}}_{j}
on it. The key idea is to formalize and make use of the following informal observation: altering some of the redundant data should not affect the nilpotent construction. To that end, we will have to make a slight change of perspective on the conditions of admissibility and protohomogeneity. We begin with a little detour into parabolic subgroups. Most of the exposition here is simply mimicry of the constructions bulleted in Subsection
2.2
but without making unnecessary choices (see Remark
4.7
below).

Let
M=G/K
be a symmetric space of noncompact type. Recall that the equivalence classes of asymptotic geodesics in
M
are called points at infinity (for more on this, see
[
Ebe96
]
). The set of such classes is denoted by
M(\infty)
, and we also write
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muM\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu=M\sqcup M(\infty)
. The topology on
M
can be extended to the so-called cone topology on
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muM\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
, which makes
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muM\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
homeomorphic to a closed Euclidean ball (with
M(\infty)
being the boundary sphere and
M
the interior). Given any
o\in M
and
X\in T_{o}M
, we write
\upgamma_{X}
for the geodesic emanating from
o
with
\dot{\upgamma}_{X}(0)=X
, and we denote its asymptotic class by
\upgamma_{X}(\infty)
. This way, sending
X
to
\upgamma_{X}(\infty)
establishes a homeomorphism between the unit sphere in
T_{o}M
and
M(\infty)
. The action of
I(M)
(and thus of
G
) on
M
extends naturally to a continuous action on
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muM\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
. For every
x\in M(\infty)
, the stabilizer
G_{x}
is a parabolic subgroup, and every proper parabolic subgroup arises in this way. A general such subgroup can stabilize several points at infinity. Even though we do not need that, it is not hard to show that for maximal proper parabolic subgroups, this is no longer the case---hence they are parametrized by a subset
6
6

6

More precisely, by a collection of orbits of
G
in
M(\infty)
, which is called the set of
maximally singular
points at infinity in
[
Ebe96
]
.
of
M(\infty)
.

Let
\widehat{{Q}}=G_{x}
be a maximal proper parabolic subgroup of
G
. Fix a base point
o\in M
and take the unique unit vector
X\in T_{o}M
with
\upgamma_{X}(\infty)=x
. Write
R
for the curvature tensor of
M
and consider the subspace
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{f}}}}=\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{Y\in T_{o}M\mid R(Y,X)=0}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
of
T_{o}M
. This subspace is curvature-invariant (meaning,
R(\widehat{{\mathfrak{{f}}}},\widehat{{\mathfrak{{f}}}})\hskip 1.0pt\widehat{{\mathfrak{{f}}}}\subseteq\widehat{{\mathfrak{{f}}}}
), hence it corresponds to a totally geodesic submanifold
\widehat{{F}}=\exp(\hskip 0.5pt\widehat{{\mathfrak{{f}}}}\hskip 0.5pt)
, which is an extended boundary component. We can split
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{f}}}}
orthogonally as the sum
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{b}}}}\oplus\mathbb{R}X
of two curvature-invariant subspaces, whose corresponding totally geodesic submanifolds
\widehat{{B}}=\exp(\widehat{{\mathfrak{{b}}}})
(a boundary component) and
\exp(\mathbb{R}X)
are the noncompact and flat parts of
\widehat{{F}}
, respectively, and we have
\widehat{{F}}=\widehat{{B}}\times\exp(\mathbb{R}X)
. This shows that
\mathbb{R}X
is actually determined by
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{f}}}}
. Consider the Cartan decomposition
\mathfrak{{g}}=\mathfrak{{k}}\oplus\mathfrak{{p}}
, where
\mathfrak{{k}}=\Lie(K)
, and let
\uptheta
be the corresponding Cartan involution. By identifying
\mathfrak{{p}}
and
T_{o}M
, we can think of
\mathbb{R}X
as an abelian subspace
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{a}}}}\subset\mathfrak{{p}}\subset\mathfrak{{g}}
and thus define
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{l}}}}=Z_{\mathfrak{{g}}}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{{a}}}})
and
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{m}}}}=\widehat{{\mathfrak{{l}}}}\ominus\widehat{{\mathfrak{{a}}}}
. These subalgebras are clearly
\uptheta
-stable and have
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{f}}}}
and
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{b}}}}
, respectively, as their
\mathfrak{{p}}
-parts. It then follows that the subgroup
\widehat{{L}}=Z_{G}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{{a}}}})
has
\widehat{{F}}
as its
o
-orbit. We can further define
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{g}}}}
to be the derived subalgebra of
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{m}}}}
(or
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{l}}}}
) and
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{g}}}}^{\prime}
to be the noncompact part of
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{g}}}}
. We write
\widehat{{G}}^{\prime}
and
\widehat{{A}}
for the connected Lie subgroups of
G
corresponding to
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{g}}}}^{\prime}
and
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{a}}}}
and let
\widehat{{K}}=K\cap\widehat{{L}}
and
\widehat{{M}}=\widehat{{K}}\widehat{{G}}^{\prime}
. Finally, we let
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{n}}}}
and
\widehat{{N}}
stand for the nilradical of
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{q}}}}
and its corresponding connected Lie subgroup, respectively. The subalgebra
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{l}}}}=\widehat{{\mathfrak{{m}}}}\oplus\widehat{{\mathfrak{{a}}}}
normalizes
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{n}}}}
, and we have the Langlands decomposition
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{q}}}}=\widehat{{\mathfrak{{m}}}}\oplus\widehat{{\mathfrak{{a}}}}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\widehat{{\mathfrak{{n}}}}
. As an operator on
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{n}}}}
,
\ad(X)
is diagonalizable with eigenvalues
\upvarepsilon,\ldots,k\upvarepsilon,\hskip 1.0pt(\upvarepsilon>0,k\geq 1)
, so
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{n}}}}
becomes graded by the corresponding eigenspaces:
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{n}}}}=\bigoplus_{\upnu\geq 1}\widehat{{\mathfrak{{n}}}}^{\hskip 0.5pt\upnu}
. The group
\widehat{{L}}
, which also normalizes
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{n}}}}
, respects this grading. We also have the horospherical decomposition
M\cong\widehat{{F}}\times\widehat{{N}}\cong\widehat{{B}}\times\widehat{{A}}\times\widehat{{N}}
.

Now, since
\widehat{{L}}
stabilizes
x
and centralizes
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{a}}}}
, if we were to carry out these constructions at a different base point
g\cdot o\in\widehat{{F}},\hskip 1.0ptg\in\widehat{{L}}
, we would obtain the same subalgebras
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{a}}}},\widehat{{\mathfrak{{l}}}},\widehat{{\mathfrak{{m}}}},\widehat{{\mathfrak{{g}}}},\widehat{{\mathfrak{{g}}}}^{\prime}\hskip 1.0pt
, the same grading on
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{n}}}}
, as well as the same submanifold
\widehat{{F}}
. The submanifolds
\widehat{{B}}
and
\exp(\mathbb{R}X)
would become
g(\widehat{{B}})
and
g(\exp(\mathbb{R}X))
, which technically can differ from
\widehat{{B}}
and
\exp(\mathbb{R}X)
, but these are still the noncompact and flat parts of
\widehat{{F}}
. If one repeats these constructions with the same
x
but at an arbitrary point of
M
, one can easily see that
M
gets foliated by extended boundary components all mutually congruent via the action of
\widehat{{N}}
, and the above subalgebras of
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{q}}}}
depend only on which leaf of this foliation we fix. Let us say that a maximal proper extended boundary component is
marked
if one has a fixed orientation on its flat factor (clearly, this can be done at just one point and then extended to other points via transvections). For example,
\widehat{{F}}
is marked because we can orient
\exp(\mathbb{R}X)
in the direction of
X
. With this additional piece of data,
\widehat{{F}}
alone fully recovers
x
and thus
\widehat{{Q}}
.

We will now relate this abstract exposition to the standard theory of parabolic subalgebras laid out in Subsection
2.2
. Fix a base point
o\in\widehat{{F}}
, take the unit vector
X\in T_{o}M
with
\upgamma_{X}(\infty)=x
, and write
\mathfrak{{g}}=\mathfrak{{k}}\oplus\mathfrak{{p}}
for the corresponding Cartan decomposition. Identify
\mathfrak{{p}}
with
T_{o}M
, pick a maximal abelian subspace
\mathfrak{{a}}\subset\mathfrak{{p}}
containing
X
, and write
\Upsigma\subset\mathfrak{{a}}^{*}
for the corresponding restricted root system. According to
[
Ebe96
, Prop. 2.17.13(1)]
, one has

(7)

\widehat{{\mathfrak{{q}}}}=\mathfrak{{g}}_{0}\oplus\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\upalpha\in\Upsigma\mathstrut\\
\upalpha(X)\geq 0\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}.

Make a choice of simple roots
\Uplambda
such that
X
lies in the closure of the positive Weyl chamber; this way, all the positive roots are nonnegative on
X
. If we write
\Upphi=\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{\upalpha\in\Uplambda\mid\langle\hskip 1.0pt{\upalpha}\hskip 0.5pt,{X}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt=0)}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
, then, using (
7
), one can easily check that
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{q}}}}=\mathfrak{{q}}_{\Upphi}
. But
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{q}}}}
is maximal, which means that
\Upphi
has to contain all but one simple roots. In other words, there exists a unique
\upalpha_{j}\in\Uplambda
that is positive on
X
, and we have
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{q}}}}=\mathfrak{{q}}_{j}
. From this, we immediately get
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{l}}}}=\mathfrak{{l}}_{j},\,\widehat{{\mathfrak{{a}}}}=\mathfrak{{a}}_{j},\,\widehat{{\mathfrak{{n}}}}=\mathfrak{{n}}_{j},\,\widehat{{F}}=F_{j},\,\widehat{{M}}=M_{j}
, and so on.

Remark 4.7
.

Having made these identifications, it is now easy to verify all the relevant statements on p.
4.2
by translating them into statements about
\mathfrak{{q}}_{j},\mathfrak{{n}}_{j}
, etc. For example,
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{f}}}}
is indeed curvature-invariant because it gets identified with
\mathfrak{{f}}_{j}
, which, as we know, is a Lie triple system.

Below, we will show that a marked extended boundary component
\widehat{{F}}
is enough to be able to formulate the nilpotent construction. Before we do that, however, let us investigate which data turns out to be redundant. Consider the following two sets:

\displaystyle\widetilde{{\mathcal{{B}}}}=\{(o,\mathfrak{{a}},\Upsigma^{+},\upalpha_{j})\mid\,

\displaystyle o\in M,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{{a}}\subset\mathfrak{{p}}\hskip 5.0pt\text{a maximal abelian subspace},

\displaystyle\Upsigma^{+}\subset\Upsigma\hskip 5.0pt\text{a set of positive roots},

\displaystyle\upalpha_{j}\in\Uplambda\subseteq\Upsigma^{+}\hskip 4.0pt\text{a simple root}\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{{B}}=\{\widehat{{F}}\subset M\mid\,

\displaystyle\widehat{{F}}\hskip 5.0pt\text{a marked maximal proper extended boundary component}\}.

Note that
\mathfrak{{p}}
is determined by
o
as the orthogonal complement of
\mathfrak{{k}}=\Lie(G_{o})
with respect to
B
. We have a map
\mathcal{{F}}\colon\widetilde{{\mathcal{{B}}}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{{B}}
that sends
(o,\mathfrak{{a}},\Upsigma^{+},\upalpha_{j})
to
F_{j}
, marked by orienting
\mathfrak{{a}}_{j}
in the direction of
H^{j}
.

Proposition 4.8
.

The map
\mathcal{{F}}
is well defined. Moreover, for any
\widehat{{F}}\in\mathcal{{B}}
, there is a natural one-to-one correspondence between the fiber
\mathcal{{F}}^{-1}(\widehat{{F}})
and the set of pairs
(\widehat{{B}},\widecheck{K}\widecheck{A}\widecheck{N})
, where
\widehat{{B}}\subset\widehat{{F}}
is the noncompact part of
\widehat{{F}}
, and
\widecheck{K}\widecheck{A}\widecheck{N}
is an Iwasawa decomposition of
\widehat{{G}}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

The first part means that
F_{j}
is indeed of the form
\widehat{{F}}
for some
\widehat{{F}}
constructed as above. To see this, note that
\mathfrak{{q}}_{j}=\mathfrak{{g}}_{0}\oplus\bigoplus_{\upalpha(H^{j})\geq 0}\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}
. In view of (
7
), this coincides with the Lie algebra
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{q}}}}
of
G_{x}
, where
x=\upgamma_{H^{j}}(\infty)
and we think of
H^{j}
as a tangent vector at
o
. But now both
\mathfrak{{f}}_{j}
and
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{f}}}}
can be described as the kernel of
R(-,H^{j})
in
T_{o}M
, which implies
F_{j}=\widehat{{F}}
.

Now we prove the second assertion. Given
(o,\mathfrak{{a}},\Upsigma^{+},\upalpha_{j})\in\mathcal{{F}}^{-1}(\upchi)
, we simply assign to it the pair
(B_{j},K^{j}A^{j}N^{j})
. Conversely, suppose we have fixed
\widehat{{B}}\subset\widehat{{F}}
and an Iwasawa decomposition
\widehat{{G}}^{\prime}=\widecheck{K}\widecheck{A}\widecheck{N}
. The latter is equivalent to choosing a point
o\in\widehat{{B}}
(this gives rise to
\mathfrak{{g}}=\mathfrak{{k}}\oplus\mathfrak{{p}}
), a maximal abelian subspace
\widecheck{\mathfrak{{a}}}
in
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{b}}}}\subset\mathfrak{{p}}
, and a set of simple roots
\widehat{{\Uplambda}}
in the resulting restricted root system
\widehat{{\Upsigma}}\subseteq\widecheck{\mathfrak{{a}}}^{*}
. Observe that
\mathfrak{{a}}=\widecheck{\mathfrak{{a}}}\oplus\widehat{{\mathfrak{{a}}}}
is a maximal abelian subspace of
\mathfrak{{p}}
and denote its corresponding root system by
\Upsigma\subset\mathfrak{{a}}^{*}
. Since
\widehat{{B}}
is a boundary component, we know that
\widehat{{\Upsigma}}=\Upsigma\cap\widecheck{\mathfrak{{a}}}^{*}
. Recall that
\widehat{{F}}
is marked, so we can pick a positively oriented vector
X\in\widehat{{\mathfrak{{a}}}}
. We claim that there is a unique completion of
\widehat{{\Uplambda}}
to a set of simple roots for
\Upsigma
all of whom are nonnegative on
X
. Indeed, it follows from our discussion after (
7
) that there exists a choice of simple roots
\Uplambda
for
\Upsigma
and
\upalpha_{j}\in\Uplambda
such that every element of
\Uplambda
is nonnegative on
X
and
\widehat{{\Upsigma}}=\Upsigma_{j}
. Since the Weyl group acts transitively on the set of Weyl chambers, there exists
w\in W(\widehat{{\Upsigma}})
mapping
\Uplambda_{j}
to
\widehat{{\Uplambda}}
. Being a composition of root hyperplane reflections with respect to some roots from
\widehat{{\Uplambda}}
,
w
extends naturally to an element
\widetilde{{w}}\in W(\Upsigma)
. But
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{a}}}}
is orthogonal to
\widecheck{\mathfrak{{a}}}
, which implies that the dual operator of
\widetilde{{w}}
acts as the identity on
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{a}}}}
. Consequently, the system
\widetilde{{w}}(\Uplambda)=\widehat{{\Uplambda}}\sqcup\widetilde{{w}}(\upalpha_{j})
of simple roots for
\Upsigma
is nonnegative on
(\widetilde{{w}}^{*})^{-1}(X)=X
, so we can take it to be our desired completion of
\widehat{{\Uplambda}}
. The uniqueness of such a completion is straightforward. By invoking the discussion after (
7
) one more time, we see that
(o,\mathfrak{{a}},\widetilde{{w}}(\Uplambda),\widetilde{{w}}(\upalpha_{j}))\in\mathcal{{F}}^{-1}(\widehat{{F}})
, hence we can assign this tuple to the pair
(\widehat{{B}},\widecheck{K}\widecheck{A}\widecheck{N})
. It it easy to see that the constructed maps between
\mathcal{{F}}^{-1}(\widehat{{F}})
and the set of such pairs are the inverses of each other. This completes the proof.
∎

We are now ready to formulate the nilpotent construction in this new setting.

Definition 4.9
.

Let
\widehat{{F}}\in\mathcal{{B}}
. A subspace
\mathfrak{{w}}\subseteq\widehat{{\mathfrak{{n}}}}^{1}
is called

(1)

admissible
if
N_{\widehat{{L}}}(\mathfrak{{w}})
acts transitively on the extended boundary component
\widehat{{F}}
,

(2)

protohomogeneous
if some compact subgroup of
N_{\widehat{{L}}}(\mathfrak{{w}})
acts transitively on the set of directions in
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{n}}}}^{1}/\mathfrak{{w}}
(that is, on
\Gr^{+}(1,\widehat{{\mathfrak{{n}}}}^{1}/\mathfrak{{w}})
).
7
7

7

Notice how we had to resort to the quotient representation to avoid talking about orthogonal complements.

If we are also given a tuple
(o,\mathfrak{{a}},\Upsigma^{+},\upalpha_{j})\in\mathcal{{F}}^{-1}(\widehat{{F}})
, we have
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{n}}}}^{1}=\mathfrak{{n}}_{j}^{1}
, so there could potentially be a conflict between this definition and the original one.

Proposition 4.10
.

Let
\widehat{{F}}\in\mathcal{{B}},\,(o,\mathfrak{{a}},\Upsigma^{+},\upalpha_{j})\in\mathcal{{F}}^{-1}(\widehat{{F}}),
and
\mathfrak{{w}}\subseteq\widehat{{\mathfrak{{n}}}}^{1}=\mathfrak{{n}}_{j}^{1}
.

(1)

\mathfrak{{w}}
is admissible with respect to Definition
3.2
if and only if it is such with respect to Definition
4.9
.

(2)

If
\mathfrak{{w}}
is protohomogeneous with respect to Definition
3.2
, it is also such with respect to Definition
4.9
. Conversely, if
\mathfrak{{w}}
is protohomogeneous with respect to Definition
4.9
, there exists
(\overline{o},\overline{\mathfrak{{a}}},\overline{\Upsigma}^{+},\overline{\upalpha}_{l})\in\mathcal{{F}}^{-1}(\widehat{{F}})
such that
\mathfrak{{w}}
is protohomogeneous with respect to Definition
3.2
and that tuple.

(3)

\mathfrak{{w}}
is both admissible and protohomogeneous with respect to Definition
3.2
if and only if it is such with respect to Definition
4.9
.

The asymmetry in part (
) of the proposition is only a technicality and will not cause any problems because we do not care about the protohomogeneity condition on its own—we always consider it only together with admissibility.

Proof of Proposition
4.10
.

The first assertion follows from the fact that
\widehat{{L}}=L_{j}
and
\widehat{{F}}=F_{j}
. To prove (
), notice that the quotient projection
\mathfrak{{v}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 6.3941pt\hskip-6.3941pt\hbox{$\xrightarrow{\phantom{\sim}}$}\hskip-6.3941pt\hskip-4.76384pt\raisebox{0.64444pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\sim\,$}}\hskip-4.76384pt\hskip 6.3941pt}}\mathfrak{{n}}_{j}^{1}/\mathfrak{{w}}
is an isomorphism of
N_{K_{j}}(\mathfrak{{w}})
-representations. If
N_{K_{j}}(\mathfrak{{w}})
acts transitively on the unit sphere in
\mathfrak{{v}}
, it also does so on the set of directions in
\mathfrak{{v}}
and thus in
\mathfrak{{n}}_{j}^{1}/\mathfrak{{w}}=\widehat{{\mathfrak{{n}}}}^{1}/\mathfrak{{w}}
. This proves the first assertion, as
N_{K_{j}}(\mathfrak{{w}})
is a compact subgroup of
N_{L_{j}}(\mathfrak{{w}})=N_{\widehat{{L}}}(\mathfrak{{w}})
. For the converse, let
K_{1}
be a compact subgroup of
N_{\widehat{{L}}}(\mathfrak{{w}})
that acts transitively on
\Gr^{+}(1,\widehat{{\mathfrak{{n}}}}^{1}/\mathfrak{{w}})
. Pick a maximal compact subgroup
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.0mu\widehat{{K}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
of
\widehat{{L}}
containing
K_{1}
. By the Cartan fixed point theorem applied to
\widehat{{L}}\curvearrowright\widehat{{F}}
,
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.0mu\widehat{{K}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
must be the isotropy of some point
\overline{o}\in\widehat{{F}}
. Complete
\overline{o}
to a tuple
(\overline{o},\overline{\mathfrak{{a}}},\overline{\Upsigma}^{+},\overline{\upalpha}_{l})\in\mathcal{{F}}^{-1}(\widehat{{F}})
. By construction,
N_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muK\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{l}}(\mathfrak{{w}})=N_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.0mu\widehat{{K}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}(\mathfrak{{w}})
acts transitively on the set of directions in
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{n}}}}^{1}/\mathfrak{{w}}=\overline{\mathfrak{{n}}}^{1}_{l}/\mathfrak{{w}}
and thus in
\overline{\mathfrak{{v}}}=\overline{\mathfrak{{n}}}^{1}_{l}\ominus\mathfrak{{w}}
, where the orthogonal complement is taken with respect to the new inner product
B_{\overline{\uptheta}}
associated with
\overline{o}
. Since
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muK\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{l}
preserves this inner product, we deduce that it acts transitively on the unit sphere in
\overline{\mathfrak{{v}}}
. This completes the proof of (
) and leaves us only with (
). In light of (
) and (
), we need only show the following: if
\mathfrak{{w}}
is admissible and protohomogeneous with respect to Definition
4.9
, then it is also protohomogeneous with respect to Definition
3.2
(and the same tuple
(o,\mathfrak{{a}},\Upsigma^{+},\upalpha_{j})
). Once again, let
K_{1}
be a compact subgroup of
N_{\widehat{{L}}}(\mathfrak{{w}})
acting transitively on
\Gr^{+}(1,\widehat{{\mathfrak{{n}}}}^{1}/\mathfrak{{w}})
. We may assume that
K_{1}
is a maximal compact subgroup of
N_{\widehat{{L}}}(\mathfrak{{w}})
. But so is
N_{K_{j}}(\mathfrak{{w}})=N_{\widehat{{K}}}(\mathfrak{{w}})
because the quotient
N_{\widehat{{L}}}(\mathfrak{{w}})/N_{\widehat{{K}}}(\mathfrak{{w}})
, which is diffeomorphic to
\widehat{{F}}
by the admissibility, is contractible (see
[
Ant12
]
). Since every two maximal compact subgroups are conjugate, we deduce that
N_{K_{j}}(\mathfrak{{w}})
acts transitively on the set of directions in
\mathfrak{{n}}_{j}^{1}/\mathfrak{{w}}
. By the same argument as above, it also acts transitively on the unit sphere in
\mathfrak{{v}}
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Fix some
\widehat{{F}}\in\mathcal{{B}}
. Given
\mathfrak{{w}}\subseteq\widehat{{\mathfrak{{n}}}}^{1}
, we have a nilpotent subalgebra
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{n}}}}_{\mathfrak{{w}}}=\mathfrak{{w}}\oplus\bigoplus_{\upnu\geq 2}\widehat{{\mathfrak{{n}}}}^{\hskip 0.5pt\upnu}
and its corresponding connected Lie subgroup
\widehat{{N}}_{\mathfrak{{w}}}
. We can also form a semidirect sum
\widehat{{\mathfrak{{h}}}}_{\mathfrak{{w}}}=N_{\widehat{{\mathfrak{{l}}}}}(\mathfrak{{w}})\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\widehat{{\mathfrak{{n}}}}_{\mathfrak{{w}}}
and its corresponding connected Lie subgroup
\widehat{{H}}_{\mathfrak{{w}}}=N^{0}_{\widehat{{L}}}(\mathfrak{{w}})\ltimes\widehat{{N}}_{\mathfrak{{w}}}
. Combining Theorem
3.3
with Proposition
4.10
leads to the following

Corollary 4.11
.

Let
M=G/K
be a symmetric space of noncompact type with a fixed choice of
\widehat{{F}}\in\mathcal{{B}}
. Suppose
\mathfrak{{w}}\subset\widehat{{\mathfrak{{n}}}}^{1}
is an admissible and protohomogeneous subspace of
\codim_{\widehat{{\mathfrak{{n}}}}^{1}}(\mathfrak{{w}})\geq 2
. Then the subgroup
\widehat{{H}}_{\mathfrak{{w}}}
acts on
M
with cohomogeneity one and has
\widehat{{F}}\times\widehat{{N}}_{\mathfrak{{w}}}\subset\widehat{{F}}\times\widehat{{N}}\cong M
as its orbit through
o
. That orbit has codimension equal to
\codim_{\widehat{{\mathfrak{{n}}}}^{1}}(\mathfrak{{w}})
and is thus singular.

Let us now introduce the following sets of
nilpotent construction data
:

\displaystyle\widetilde{{\mathcal{{N}}}}=\{(o,\mathfrak{{a}},\Upsigma^{+},\upalpha_{j},\mathfrak{{w}})\mid\,

\displaystyle(o,\mathfrak{{a}},\Upsigma^{+},\upalpha_{j})\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{{B}}}},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{{w}}\subset\mathfrak{{n}}_{j}^{1}\hskip 4.5pt\text{an admissible protohomogeneous subspace of $\codim\geq 2$}\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{{N}}=\{(\widehat{{F}},\mathfrak{{w}})\mid\,

\displaystyle\widehat{{F}}\in\mathcal{{B}},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{{w}}\subset\widehat{{\mathfrak{{n}}}}^{1}\hskip 4.5pt\text{an admissible protohomogeneous subspace of $\codim\geq 2$}\}.

Thanks to Propositions
4.8
and
4.10
, we have a map
\mathcal{{G}}\colon\widetilde{{\mathcal{{N}}}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{{N}}
that sends
(o,\mathfrak{{a}},\Upsigma^{+},\upalpha_{j},\mathfrak{{w}})
to
(F_{j},\mathfrak{{w}})
, where
F_{j}
is marked by orienting
\mathfrak{{a}}_{j}
in the direction of
H^{j}
. This map is surjective and its fibers admit a simple description thanks to Proposition
4.8
. Let
\mathcal{{C}}
stand for the set of closed connected subgroups of
G
acting on
M
with cohomogeneity one. We can then define another map
\mathcal{{H}}\colon\mathcal{{N}}\to\mathcal{{C}}
that sends
(\widehat{{F}},\mathfrak{{w}})
to
\widehat{{H}}_{\mathfrak{{w}}}
. The original nilpotent construction is then simply the composition
\mathcal{{H}}\circ\mathcal{{G}}
. The group
G
(or even
I(M)
) acts naturally on each of these three sets. For instance, given
g\in G
, we have

g\cdot(o,\mathfrak{{a}},\Upsigma^{+},\upalpha_{j},\mathfrak{{w}})=(g\cdot o,\Ad(g)(\mathfrak{{a}}),\Ad(g)_{\mathfrak{{a}}}(\Upsigma^{+}),\Ad(g)_{\mathfrak{{a}}}(\upalpha_{j}),\Ad(g)(\mathfrak{{w}})),

where
\Ad(g)_{\mathfrak{{a}}}=({\left.\kern-1.2pt\Ad(g)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathfrak{{a}}}}^{*})^{-1}\colon\mathfrak{{a}}^{*}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 6.3941pt\hskip-6.3941pt\hbox{$\xrightarrow{\phantom{\sim}}$}\hskip-6.3941pt\hskip-4.76384pt\raisebox{0.64444pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\sim\,$}}\hskip-4.76384pt\hskip 6.3941pt}}\Ad(g)(\mathfrak{{a}})^{*}
. It is a matter of routine checks that this map is well-defined and that
\Ad(g)(\mathfrak{{w}})
is admissible and protohomogeneous. Essentially, this boils down to the fact that all the relevant subalgebras and representations arising from the 5-tuple on the right are identified with those arising on the left via
\Ad(g)
. Similarly,
G
acts on
\mathcal{{N}}
by
g\cdot(\widehat{{F}},\mathfrak{{w}})=(g(\widehat{{F}}),\Ad(g)(\mathfrak{{w}}))
, where the orientation on the flat factor of
g(\widehat{{F}})
is carried over from
\widehat{{F}}
via
g
. Finally,
G
acts on
\mathcal{{C}}
via subgroup conjugation. One can readily see that the maps
\mathcal{{G}}
and
\mathcal{{H}}
are
G
-equivariant (see
[
Sol24
, Lem. 4.1.5]
for a similar statement and its proof).

Remark 4.12
.

If we already have some nilpotent construction data
(\widehat{{F}},\mathfrak{{w}})\in\mathcal{{N}}
fixed, it is useful to consider the action of
\widehat{{L}}
on
\mathcal{{N}}
and its orbit through
(\widehat{{F}},\mathfrak{{w}})
: since
\widehat{{L}}
preserves
