\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}(\widetilde{{\mathfrak{{a}}}}_{nc}^{\perp})^{*},|\langle\hskip 1.0pt{H^{\prime\prime}}\hskip 0.5pt,{\upalpha}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt|<|\langle\hskip 1.0pt{H^{\prime}}\hskip 0.5pt,{\upalpha}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt|
.

Finally, we let
H=H^{\prime}+H^{\prime\prime}
. We can now make the following choices of positive roots:

\displaystyle\widetilde{{\Upsigma}}^{+}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\{\widetilde{{\upalpha}}\in\widetilde{{\Upsigma}}\mid\langle\hskip 1.0pt{H^{\prime}}\hskip 0.5pt,{\widetilde{{\upalpha}}}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt>0\},

\displaystyle\Upsigma^{+}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{\upalpha\in\Upsigma\mid\langle\hskip 1.0pt{H}\hskip 0.5pt,{\upalpha}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt>0}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}.

By construction,
H^{\prime}
does not vanish on any roots in
\widetilde{{\Upsigma}}
, and the same is true for
H
and
\Upsigma
, so these are indeed valid choices of positive roots for the two root systems. Given
\widetilde{{\upalpha}}\in\widetilde{{\Upsigma}}^{+}
and any
\upalpha\in\Upsigma(\widetilde{{\upalpha}})
, we have
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{H}\hskip 0.5pt,{\upalpha}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt=\langle\hskip 1.0pt{H^{\prime}}\hskip 0.5pt,{\upalpha}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt+\langle\hskip 1.0pt{H^{\prime\prime}}\hskip 0.5pt,{\upalpha}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt
, which has the same sign as
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{H^{\prime}}\hskip 0.5pt,{\upalpha}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt=\langle\hskip 1.0pt{H^{\prime}}\hskip 0.5pt,{\widetilde{{\upalpha}}}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt>0
thanks to (
) above. This implies
\upalpha\in\Upsigma^{+}
and thus
\Upsigma(\widetilde{{\upalpha}})\subseteq\Upsigma^{+}
. In view of (
8
), we arrive at:

(9)

\widetilde{{\mathfrak{{n}}}}=\bigoplus_{\widetilde{{\upalpha}}\in\widetilde{{\Upsigma}}^{+}}(\widetilde{{\mathfrak{{g}}}}_{nc})_{\widetilde{{\upalpha}}}\subseteq\bigoplus_{\upalpha\in\Upsigma^{+}}\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}=\mathfrak{{n}}.

Having made all these choices, we have the induced Iwasawa decompositions
G=KAN
and
\widetilde{{G}}_{nc}=\widetilde{{K}}\widetilde{{A}}_{nc}\widetilde{{N}}
. By virtue of (
9
), we have an inclusion between the solvable parts
\widetilde{{A}}_{nc}\widetilde{{N}}\subseteq AN
, and the orbit of the former through
o
is still the whole
S_{nc}
. We can also consider the subgroup
\widetilde{{A}}\widetilde{{N}}=\widetilde{{A}}_{nc}\widetilde{{A}}_{0}\widetilde{{N}}
of
AN
, whose orbit through
o
is the original submanifold
S
. We arrive at the following

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
M=G/K
be a symmetric space of noncompact type and
S\subseteq M
a complete connected totally geodesic submanifold. Then there exist an Iwasawa decomposition
G=KAN
and a connected Lie subgroup
H\subseteq AN
,
H=(H\cap A)\ltimes(H\cap N)
, such that
S=H\cdot o
.

We are now in a position to prove a result similar to Proposition
5.1
but pertaining to singular orbits of C1-actions. We will formulate it in a slightly more general setting to include actions without singular orbits.

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
M=G/K
be a symmetric space of noncompact type and
H
a connected Lie group acting on
M
properly, isometrically, and with cohomogeneity one. Then there exist an Iwasawa decomposition
G=KAN
and a connected Lie subgroup
H^{\prime}\subset AN
of the form
H^{\prime}=(H^{\prime}\cap A)\ltimes(H^{\prime}\cap N)
such that the following is true:

(1)

If
H
has no singular orbits, then its orbit foliation coincides with that of
H^{\prime}
.

(2)

If
H
has a singular orbit, then that orbit coincides with the orbit of
H^{\prime}
through the base point
o
.

Proof.

Part (
) follows directly from
[
BT03
]
. Suppose
H
has a singular orbit
S
. (As we know from Subsection
3.1
, it must then be unique.) If
S
is totally geodesic, the assertion follows immediately from Proposition
5.1
. Otherwise, in view of Theorem
3.5
, the action of
H
has to come from either the canonical extension or the nilpotent construction up to orbit-equivalence. First, consider the latter. It follows from Theorem
4.18
that there exist nilpotent construction data
(o,\mathfrak{{a}},\Upsigma^{+},\upalpha_{j},\mathfrak{{w}})
with
A^{j}N^{j}\subseteq N^{0}_{M_{j}}(\mathfrak{{w}})
such that the orbits of
H
coincide with those of
H_{j,\mathfrak{{w}}}=(N^{0}_{M_{j}}(\mathfrak{{w}})\times A_{j})\ltimes N_{j,\mathfrak{{w}}}
. Note that the orbit of
A^{j}N^{j}
through
o
is the same as that of
N^{0}_{M_{j}}(\mathfrak{{w}})
—i.e., the whole boundary component
B_{j}
. But then we can consider the Lie subgroup
H^{\prime}=(A^{j}N^{j}\times A_{j})\ltimes N_{j,\mathfrak{{w}}}=A\ltimes(N^{j}\ltimes N_{j,\mathfrak{{w}}})\subseteq H_{j,\mathfrak{{w}}}
. With respect to the horospherical decomposition, the orbit of
H^{\prime}
through
o
is given by
B_{j}\times A_{j}\times N_{j,\mathfrak{{w}}}
, which is nothing but the
o
-orbit of
H_{j,\mathfrak{{w}}}
and thus the singular orbit of
H
. Plainly,
H^{\prime}
satisfies the desired requirements.

Finally, assume that the action of
H
arises from the canonical extension. This means that there exist a choice of
o\in M,\,\mathfrak{{a}}\subset\mathfrak{{p}},\,\Upsigma^{+}\subset\Upsigma
, and
\Upphi\subset\Uplambda
and a closed connected subgroup
L\subset G^{\prime}_{\Upphi}
such that the canonical extension of
L\curvearrowright B_{\Upphi}
has the same orbits in
M
as
H
. (We are assuming that the singular orbit of
H
passes through
o
.) The action of
L
on
B_{\Upphi}
also has cohomogeneity one, and with respect to the horospherical decomposition, the singular orbit of
H
is given by
(L\cdot o)\times A_{\Upphi}\times N_{\Upphi}
. Since the canonical extension procedure is transitive, we may assume the action
L\curvearrowright B_{\Upphi}
does not come from any further canonical extension from a proper boundary component of
B_{\Upphi}
. Thanks to Theorem
3.5
, this means that this action must either come from the nilpotent construction or have a totally geodesic singular orbit. Either way, we already know by now that it must then satisfy the assertion of the proposition: there exists an Iwasawa decomposition
G^{\prime}_{\Upphi}=\widecheck{K}\widecheck{A}\widecheck{N}
(this entails a new base point
p\in B_{\Upphi}
) and a connected Lie subgroup
L^{\prime}\subset\widecheck{A}\widecheck{N}
of the form
L^{\prime}=(L^{\prime}\cap\widecheck{A})\ltimes(L^{\prime}\cap\widecheck{N})
such that the singular orbit
L\cdot o
of
L
in
B_{\Upphi}
coincides with
L^{\prime}\cdot p
. This last condition implies that
p
lies on
L\cdot o
, hence there exists an element
\upvarphi\in G^{\prime}_{\Upphi}
that preserves
L\cdot o
and maps
o
to
p
(
\upvarphi
can be taken to lie in
L
or
L^{\prime}
). By conjugating the new Iwasawa decomposition of
G^{\prime}_{\Upphi}
by
\upvarphi
, we can additionally assume that
p=o
. (We keep the rest of the notation the same.) In particular, we have
\widecheck{K}=K^{\Upphi}
, as both are now the stabilizers of
o
in
G^{\prime}_{\Upphi}
.

We claim that
G=K(\widecheck{A}A_{\Upphi})(\widecheck{N}N_{\Upphi})
is also an Iwasawa decomposition. Indeed, since every two Iwasawa decompositions of
G^{\prime}_{\Upphi}
are inner-conjugate, there exists an element
\uppsi\in G^{\prime}_{\Upphi}
that conjugates
K^{\Upphi},A^{\Upphi},
and
N^{\Upphi}
to
\widecheck{K},\widecheck{A},
and
\widecheck{N}
, respectively. But that element also conjugates the ambient decomposition
G=KAN
to some new Iwasawa decomposition of
G
. Since
G^{\prime}_{\Upphi}
centralizes
A_{\Upphi}
and normalizes
N_{\Upphi}
, we easily compute:

\uppsi A\uppsi^{-1}=\uppsi A^{\Upphi}A_{\Upphi}\uppsi^{-1}=\uppsi A^{\Upphi}\uppsi^{-1}A_{\Upphi}=\widecheck{A}A_{\Upphi},

and the same for
N=N^{\Upphi}N_{\Upphi}
. Finally, note that
\uppsi
conjugates
K^{\Upphi}=\widecheck{K}
to itself, which means that it preserves
o
and thus
\uppsi K\uppsi^{-1}=K
. This proves our claim. Now,
G=K(\widecheck{A}A_{\Upphi})(\widecheck{N}N_{\Upphi})
is going to be our desired Iwasawa decomposition. As for the group
H^{\prime}
, we take it to be

H^{\prime}=[(L^{\prime}\cap\widecheck{A})\times A_{\Upphi}]\ltimes[(L^{\prime}\cap\widecheck{N})\ltimes N_{\Upphi}].

We need to show that
H^{\prime}\cdot o=H\cdot o=(L\cdot o)\times A_{\Upphi}\times N_{\Upphi}
, where the latter equality is taken with respect to the horospherical decomposition
M\cong B_{\Upphi}\times A_{\Upphi}\times N_{\Upphi}
. Note that with respect to the Langlands decomposition
Q_{\Upphi}=M_{\Upphi}\times A_{\Upphi}\ltimes N_{\Upphi}
, we can rewrite
H^{\prime}
simply as
L^{\prime}\times A_{\Upphi}\ltimes N_{\Upphi}
. But thanks to (
1
), the orbit of this through
o
is
(L^{\prime}\cdot o)\times A_{\Upphi}\times N_{\Upphi}
. By construction,
L^{\prime}\cdot o=L\cdot o
. This completes our proof.
∎

When combined, Propositions
5.1
and
5.2
imply Theorem
B
. We close this section with an open question. Note that submanifolds of both types (
) and (
) in Theorem
B
are examples of homogeneous CPC submanifolds. (Here by a
homogeneous submanifold
we mean an orbit of an isometric Lie group action.) This class is known to contain many other types of submanifolds (see
[
BSL23
]
), but all of them share one thing in common: they can be realized by a Lie subgroup in the solvable model of
M
. This leads to a natural question:

Open question
.

Let
M=G/K
be a symmetric space of noncompact type. Can every connected homogeneous CPC submanifold
S\subset M
be realized as
H\cdot o
for some Iwasawa decomposition
G=KAN
and connected Lie subgroup
H\subseteq AN
?

To our knowledge, there are even no known examples of homogeneous
minimal
submanifolds that cannot be realized in this way.

6.
Solving the nilpotent construction problem

In this final section, we fully resolve the nilpotent construction problem for all symmetric spaces of noncompact type and rank greater than one. In Section
4
, we squeezed everything we could out of the admissibility condition—which culminated in Theorem
4.18
. So in this section, our focus will be on the remaining protohomogeneity condition. Using the conclusions of Theorem
4.18
, we are going to investigate this condition closely within the framework of the restricted root space decomposition and obtain strong restrictions on the subspace
\mathfrak{{w}}
, the root system
\Upsigma
, and the root multiplicities. Ultimately, this will allow us to determine all possible choices of
\mathfrak{{w}}
for all the aforementioned spaces. We will conclude the section by proving the
Main theorem
.

From now on and until the end of the section, we fix a symmetric space
M=G/K
of noncompact type and let
(o,\mathfrak{{a}},\Upsigma^{+},\upalpha_{j},\mathfrak{{w}})
be some
positive
nilpotent construction data (unless otherwise mentioned). We stress that most of the results of this section do not apply to nonpositive nilpotent construction data. We assume that the metric on
M
is Killing (Remark
3.6
). Since the nilpotent construction problem has been fully resolved in rank one, we also assume that
\rk(M)>1
. Finally, we assume that
M
is irreducible, which is justified by the comment right after Theorem
3.5
. By virtue of Theorem
4.18
, the subspace
\mathfrak{{w}}
splits as
\mathfrak{{w}}=\bigoplus_{\uplambda\in\Updelta_{j}^{1}}\mathfrak{{w}}_{\uplambda}
, and hence so does the normal space
\mathfrak{{v}}=\mathfrak{{n}}_{j}^{1}\ominus\mathfrak{{w}}=\bigoplus_{\uplambda\in\Updelta_{j}^{1}}\mathfrak{{v}}_{\uplambda}
. (Recall that we denote
\mathfrak{{w}}_{\uplambda}=\mathfrak{{w}}\cap\mathfrak{{g}}_{\uplambda}
and
\mathfrak{{v}}_{\uplambda}=\mathfrak{{v}}\cap\mathfrak{{g}}_{\uplambda}
.) Moreover, the normalizer
N_{\mathfrak{{m}}_{j}}(\mathfrak{{w}})
contains
\mathfrak{{a}}^{j}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}^{j}
. To start off, we show that this normalizer also decomposes nicely with respect to the restricted root space decomposition.

Lemma 6.1
.

We have:

N_{\mathfrak{{m}}_{j}}(\mathfrak{{w}})=N_{\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}}(\mathfrak{{w}})\oplus\mathfrak{{a}}^{j}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}^{j}\oplus\bigoplus_{\upalpha\in\Upsigma_{j}^{-}}N_{\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}}(\mathfrak{{w}})\quad\text{and}\quad N_{\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}}(\mathfrak{{w}})=\bigcap_{\uplambda\in\Updelta_{j}^{1}}N_{\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}}(\mathfrak{{w}}_{\uplambda}).

Proof.

This lemma can be proven in a fashion similar to how we proved Lemma
4.16
. However, we will take a slightly different approach. Take any vector
X=X_{0}+H+\sum_{\upalpha\in\Upsigma_{j}}X_{\upalpha}
in
N_{\mathfrak{{m}}_{j}}(\mathfrak{{w}})
, where
X_{0}\in\mathfrak{{k}}_{0},\,H\in\mathfrak{{a}}^{j},
and
X_{\upalpha}\in\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}
. We want to show that each of these summands lies in the normalizer by itself. Since we already know that
\mathfrak{{a}}^{j}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}^{j}\subseteq N_{\mathfrak{{m}}_{j}}(\mathfrak{{w}})
, we may assume that
H=0
and
X_{\upalpha}=0
whenever
\upalpha
is positive. Now take
\upalpha\in\Upsigma_{j}^{-}
. In order to normalize
\mathfrak{{w}}
,
X_{\upalpha}
should send
\mathfrak{{w}}_{\uplambda}
to
\mathfrak{{w}}
for each
\uplambda\in\Updelta_{j}^{1}
. Take any such
\uplambda
and fix an arbitrary
Y\in\mathfrak{{w}}_{\uplambda}
. We have
[X,Y]=[X_{0},Y]+\sum_{\upalpha\in\Upsigma_{j}^{-}}[X_{\upalpha},Y]
. Each summand on the right-hand side lies in a single root space. What is more, the only summand that lies in
\mathfrak{{g}}_{\uplambda+\upalpha}
and is potentially nonzero is
[X_{\upalpha},Y]
. Since
[X,Y]\in\mathfrak{{w}}
, we see that
[X_{\upalpha},Y]
lies in
\mathfrak{{w}}_{\uplambda+\upalpha}
. This shows that
X_{\upalpha}
sends
\mathfrak{{w}}_{\uplambda}
to
\mathfrak{{w}}
and hence
X_{\upalpha}\in N_{\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}}(\mathfrak{{w}})=N_{\mathfrak{{m}}_{j}}(\mathfrak{{w}})\cap\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}
. As this applies to every
\upalpha\in\Upsigma_{j}^{-}
, we are left with
X_{0}\in N_{\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}}(\mathfrak{{w}})
. The second equality in the lemma is straightforward, as
\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}
preserves every root space.
∎

There are three normalizers at play in the nilpotent construction:
N_{\mathfrak{{m}}_{j}}(\mathfrak{{w}}),N_{\mathfrak{{m}}_{j}}(\mathfrak{{v}}),
and
N_{\mathfrak{{k}}_{j}}(\mathfrak{{v}})
. Our next goal is to relate them with each other.

Lemma 6.2
.

The following relations hold:

(1)

N_{\mathfrak{{m}}_{j}}(\mathfrak{{v}})=\uptheta N_{\mathfrak{{m}}_{j}}(\mathfrak{{w}})
.

(2)

For any
\upalpha\in\Upsigma_{j}^{+}
,
N_{\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}}(\mathfrak{{v}})=\uptheta N_{\mathfrak{{g}}_{-\upalpha}}(\mathfrak{{w}})
. We denote this subspace of
\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}
by
\bm{\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\upalpha}}
.

(3)

For any
\upbeta\in\Upsigma_{j}^{-}
,
N_{\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upbeta}}(\mathfrak{{w}})=\uptheta N_{\mathfrak{{g}}_{-\upbeta}}(\mathfrak{{v}})
. We denote this subspace of
\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upbeta}
by
\bm{\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\upbeta}}
.

(4)

For any
\upalpha\in\Upsigma_{j}
,
N_{\mathfrak{{k}}_{\upalpha}}(\mathfrak{{v}})=N_{\mathfrak{{k}}_{\upalpha}}(\mathfrak{{w}})=\pr_{\mathfrak{{k}}_{\upalpha}}(\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\upalpha})=\pr_{\mathfrak{{k}}_{\upalpha}}(\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{-\upalpha})
. We denote this subspace of
\mathfrak{{k}}_{\upalpha}
by
\bm{\mathfrak{{k}}^{\prime}_{\upalpha}}
.

The projections in part (
) are assumed to be—as always—orthogonal with respect to
B_{\uptheta}
.

Proof of Lemma
6.2
.

Part (
) is a special case of Lemma
3.1
(
). Part (
) follows from (
) because
N_{\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}}(\mathfrak{{v}})=N_{\mathfrak{{m}}_{j}}(\mathfrak{{v}})\hskip 1.0pt\cap\hskip 1.0pt\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}
and
\uptheta
interchanges
\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}
and
\mathfrak{{g}}_{-\upalpha}
. Part (
) follows from (
), since
\uptheta
is an involution. Note that, by definition,
\uptheta
interchanges
\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\upalpha}
and
\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{-\upalpha}
for any
\upalpha\in\Upsigma_{j}
. This immediately implies the last equality in (
). The first equality therein follows from Lemma
3.1
(
). Finally, assuming
\upalpha\in\Upsigma_{j}^{+}
without loss of generality, we calculate:

\displaystyle N_{\mathfrak{{k}}_{\upalpha}}(\mathfrak{{w}})

\displaystyle=(N_{\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}}(\mathfrak{{w}})\oplus N_{\mathfrak{{g}}_{-\upalpha}}(\mathfrak{{w}}))\cap\mathfrak{{k}}

\displaystyle=(\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}\oplus\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{-\upalpha})\cap\mathfrak{{k}}

\displaystyle(\text{Lemma \ref{lem:normalizer_RRSD}})

\displaystyle=(\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\upalpha}\oplus\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{-\upalpha})\cap\mathfrak{{k}}=\pr_{\mathfrak{{k}}_{\upalpha}}(\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\upalpha})

\displaystyle(\uptheta\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\upalpha}=\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{-\upalpha}).

Combining Lemmas
6.1
and
6.2
yields the following neat description of the three normalizers:

Corollary 6.3
.

We have:

(1)

N_{\mathfrak{{m}}_{j}}(\mathfrak{{w}})=N_{\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}}(\mathfrak{{w}})\oplus\mathfrak{{a}}^{j}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}^{j}\oplus\bigoplus_{\upalpha\in\Upsigma_{j}^{-}}\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\upalpha}
.

(2)

N_{\mathfrak{{m}}_{j}}(\mathfrak{{v}})=N_{\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}}(\mathfrak{{v}})\oplus\mathfrak{{a}}^{j}\oplus\bigoplus_{\upalpha\in\Upsigma_{j}^{+}}\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\upalpha}\oplus\uptheta\mathfrak{{n}}^{j}
.

(3)

N_{\mathfrak{{k}}_{j}}(\mathfrak{{v}})=N_{\mathfrak{{k}}_{j}}(\mathfrak{{w}})=N_{\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}}(\mathfrak{{v}})\oplus\bigoplus_{\upalpha\in\Upsigma_{j}^{+}}\mathfrak{{k}}^{\prime}_{\upalpha}
.

(4)

N_{\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}}(\mathfrak{{v}})=N_{\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}}(\mathfrak{{w}})=\bigcap_{\hskip 1.0pt\uplambda\in\Updelta_{j}^{1}}N_{\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}}(\mathfrak{{v}}_{\uplambda})=\bigcap_{\hskip 1.0pt\uplambda\in\Updelta_{j}^{1}}N_{\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}}(\mathfrak{{w}}_{\uplambda})
.

Now we come to the crux of our argument, in which we will render the protohomogeneity condition considerably more accessible and relate it to the roots. Recall that this condition asks for the representation
N_{K_{j}}^{0}(\mathfrak{{v}})\curvearrowright\mathfrak{{v}}
to be of cohomogeneity one, or equivalently, to be transitive on the spheres centered at the origin. In general, the problem of determining whether an orthogonal representation is transitive on spheres can be easily restated on the level of Lie algebras. Given a Lie algebra representation
\uprho\colon\mathfrak{{g}}\to\mathfrak{{gl}}(V)
and a vector
v\in V
, we write
\mathfrak{{g}}\cdot v=\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{\uprho(X)v\mid X\in\mathfrak{{g}}}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
G
be a compact Lie group equipped with a finite-dimensional representation
\uprho\colon G\to\GL(V)
, and consider the induced representation
\uprho_{*}
of
\mathfrak{{g}}=\Lie(G)
on
V
. The following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

The action
G\curvearrowright V
is of cohomogeneity one.

(2)

For some
(
hence any
)
nonzero vector
v\in V
,
\mathfrak{{g}}\cdot v
is a hyperplane in
V
.

If these conditions are satisfied and we have a fixed
G
-invariant inner product on
V
, then
\mathfrak{{g}}\cdot v=v^{\perp}
.

Proof.

First, we claim that the orbit of a nonzero vector
v\in V
is a hypersurface if and only if
\mathfrak{{g}}\cdot v
is a hyperplane. Indeed, that orbit is a hypersurface precisely when
T_{v}(G\cdot v)
is a hyperplane, so it suffices to show that, in general,
T_{v}(G\cdot v)=\mathfrak{{g}}\cdot v
. Given
X\in\mathfrak{{g}}
, let us write
\widehat{{X}}
for its corresponding fundamental vector field on
V
. The tangent space in question can be described as

T_{v}(G\cdot v)=\bigl\{\widehat{{X}}(v)\mid X\in\mathfrak{{g}}\bigr\}.

So the claim will follow if we show that, under the identification
T_{v}V\cong V
, one has
\widehat{{X}}(v)=\uprho_{*}(X)v
. This is a matter of a standard computation:

\displaystyle\widehat{{X}}(v)

\displaystyle={\left.\kern-1.2pt\frac{d}{dt}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{t=0}}\uprho(\exp(tX))v={\left.\kern-1.2pt\frac{d}{dt}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{t=0}}e^{t\uprho_{*}(X)}v

\displaystyle={\left.\kern-1.2pt\frac{d}{dt}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{t=0}}\left(v+t\uprho_{*}(X)v+\frac{t^{2}\uprho_{*}(X)^{2}}{2!}v+\cdots\right)=\uprho_{*}(X)v.

The action
G\curvearrowright V
has cohomogeneity one precisely when it has at least one codimension-one orbit, so the above claim implies that (
) is equivalent to (
) being true for some
v
. To show that it is then true for every nonzero vector, equip
V
with a
G
-invariant inner product. The orbit of
v
is bound to be the sphere of radius
||v||
centered at the origin. But then every nonzero orbit of
G
is also a sphere, so condition (
) holds for every nonzero vector. Note that, with respect to an invariant inner product, the representation
\uprho_{*}
is by skew-symmetric operators, hence
\mathfrak{{g}}\cdot v\perp v
, which implies the last assertion.
∎

This lemma yields the following reformulation of the protohomogeneity condition:

Corollary 6.5
.

Let
M=G/K
be a symmetric space of noncompact type with a fixed choice of
o\in M,\,\mathfrak{{a}}\subset\mathfrak{{p}},\,\Upsigma^{+}\subset\Upsigma\subset\mathfrak{{a}}^{*},
and
\upalpha_{j}\in\Uplambda
. Then a subspace
\mathfrak{{w}}\subset\mathfrak{{n}}_{j}^{1}
is protohomogeneous if and only if for some
(
and hence any
)
nonzero
v\in\mathfrak{{v}}=\mathfrak{{n}}_{j}^{1}\ominus\mathfrak{{w}}
,
[N_{\mathfrak{{k}}_{j}}(\mathfrak{{v}}),v]=\mathfrak{{v}}\ominus\mathbb{R}v
.

Note that in this corollary, we do not require
\mathfrak{{w}}
to be admissible, let alone the 5-tuple
(o,\mathfrak{{a}},\Upsigma^{+},\upalpha_{j},\mathfrak{{w}})
to be positive. Let us now come back to our problem. In general, Corollary
6.5
is just a linearized reformulation of the protohomogeneity condition and is not of much use. Everything changes when we restrict to positive nilpotent construction data and put it in the context of the results we have accumulated so far.

Proposition 6.6
.

The set
\Updelta_{j}^{1}
and the subspace
\mathfrak{{v}}
are related via the following:

(1)

Whenever
\uplambda,\hskip 1.0pt\upgamma\in\Updelta_{j}^{1}\,

(
\uplambda\neq\upgamma
)
are such that both
\mathfrak{{v}}_{\uplambda}
and
\mathfrak{{v}}_{\upgamma}
are nonzero, we must have
\upgamma-\uplambda\in\Upsigma_{j}
and
\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\upgamma-\uplambda}\neq\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{0}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
. Moreover, for every nonzero
v\in\mathfrak{{v}}_{\uplambda}
, we have
[\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\upgamma-\uplambda},v]=\mathfrak{{v}}_{\upgamma}
.

(2)

For each
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, there is at most one root
\uplambda\in\Updelta_{j}^{1}
of height
k
such that
\mathfrak{{v}}_{\uplambda}\neq\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{0}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
.

Proof.

We begin with part (
). Take any nonzero vector
v\in\mathfrak{{v}}_{\uplambda}
. Thanks to Corollaries
6.5
and
6.3
(
), we have

(10)

(\mathfrak{{v}}_{\uplambda}\ominus\mathbb{R}v)\oplus\bigoplus_{\updelta\hskip 0.5pt\in\hskip 0.5pt\Updelta_{j}^{1}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to8.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.84 -2.08 L 0 10.03}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\hskip-1.0pt}{\hbox{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to8.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.84 -2.08 L 0 10.03}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\hskip-1.0pt}{\hbox{\hbox to2.95pt{\vbox to5.45pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.72499pt\lower-0.22499pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.45pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.77 0 L -0.69 6.92}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\hskip 1.0pt}{\hbox{\hbox to1.9pt{\vbox to3.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.08 0 L 0 4.15}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\{\uplambda\}}\hskip 1.5pt\mathfrak{{v}}_{\updelta}=[N_{\mathfrak{{k}}_{j}}(\mathfrak{{v}}),v]=[N_{\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}}(\mathfrak{{v}}),v]\oplus\bigoplus_{\upalpha\in\Upsigma_{j}^{+}}[\mathfrak{{k}}^{\prime}_{\upalpha},v].

Assume for a moment that
\hgt(\upgamma)\geq\hgt(\uplambda)
. The only summand on the right-hand side of (
10
) that can have a nonzero projection in
\mathfrak{{v}}_{\upgamma}\neq\{0\}
is
[\mathfrak{{k}}^{\prime}_{\upgamma-\uplambda},v]
—but this is only possible when
\upgamma-\uplambda\in\Upsigma_{j}^{+}
and
\mathfrak{{k}}^{\prime}_{\upgamma-\uplambda}\neq\{0\}
. If
\hgt(\upgamma)<\hgt(\uplambda)
, the same is true with
\upgamma-\uplambda
replaced by
\uplambda-\upgamma
. (Note that
\mathfrak{{k}}^{\prime}_{\upgamma-\uplambda}=\mathfrak{{k}}^{\prime}_{\uplambda-\upgamma}
.) In any case, we see that
\upgamma-\uplambda
must be a root. This root is either positive or negative, which means that
\uplambda
and
\upgamma
cannot have the same height. This proves part (
), while we continue with part (
). Since
\mathfrak{{k}}^{\prime}_{\upgamma-\uplambda}\neq\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{0}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
, we also have
\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\upgamma-\uplambda}\neq\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{0}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
(and thus
\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\uplambda-\upgamma}=\uptheta\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\upgamma-\uplambda}\neq\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{0}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
), which follows from Lemma
6.2
(
). We are left to show that
[\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\upgamma-\uplambda},v]=\mathfrak{{v}}_{\upgamma}
. The left-to-right inclusion follows from the definition of
\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\upgamma-\uplambda}
(Lemma
6.2
(
)). For the other direction, take any vector
w\in\mathfrak{{v}}_{\upgamma}
. By (
10
), there must exist an element
X+\uptheta X\in\mathfrak{{k}}^{\prime}_{\upgamma-\uplambda}
(here
X\in\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\upgamma-\uplambda}
in accordance with Lemma
6.2
(
)) such that
[X+\uptheta X,v]=w
. The fact that this lies in
\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upgamma}
forces
[\uptheta X,v]
to be zero, which leaves us with
[X,v]=w
. This completes the proof.
∎

The upshot of the above proposition is that, whenever the normal space
\mathfrak{{v}}
has nonzero summands in different root spaces, that has direct implications for the root system
\Upsigma_{j}
and how it interacts with
\Updelta_{j}^{1}
. As explained at the end of Subsection
2.2
,
\Updelta_{j}^{1}
can be regarded as the
\Uplambda_{j}
-string of
\upalpha_{j}
. Note that
\upalpha_{j}
is a root of minimum height in this string, and any other root therein has height at least two. By invoking
[
SL24
, Prop. 3.3]
, we obtain:

Corollary 6.7
.

For every
\uplambda\in\Updelta_{j}^{1}
of height
\geq 2
, there exists a simple root
\upalpha_{i}\in\Uplambda_{j}
such that
\uplambda-\upalpha_{i}\in\Updelta_{j}^{1}
.

Equipped with this corollary—which will prove extremely useful—we can establish the following important result:

Proposition 6.8
.

If there exists
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
such that, for every root
\uplambda\in\Updelta_{j}^{1}
of height
k
, we have
\mathfrak{{v}}_{\uplambda}=\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{0}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
, then the same is true for every height
m>k
.

Proof.

We can argue inductively, so it suffices to show that for every
\upgamma\in\Updelta_{j}^{1}
of height
k+1
,
\mathfrak{{v}}_{\upgamma}=\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{0}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
. By Corollary
6.7
, there exists
\upalpha_{i}\in\Uplambda_{j}
such that
\upgamma-\upalpha_{i}\in\Updelta_{j}^{1}
. We have
\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha_{i}}\subset N_{\mathfrak{{m}}_{j}}(\mathfrak{{w}})
(by Corollary
6.3
(
)) and
\mathfrak{{w}}_{\upgamma-\upalpha_{i}}=\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upgamma-\upalpha_{i}}
(by assumption). Therefore,

\mathfrak{{w}}_{\upgamma}\supseteq[\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha_{i}},\mathfrak{{w}}_{\upgamma-\upalpha_{i}}]=[\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha_{i}},\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upgamma-\upalpha_{i}}]=\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upgamma},

which means
\mathfrak{{w}}_{\upgamma}=\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upgamma}
, or in other words,
\mathfrak{{v}}_{\upgamma}=\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{0}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
.
∎

Since
\mathfrak{{v}}\neq\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{0}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
and the only root of height 1 in
\Updelta_{j}^{1}
is
\upalpha_{j}
, this proposition implies in particular that
\mathfrak{{v}}_{\upalpha_{j}}\neq\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{0}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
. Combining Propositions
6.6
(
) and
6.8
leads to the following conclusion:

Corollary 6.9
.

There exist
m\geq 1
and roots
\uplambda_{1},\ldots,\uplambda_{m}\in\Updelta_{j}^{1}
with
\hgt(\uplambda_{k})=k
such that
\mathfrak{{v}}=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{m}\mathfrak{{v}}_{\uplambda_{k}}
and
\mathfrak{{v}}_{\uplambda_{k}}\neq\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{0}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
for each
k=1,\ldots,m
.

From here on, and for the rest of the section, we fix
m
and
\uplambda_{1},\ldots,\uplambda_{m}
as in the corollary. As is becoming evident by now, applying Lemma
6.4
to our problem has led to a number of powerful conclusions. We are now going to introduce the second key ingredient of this section, which will help immensely in our arguments and yield strong restrictions on
\mathfrak{{v}}
. The main idea is simple: we may assume that the C1-action resulting from the nilpotent construction cannot be obtained via canonical extension—otherwise, we could disregard it. Our goal is the following proposition, which, loosely speaking, says that
\mathfrak{{v}}
has to be large enough for the resulting action to not come from the canonical extension.

Proposition 6.10
.

Suppose there exists
\upalpha_{i}\in\Uplambda_{j}
such that
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{H^{i}}\hskip 0.5pt,{\uplambda_{k}}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt=0
for each
k=1,\ldots,m
. Then the resulting C1-action on
M
is the canonical extension of a C1-action on the boundary component
B_{i}
.

Before we proceed with the proof, we need the following technical

Lemma 6.11
.

Let
M=G/K
be a symmetric space of noncompact type with
o\in M,\mathfrak{{a}}\subset\mathfrak{{p}},
and
\Upsigma^{+}\subset\Upsigma\subset\mathfrak{{a}}^{*}
fixed. Pick a subset
\Upphi\subseteq\Uplambda
and consider the subalgebra
\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\Upphi}
. The adjoint representation of
\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}
on
\mathfrak{{g}}
preserves
\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\Upphi}
and factors through the adjoint representation
\mathfrak{{k}}^{\Upphi}_{0}\to\mathfrak{{gl}}(\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\Upphi})
. To be more precise, there exists a surjective Lie algebra homomorphism
\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}\twoheadrightarrow\mathfrak{{k}}^{\Upphi}_{0}
that makes the following diagram commutative:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{{k}}^{\Upphi}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{{gl}}(\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\Upphi})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ad}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ad}

Roughly speaking, this lemma says the following. We have the isometry Lie algebra
\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\Upphi}
of the boundary component
B_{\Upphi}
, as well as its
\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}
-part
\mathfrak{{k}}^{\Upphi}_{0}
. The representation of
\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}
on
\mathfrak{{g}}
(and of
\mathfrak{{k}}^{\Upphi}_{0}
on
\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\Upphi}
) is an important part of the whole adjoint representation, as it preserves the restricted root space decomposition and acts on each root space individually. We can restrict the representation of
\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}
to
\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\Upphi}
and see if it brings any new operators. Lemma
6.11
states that the answer is “no”: all such operators already come from
\mathfrak{{k}}^{\Upphi}_{0}
.

Proof of Lemma
6.11
.

Recall that the adjoint representation of
\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}
on
\mathfrak{{g}}
preserves each root space. Since
\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\Upphi}
is generated by
\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha},\,\upalpha\in\Upsigma_{\Upphi}
, it is also preserved by
\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}
. The representation of
\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}
on
\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\Upphi}
is by derivations. But
\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\Upphi}
is semisimple, so we have an isomorphism
\ad\colon\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\Upphi}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 6.3941pt\hskip-6.3941pt\hbox{$\xrightarrow{\phantom{\sim}}$}\hskip-6.3941pt\hskip-4.76384pt\raisebox{0.64444pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\sim\,$}}\hskip-4.76384pt\hskip 6.3941pt}}\Der(\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\Upphi})
. Therefore, the representation
\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}\to\Der(\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\Upphi})
lifts to a Lie algebra homomorphism
\upkappa\colon\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}\to\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\Upphi}
. Now,
\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}
preserves the Cartan decomposition of
\mathfrak{{g}}
and hence of
\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\Upphi}
, so
\upkappa
takes values in
\mathfrak{{k}}^{\Upphi}
. What is more,
\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}
commutes with
\mathfrak{{a}}
and thus with
\mathfrak{{a}}^{\Upphi}
. Consequently, the image of
\upkappa
lies in
(\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\Upphi})_{0}
. We conclude that
\upkappa(\mathfrak{{k}}_{0})\subseteq\mathfrak{{k}}^{\Upphi}\cap(\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\Upphi})_{0}=\mathfrak{{k}}^{\Upphi}_{0}
. On the other hand,
\mathfrak{{k}}^{\Upphi}_{0}
is a subalgebra of
\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}
, and
\upkappa
is the identity map on
\mathfrak{{k}}^{\Upphi}_{0}
by construction. Consequently,
\upkappa\colon\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}\twoheadrightarrow\mathfrak{{k}}^{\Upphi}_{0}
is surjective. Recall from Subsection
2.2
that we have a decomposition
\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}=Z_{\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}}(\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\Upphi})\oplus\mathfrak{{k}}^{\Upphi}_{0}
. One can check that
\upkappa\colon\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}\to\mathfrak{{k}}^{\Upphi}_{0}
is actually the projection along the ideal
Z_{\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}}(\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\Upphi})
—although we do not need that, as the proof is already complete.
∎

We are now ready to prove Proposition
6.10
. The assumption in the proposition ensures that the normal space
\mathfrak{{v}}
of the singular orbit of our C1-action is tangent to the boundary component
B_{i}
. Note that any C1-action canonically extended from
B_{i}
also shares this property. So it is indeed reasonable to expect that our C1-action arises via canonical extension from
B_{i}
.

Proof of Proposition
6.10
.

Our goal is to provide a C1-action on
B_{i}
whose canonical extension has the same orbits in
M
as
H_{j,\mathfrak{{w}}}
. We are going to construct the desired action via the nilpotent construction
9
9

9

This is a special case of the following general fact: the canonical extension of an action arising via the nilpotent construction itself comes from the nilpotent construction performed on the ambient space. See
[
DRDVO23
, Lemma 4.3]
.
. To that end, we need to provide nilpotent construction data for
B_{i}
. We choose the base point
o\in B_{i}
, the maximal abelian subspace
\mathfrak{{a}}^{i}\subset\mathfrak{{b}}_{i}
, the set of positive roots
\Upsigma_{i}^{+}\subset\Upsigma_{i}
, and the simple root
\upalpha_{j}\in\Uplambda_{i}
. In order to avoid notational confusion, we are going to denote the corresponding maximal parabolic subalgebra of
\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{i}
and its various related subalgebras by
\mathfrak{{q}}(i)_{j},\mathfrak{{m}}(i)_{j},\mathfrak{{a}}(i)^{j},\mathfrak{{n}}(i)^{j},\mathfrak{{n}}(i)_{j}
, and so on. These can also be expressed in terms of the subalgebras
\mathfrak{{q}}_{j},\mathfrak{{m}}_{j}
, etc.:
\mathfrak{{q}}(i)_{j}=\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{i}\cap\mathfrak{{q}}_{j},\mathfrak{{m}}(i)_{j}=\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{i}\cap\mathfrak{{m}}_{j}
, and so on. We will be particularly interested in
\mathfrak{{m}}(i)_{j}
and how it compares to
\mathfrak{{m}}_{j}
. We have:

(11)

\mathfrak{{m}}_{j}=\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{{a}}^{j}\oplus\bigoplus_{\upalpha\in\Upsigma_{j}}\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha},\quad\mathfrak{{m}}(i)_{j}=\mathfrak{{k}}^{i}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{{a}}(i)^{j}\oplus\bigoplus_{\upalpha\in\Upsigma_{\Upphi}}\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha},

where
\Upphi=\Uplambda\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to8.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.84 -2.08 L 0 10.03}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\hskip-1.0pt}{\hbox{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to8.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.84 -2.08 L 0 10.03}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\hskip-1.0pt}{\hbox{\hbox to2.95pt{\vbox to5.45pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.72499pt\lower-0.22499pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}

. The merit of this decomposition is that

“preserves” each of its two parts: by this we mean that adding a root from

to one in

results in either another root in
\Updelta(i)_{j}^{1}
or not a root at all, and the same goes for
\Updelta_{j}^{1}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to8.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
