\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\Updelta(i)_{j}^{1}
. The corresponding decomposition of
\mathfrak{{n}}_{j}^{1}
is

(12)

\mathfrak{{n}}_{j}^{1}=\mathfrak{{n}}(i)_{j}^{1}\oplus(\mathfrak{{n}}_{j}^{1}\ominus\mathfrak{{n}}(i)_{j}^{1}),

and
\mathfrak{{m}}(i)_{j}
preserves each of the two summands. By assumption, for every
k=1,\ldots,m
, the root
\uplambda_{k}
does not have
\upalpha_{i}
in its expression via the simple roots, hence
\uplambda_{k}=\upalpha_{j}+\sum_{\upalpha_{l}\in\Upphi}n_{l}\upalpha_{l}
. This means that it lies in
\Updelta(i)_{j}^{1}
. In view of Corollary
6.9
, we have
\mathfrak{{v}}\subseteq\mathfrak{{n}}(i)_{j}^{1}
, which means that we can write
\mathfrak{{w}}
as

(13)

\mathfrak{{w}}=\mathfrak{{w}}^{\prime}\oplus(\mathfrak{{n}}_{j}^{1}\ominus\mathfrak{{n}}(i)_{j}^{1}),

where
\mathfrak{{w}}^{\prime}\subset\mathfrak{{n}}(i)_{j}^{1}
. We consider the nilpotent construction on
B_{i}
with respect to
\mathfrak{{w}}^{\prime}
. First, we would like to show that the normal space
\mathfrak{{v}}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{{n}}(i)_{j}^{1}\ominus\mathfrak{{w}}^{\prime}
, taken with respect to the inner product
B^{i}_{\uptheta}
coming from the Killing form
B^{i}
of
\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{i}
, coincides with
\mathfrak{{v}}
. To see this, first note that
\mathfrak{{w}}=\bigoplus_{\uplambda\in\Updelta_{j}^{1}}\mathfrak{{w}}_{\uplambda}
implies
\mathfrak{{w}}^{\prime}=\bigoplus_{\uplambda\in\Updelta(i)_{j}^{1}}\mathfrak{{w}}_{\uplambda}
. This latter space has two a priori different orthogonal complements in
\mathfrak{{n}}(i)_{j}^{1}
, namely
\mathfrak{{v}}
and
\mathfrak{{v}}^{\prime}
(with respect to
B_{\uptheta}
and
B^{i}_{\uptheta}
, respectively), and the former decomposes as
\mathfrak{{v}}=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{m}\mathfrak{{v}}_{\uplambda_{k}}
by Corollary
6.9
. But since the root spaces of
\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{i}
are mutually orthogonal with respect to both
B_{\uptheta}
and
B^{i}_{\uptheta}
, we have
\mathfrak{{v}}^{\prime}=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{m}\mathfrak{{v}}^{\prime}_{\uplambda_{k}}
, so it suffices to show that
\mathfrak{{v}}_{\uplambda_{k}}=\mathfrak{{v}}^{\prime}_{\uplambda_{k}}
for each
k=1,\ldots,m
. Consider the compact connected subgroup
(K_{0}^{i})^{0}
of
G^{\prime}_{i}
corresponding to
\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}^{i}
. Its representation on
\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{i}
has each root space of
\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{i}
as an irreducible subrepresentation (see, e.g.,
[
Sol24
, Lem. 4.2.5]
). The restrictions of
B_{\uptheta}
and
B^{i}_{\uptheta}
to each such root space are
(K_{0}^{i})^{0}
-invariant, so they have to be proportional (see
[
Zil10
, Lem. 6.15]
or
[
Sol24
, Cor. 2.1.108]
). Since
\mathfrak{{v}}_{\uplambda_{k}}
and
\mathfrak{{v}}^{\prime}_{\uplambda_{k}}
are the orthogonal complements of
\mathfrak{{w}}_{\uplambda_{k}}=\mathfrak{{w}}^{\prime}_{\uplambda_{k}}
in
\mathfrak{{g}}_{\uplambda_{k}}
with respect to
B_{\uptheta}
and
B^{i}_{\uptheta}
, respectively, they have to coincide. Altogether, we deduce
\mathfrak{{v}}=\mathfrak{{v}}^{\prime}
.

In order to carry out the nilpotent construction on
B_{i}
, we need to show that
\mathfrak{{w}}^{\prime}
is admissible and protohomogeneous. We start by deriving an explicit description of the normalizer
N_{\mathfrak{{m}}(i)_{j}}(\mathfrak{{w}}^{\prime})
. Due to (
13
) and the fact that
\mathfrak{{m}}(i)_{j}
preserves each summand in (
12
), an element of
\mathfrak{{m}}(i)_{j}
preserves
\mathfrak{{w}}^{\prime}
if and only if it preserves
\mathfrak{{w}}
. Consequently,
N_{\mathfrak{{m}}(i)_{j}}(\mathfrak{{w}}^{\prime})=N_{\mathfrak{{m}}(i)_{j}}(\mathfrak{{w}})=N_{\mathfrak{{m}}_{j}}(\mathfrak{{w}})\cap\mathfrak{{m}}(i)_{j}
. By combining this with Corollary
6.3
(
) and (
11
), we get:

(14)

N_{\mathfrak{{m}}(i)_{j}}(\mathfrak{{w}}^{\prime})=N_{\mathfrak{{k}}^{i}_{0}}(\mathfrak{{w}}^{\prime})\oplus\mathfrak{{a}}(i)^{j}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}(i)^{j}\oplus\bigoplus_{\upalpha\in\Upsigma_{\Upphi}^{-}}\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\upalpha}.

In particular, we see that
\mathfrak{{a}}(i)^{j}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}(i)^{j}\subseteq N_{\mathfrak{{m}}(i)_{j}}(\mathfrak{{w}}^{\prime})
, which implies that
\mathfrak{{w}}^{\prime}
is admissible. We are left to prove that it is also protohomogeneous. We would like to use (
14
) to describe
N_{\mathfrak{{k}}(i)_{j}}(\mathfrak{{v}})
. Thanks to Lemma
3.1
(
), we have
N_{\mathfrak{{k}}(i)_{j}}(\mathfrak{{v}})=N_{\mathfrak{{k}}(i)_{j}}(\mathfrak{{w}}^{\prime})
, and the latter can be written as
N_{\mathfrak{{k}}(i)_{j}}(\mathfrak{{w}}^{\prime})=N_{\mathfrak{{m}}(i)_{j}}(\mathfrak{{w}}^{\prime})\cap\mathfrak{{k}}(i)_{j}
. By plugging (
14
) into this and making use of Lemma
6.2
(
), we arrive at:

(15)

N_{\mathfrak{{k}}(i)_{j}}(\mathfrak{{v}})=N_{\mathfrak{{k}}^{i}_{0}}(\mathfrak{{v}})\oplus\bigoplus_{\upalpha\in\Upsigma_{\Upphi}^{+}}\mathfrak{{k}}^{\prime}_{\upalpha}.

Take any nonzero vector
v\in\mathfrak{{v}}_{\uplambda_{1}}
. Thanks to Corollary
6.5
, we know that
[N_{\mathfrak{{k}}_{j}}(\mathfrak{{v}}),v]=\mathfrak{{v}}\ominus\mathbb{R}v=(\mathfrak{{v}}_{\uplambda_{1}}\ominus\mathbb{R}v)\oplus\bigoplus_{k=2}^{m}\mathfrak{{v}}_{\uplambda_{k}}
. Let us look at this through the lens of the decomposition of
N_{\mathfrak{{k}}_{j}}(\mathfrak{{v}})
in Corollary
6.3
(
). First of all, we must have
[N_{\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}}(\mathfrak{{v}}),v]=\mathfrak{{v}}_{\uplambda_{1}}\ominus\mathbb{R}v
. By virtue of Lemma
6.11
, the representation of
\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}
on
\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{i}
has the same image in
\mathfrak{{gl}}(\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{i})
as
\mathfrak{{k}}^{i}_{0}
. This implies that
[N_{\mathfrak{{k}}^{i}_{0}}(\mathfrak{{v}}),v]=\mathfrak{{v}}_{\uplambda_{1}}\ominus\mathbb{R}v
. Next, we also must have
[\mathfrak{{k}}^{\prime}_{\uplambda_{k}-\uplambda_{1}},v]=\mathfrak{{v}}_{\uplambda_{k}}
for every
k=2,\ldots,m
. Note that the root
\uplambda_{k}-\uplambda_{1}
lies in
\Upsigma_{\Upphi}^{+}
. Finally, if
\upalpha\in\Upsigma_{j}^{+}
differs from
\uplambda_{k}-\uplambda_{1}
for every
k=2,\ldots,m
, then
[\mathfrak{{k}}^{\prime}_{\upalpha},v]=\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{0}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
. Let us now consider
[N_{\mathfrak{{k}}(i)_{j}}(\mathfrak{{v}}),v]
. Putting all of the above together with (
15
), we obtain:

\displaystyle[N_{\mathfrak{{k}}(i)_{j}}(\mathfrak{{v}}),v]

\displaystyle=\bigl[N_{\mathfrak{{k}}^{i}_{0}}(\mathfrak{{v}})\oplus\bigoplus_{\upalpha\in\Upsigma_{\Upphi}^{+}}\mathfrak{{k}}^{\prime}_{\upalpha},v\bigr]=\bigl[N_{\mathfrak{{k}}^{i}_{0}}(\mathfrak{{v}}),v\bigr]\oplus\bigoplus_{k=2}^{m}\bigl[\mathfrak{{k}}^{\prime}_{\uplambda_{k}-\uplambda_{1}},v\bigr]

\displaystyle=(\mathfrak{{v}}_{\uplambda_{1}}\ominus\mathbb{R}v)\oplus\bigoplus_{k=2}^{m}\mathfrak{{v}}_{\uplambda_{k}}=\mathfrak{{v}}\ominus\mathbb{R}v.

This shows that the representation of
N_{\mathfrak{{k}}(i)_{j}}(\mathfrak{{v}})
on
\mathfrak{{v}}
satisfies condition (
) of Lemma
6.4
and thus
\mathfrak{{w}}^{\prime}
is protohomogeneous. Altogether, we have shown that
(o,\mathfrak{{a}}^{i},\Upsigma_{i}^{+},\upalpha_{j},\mathfrak{{w}}^{\prime})
is nilpotent construction data for
B_{i}
, hence the subalgebra
\mathfrak{{h}}(i)_{j,\mathfrak{{w}}^{\prime}}
of
\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{i}
induces a C1-action on
B_{i}
. Its canonical extension is given by

(\mathfrak{{h}}(i)_{j,\mathfrak{{w}}^{\prime}})^{\Uplambda}=\mathfrak{{h}}(i)_{j,\mathfrak{{w}}^{\prime}}\oplus\mathfrak{{a}}_{i}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{{n}}_{i}=(N_{\mathfrak{{m}}(i)_{j}}(\mathfrak{{w}}^{\prime})\oplus\mathfrak{{a}}(i)_{j}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{{n}}(i)_{j,\mathfrak{{w}}^{\prime}})\oplus\mathfrak{{a}}_{i}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{{n}}_{i}.

In view of (
14
), this can be simplified to

(\mathfrak{{h}}(i)_{j,\mathfrak{{w}}^{\prime}})^{\Uplambda}=N_{\mathfrak{{k}}^{i}_{0}}(\mathfrak{{w}}^{\prime})\oplus\mathfrak{{a}}\oplus(\mathfrak{{n}}\ominus\mathfrak{{v}})\oplus\bigoplus_{\upalpha\in\Upsigma_{\Upphi}^{-}}\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\upalpha}.

If we compare this to
\mathfrak{{h}}_{j,\mathfrak{{w}}}
and take into account Corollary
6.3
(
) and the fact that
N_{\mathfrak{{k}}^{i}_{0}}(\mathfrak{{w}}^{\prime})=N_{\mathfrak{{k}}^{i}_{0}}(\mathfrak{{w}})
, we see that
(\mathfrak{{h}}(i)_{j,\mathfrak{{w}}^{\prime}})^{\Uplambda}\subseteq\mathfrak{{h}}_{j,\mathfrak{{w}}}
. As either subalgebra induces a C1-action on
M
, these actions must have the same orbits. This completes the proof.
∎

From now on,
we assume that the C1-action coming from
\mathfrak{{w}}
does not arise via nontrivial canonical extension
. In our further arguments, Proposition
6.10
will manifest itself in the form of the following

Corollary 6.12
.

For each
\upalpha_{i}\in\Uplambda_{j}
, the subspace
\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\upalpha_{i}}
is nonzero.

Proof.

Fix a simple root
\upalpha_{i}\in\Uplambda_{j}
. As
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{H^{i}}\hskip 0.5pt,{\upalpha_{j}}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt=0
, Proposition
6.10
guarantees that there exists some
2\leq l\leq m
such that
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{H^{i}}\hskip 0.5pt,{\uplambda_{l}}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt\neq 0
. By reducing
l
if necessary, we may additionally assume that
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{H^{i}}\hskip 0.5pt,{\uplambda_{l-1}}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt=0
(as
\uplambda_{1}=\upalpha_{j}
). Recall that
\mathfrak{{v}}_{\uplambda_{k}}
is nonzero for every
k=1,\ldots,m
by Corollary
6.9
. By virtue of Proposition
6.6
(
), the difference
\uplambda_{l}-\uplambda_{l-1}
must lie in
\Upsigma_{j}^{+}
. Since this is a height-1 root, it has to be
\upalpha_{i}
. Invoking Proposition
6.6
(
) one more time, we get
\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\upalpha_{i}}\neq\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{0}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
.
∎

We are now in a position to prove the following crucial

Proposition 6.13
.

For every root
\uplambda\in\Updelta_{j}^{1}
, we have
\mathfrak{{v}}_{\uplambda}\neq\{0\}
.

Proof.

We will proceed by induction on the height of
\uplambda
. If
\hgt(\uplambda)=1
, then
\uplambda=\upalpha_{j}=\uplambda_{1}
and
\mathfrak{{v}}_{\uplambda}\neq\{0\}
by Corollary
6.9
. Assume that
\mathfrak{{v}}_{\upgamma}\neq\{0\}
for every
\upgamma\in\Updelta_{j}^{1}
of height
\leq k
and take
\uplambda\in\Updelta_{j}^{1}
of height
k+1
. From Corollary
6.7
we get the existence of a root
\upalpha_{i}\in\Uplambda_{j}
such that
\uplambda-\upalpha_{i}\in\Updelta_{j}^{1}
. Put
\upvarepsilon
to be the largest positive integer such that
\uplambda-\upvarepsilon\upalpha_{i}
is a root. By the induction hypothesis, we have
\mathfrak{{v}}_{\uplambda-\upvarepsilon\upalpha_{i}}\neq\{0\}
. Now, take a nonzero vector
X\in\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\upalpha_{i}}
—this is possible thanks to Corollary
6.12
. Lemma
2.4
ensures that
\ad(X)^{\upvarepsilon}(\mathfrak{{v}}_{\uplambda-\upvarepsilon\upalpha_{i}})
is a nonzero subspace of
\mathfrak{{g}}_{\uplambda}
. As
\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\upalpha_{i}}
normalizes
\mathfrak{{v}}
, this subspace actually lies in
\mathfrak{{g}}_{\uplambda}\cap\mathfrak{{v}}=\mathfrak{{v}}_{\uplambda}
. This concludes the induction and the proof.
∎

When combined with Corollary
6.9
and Proposition
6.6
(
), this proposition yields very strong restrictions on the root system
\Upsigma
and the choice of
\upalpha_{j}
:

Corollary 6.14
.

We have
\Updelta_{j}^{1}=\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{\uplambda_{1},\ldots,\uplambda_{m}}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
with
\hgt(\uplambda_{k})=k
and
\uplambda_{1}=\upalpha_{j}
. Moreover,
\mathfrak{{v}}=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{m}\mathfrak{{v}}_{\uplambda_{k}}
, and
\mathfrak{{v}}_{\uplambda_{k}}
is nonzero for each
k=1,\ldots,m
. Finally, for every
1\leq k,l\leq m
with
k\neq l
, we have
\uplambda_{k}-\uplambda_{l}\in\Upsigma_{j}
.

This immediately leads to the following powerful

Corollary 6.15
.

The root
\upalpha_{j}
must be connected to precisely one simple root in the Dynkin diagram of
\Upsigma
.

Proof.

Since
M
is irreducible and of rank at least two, the Dynkin diagram
\DD
of
\Upsigma
is connected and contains at least two vertices. Therefore,
\upalpha_{j}
must be connected to at least one other simple root in
\DD
—say, to
\upalpha_{i}\in\Uplambda_{j}
. Then, the Cartan integer
n_{\upalpha_{j}\upalpha_{i}}
is negative and thus
\upalpha_{j}+\upalpha_{i}
is a root, as follows from
[
Kna02
, Prop. 2.48(e)]
. This makes
\upalpha_{j}+\upalpha_{i}
a root of height 2 in
\Updelta_{j}^{1}
—and there can be at most one such root according to Corollary
6.14
. Consequently, there can be only one such
\upalpha_{i}
in
\Uplambda_{j}
.
∎

This corollary whittles down the list of potential candidates for
\upalpha_{j}
to only a few options. But for many spaces, we can actually carry this further and rule out all choices of
\upalpha_{j}
altogether. Namely, we will now use Corollary
6.14
to rule out the spaces whose root systems are of type
\mathrm{{E}}_{6}
,
\mathrm{{E}}_{7}
,
\mathrm{{E}}_{8}
,
\mathrm{{F}}_{4}
, or
\mathrm{{D}}_{r}
, for any
r\geq 4
. We start with the types
\mathrm{{D}}
and
\mathrm{{E}}
. According to Corollary
6.15
, we may assume that
\upalpha_{j}
is connected to only one simple root in the Dynkin diagram
\DD
of
\Upsigma
. But for the types
\mathrm{{D}}
and
\mathrm{{E}}
, this means that
\DD
has a connected subgraph of the form

\upbeta_{1}
\upbeta_{3}
\upbeta_{2}
\upalpha_{j}

(here
\upalpha_{j}
may be connected directly to
\upbeta_{1}
). Since the sum of the simple roots in any connected subgraph of a Dynkin diagram is still a root (
[
SL24
, Lem. 2.5]
), we see that

(16)

\upalpha_{j}+\dots+\upbeta_{1}+\upbeta_{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\upalpha_{j}+\dots+\upbeta_{1}+\upbeta_{3}

are both roots in
\Updelta_{j}^{1}
. What is more, they have the same height, which contradicts Corollary
6.14
. Let us now focus on the spaces with root systems of type
\mathrm{{F}}_{4}
. Consider the corresponding Dynkin diagram:
\upalpha_{1}
\upalpha_{2}
\upalpha_{3}
\upalpha_{4}

First, put
j=1
. We know from
[
SL24
, Prop. 5.6]
that

\upalpha_{1}+\upalpha_{2}+2\upalpha_{3}\quad\text{and}\quad\upalpha_{1}+\upalpha_{2}+\upalpha_{3}+\upalpha_{4}

are both roots, and they are then clearly elements of
\Updelta_{1}^{1}
of the same height, which again contradicts Corollary
6.14
. For the sake of completeness, we include the diagram of
\Updelta_{1}^{1}
in Figure
1
with these two roots corresponding to the blue nodes.

\upalpha_{2}
\upalpha_{3}
\upalpha_{3}
\upalpha_{2}
\upalpha_{2}
\upalpha_{3}
\upalpha_{3}
\upalpha_{2}
\upalpha_{3}
\upalpha_{2}
\upalpha_{3}
\upalpha_{4}
\upalpha_{4}
\upalpha_{4}
\upalpha_{4}
\upalpha_{4}
\upalpha_{4}
\upalpha_{1}

Figure 1.
\Updelta_{j}^{1}
for
\Upsigma\cong\mathrm{{F}}_{4}
and
j=1
.

Now suppose that
j=4
. According to
[
SL24
, Prop. 5.5]
, both combinations

\upalpha_{2}+2\upalpha_{3}+\upalpha_{4}\quad\text{and}\quad\upalpha_{1}+\upalpha_{2}+\upalpha_{3}+\upalpha_{4}

are roots, hence they are elements of
\Updelta_{4}^{1}
of the same height, and we once again arrive at a contradiction. Again, for the sake of completeness, we include the diagram of
\Updelta_{4}^{1}
in Figure
2
, where these roots correspond to the blue nodes.

\upalpha_{3}
\upalpha_{2}
\upalpha_{3}
\upalpha_{3}
\upalpha_{2}
\upalpha_{3}
\upalpha_{1}
\upalpha_{1}
\upalpha_{4}

Figure 2.
\Updelta_{j}^{1}
for
\Upsigma\cong\mathrm{{F}}_{4}
and
j=4
.

The above discussion reveals that, for the spaces of types
\mathrm{{D}},\mathrm{{E}},
and
\mathrm{{F}}
, the assumption that the action of
H_{j,\mathfrak{{w}}}
does not arise via nontrivial canonical extension is too restrictive and it rules out all choices of
\mathfrak{{w}}
. We conclude:

Proposition 6.16
.

Any nilpotent construction action on a symmetric space
M
of noncompact type with root system of type
\mathrm{{D}}_{r}\,(r\geq 4),\mathrm{{E}}_{6},\mathrm{{E}}_{7},\mathrm{{E}}_{8},
or
\mathrm{{F}}_{4}
can be obtained via canonical extension from a proper boundary component of
M
.

This leaves us with the spaces whose root systems are of type
\mathrm{{A}}_{r}
,
\mathrm{{B}}_{r}
,
\mathrm{{C}}_{r+1}
, or
\mathrm{{BC}}_{r}
, with
r\geq 2
. For such spaces, arguing on the level of roots is not going to be sufficient, and we have to ascend to the level of root spaces. We are going to investigate the shape operators of the singular orbit
S=H_{j,\mathfrak{{w}}}\cdot o
. By virtue of Corollary
6.3
(
), the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{{h}}_{j,\mathfrak{{w}}}=N_{\mathfrak{{m}}_{j}}(\mathfrak{{w}})\oplus\mathfrak{{a}}_{j}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}_{j,\mathfrak{{w}}}
of our action has a subalgebra
\mathfrak{{h}}
of the form

(17)

\mathfrak{{h}}=\mathfrak{{a}}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}^{j}\oplus\mathfrak{{w}}\oplus\Bigl(\bigoplus_{\upnu\geq 2}\mathfrak{{n}}_{j}^{\upnu}\Bigr)=\mathfrak{{a}}\oplus\Bigl(\bigoplus_{\upalpha\in\Upsigma_{j}^{+}}\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}\Bigr)\oplus\Bigl(\bigoplus_{k=1}^{m}\mathfrak{{w}}_{\uplambda_{k}}\Bigr)\oplus\Bigl(\bigoplus_{\upgamma\in\bigcup_{\upnu\geq 2}\Updelta_{j}^{\upnu}}\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upgamma}\Bigr).

This subalgebra has the same projection in
\mathfrak{{p}}
as
\mathfrak{{h}}_{j,\mathfrak{{w}}}
, which means that its corresponding connected Lie subgroup
H
also has
S
as its orbit through
o
. But
H
is a subgroup of
AN
, which gives us access to the tools introduced in Subsection
2.3
to study the geometry of
S
. In what follows, we will embrace the notation established in Subsection
2.3
. We also extend the operator
\top
to the whole
\mathfrak{{g}}
by defining it to be the orthogonal projection onto
\mathfrak{{h}}
with respect to
B_{\uptheta}
. Thanks to (
3
) and (
17
), this definition agrees with the old one (which was the orthogonal projection onto
\mathfrak{{h}}
with respect to
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{-}\hskip 1.0pt|\hskip 1.5pt{-}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt_{AN}
) on
\mathfrak{{a}}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}
. Our first goal is to show that the general formula (
6
) for the shape operators becomes much simpler in the context of the nilpotent construction.

Proposition 6.17
.

Take
\uplambda\in\Updelta_{j}^{1}
and
\upalpha\in\Upsigma^{+}
and pick any vectors
\upxi\in\mathfrak{{v}}_{\uplambda}
and
X\in\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}\cap\mathfrak{{h}}
. Then the shape operator
\mathcal{{A}}_{\upxi}
satisfies the following:

(1)

\mathcal{A}_{\upxi}H=0
for any
H\in\mathfrak{{a}}
.

(2)

\mathcal{A}_{\upxi}X=\dfrac{1}{2}([\upxi,X]-[\uptheta\upxi,X])^{\top}
.

(3)

\mathcal{A}_{\upxi}X=\dfrac{1}{2}[\upxi,X]^{\top}
if
\upalpha\in\Upsigma_{j}^{+}
.

(4)

\mathcal{A}_{\upxi}X=0
if
\upalpha\in\Upsigma_{j}^{+}
and
\uplambda=\uplambda_{m}
.

Proof.

We begin with (
). From (
6
) and the fact that
\uptheta(\mathfrak{{g}}_{\uplambda})=\mathfrak{{g}}_{-\uplambda}
, we have, for any
Y\in\mathfrak{{h}}
:

(18)

\langle\hskip 1.0pt{\mathcal{{A}}_{\upxi}H}\hskip 1.0pt|\hskip 1.5pt{Y}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt_{AN}=\frac{1}{4}\langle\hskip 1.0pt{[\upxi,H]-[\uptheta\upxi,H]}\hskip 1.0pt|\hskip 1.5pt{Y}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt_{B_{\uptheta}}=-\frac{1}{4}\uplambda(H)\langle\hskip 1.0pt{\upxi}\hskip 1.0pt|\hskip 1.5pt{Y}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt_{B_{\uptheta}}-\frac{1}{4}\uplambda(H)\langle\hskip 1.0pt{\uptheta\upxi}\hskip 1.0pt|\hskip 1.5pt{Y}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt_{B_{\uptheta}}.

The second term on the right vanishes because
\uptheta\upxi
lies in
\mathfrak{{g}}_{-\uplambda}
, which is orthogonal to
\mathfrak{{a}}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}
and thus to
Y
. Since
\upxi\in\mathfrak{{n}}
, we can use the comment after (
3
) to rewrite the first term on the right-hand side of (
18
) as
-\frac{1}{2}\uplambda(H)\langle\hskip 1.0pt{\upxi}\hskip 1.0pt|\hskip 1.5pt{Y}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt_{AN}
, which vanishes because
\mathfrak{{v}}
and
\mathfrak{{h}}
are orthogonal with respect to
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{-}\hskip 1.0pt|\hskip 1.5pt{-}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt_{AN}
. All in all, this shows that
\mathcal{{A}}_{\upxi}H
must be zero. To show (
), we first claim that either
[\uptheta\upxi,X]
lies in
\mathfrak{{n}}
, or else it is orthogonal to
Y
with respect to
B_{\uptheta}
. Since
[\uptheta\upxi,X]\in\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha-\uplambda}
, we need only show that
[\uptheta\upxi,X]\perp\mathfrak{{a}}
whenever
\upalpha=\uplambda
. This follows directly from Lemma
2.3
. Since
[\upxi,X]\in\mathfrak{{n}}
, we can use the same discussion after (
3
) to rewrite (
6
) in our case as:

\langle\hskip 1.0pt{\mathcal{{A}}_{\upxi}X}\hskip 1.0pt|\hskip 1.5pt{Y}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt_{AN}=\frac{1}{2}\langle\hskip 1.0pt{[\upxi,X]-[\uptheta\upxi,X]}\hskip 1.0pt|\hskip 1.5pt{Y}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt_{AN}

As
Y
is arbitrary, this implies (
). For (
), just note that
\upalpha-\uplambda=-\upalpha_{j}+\sum_{i\neq j}n_{i}\upalpha_{i}
with
n_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
—this means that
\upalpha-\uplambda
is either a negative root or not a root at all. In either case,
[\uptheta\upxi,X]
has trivial projection in
\mathfrak{{h}}
. The rest follows from (
). Finally, we prove (
). If
\uplambda_{m}+\upalpha
were a root, it would be an element of
\Updelta_{j}^{1}
of height greater than
m
, which would contradict Corollary
6.14
. The assertion then follows from (
).
∎

Our next step is to utilize the protohomogeneity condition to show that part (
) of the above proposition must in fact hold for any normal vector.

Proposition 6.18
.

For any
\upxi\in\mathfrak{{v}}
and
X\in\mathfrak{{n}}^{j}
, we have
\mathcal{A}_{\xi}X=0
.

Proof.

This proposition is one of the few places where it is actually more convenient to bring our perspective from
\mathfrak{{a}}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}
back to
\mathfrak{{p}}\cong T_{o}M
. Recall that the identification
\upvarphi\colon AN\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 6.3941pt\hskip-6.3941pt\hbox{$\xrightarrow{\phantom{\sim}}$}\hskip-6.3941pt\hskip-4.76384pt\raisebox{0.64444pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\sim\,$}}\hskip-4.76384pt\hskip 6.3941pt}}M
is by definition an isometry with respect to the metric
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{-}\hskip 1.0pt|\hskip 1.5pt{-}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt_{AN}
, and its differential at the identity can be identified with the projection of
\mathfrak{{a}}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}
onto
\mathfrak{{p}}
along
\mathfrak{{k}}
, i.e.,
d\upvarphi_{e}={\left.\kern-1.2pt(\Id_{\mathfrak{{g}}}\hskip-1.0pt-\hskip 1.5pt\uptheta)/2\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathfrak{{a}}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}}}
. Given a vector
X\in\mathfrak{{g}}
(resp., a subspace
V\subseteq\mathfrak{{g}}
), we are going to write
X_{\mathfrak{{p}}}
(resp.,
V_{\mathfrak{{p}}}
) for its projection in
\mathfrak{{p}}
along
\mathfrak{{k}}
. Since
\upvarphi(H)=S
, we have
\mathfrak{{h}}_{\mathfrak{{p}}}=T_{o}S
and
\mathfrak{{v}}_{\mathfrak{{p}}}=N_{o}S
. Note that
\mathfrak{{v}}_{\mathfrak{{p}}}
splits as
\bigoplus_{k=1}^{m}\mathfrak{{p}}_{\uplambda_{k}}
. We claim that the sum
\mathfrak{{a}}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}^{j}_{\mathfrak{{p}}}
is invariant under the representation of
K_{j}
on
\mathfrak{{p}}
. Indeed, note that
\mathfrak{{a}}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}^{j}_{\mathfrak{{p}}}=(\mathfrak{{a}}^{j}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}^{j}_{\mathfrak{{p}}})\oplus\mathfrak{{a}}_{j}=\mathfrak{{b}}_{j}\oplus\mathfrak{{a}}_{j}=\mathfrak{{f}}_{j}
. The group
K_{j}
is the isotropy of the point
o
under the action of
L_{j}
, hence
K_{j}
preserves
T_{o}(L_{j}\cdot o)=T_{o}F_{j}\cong\mathfrak{{f}}_{j}
. Given a normal vector
v\in\mathfrak{{v}}_{\mathfrak{{p}}}
, let us denote the corresponding shape operator of
S
at
o
by
\mathcal{{A}}^{\mathfrak{{p}}}_{v}
. Since
\upvarphi
is an isometry that takes
H
to
S
, it identifies the shape operators of
H
at
e
and of
S
at
o
: for any
\upxi\in\mathfrak{{v}}
and
X\in\mathfrak{{h}}
, we have
(\mathcal{{A}}_{\upxi}X)_{\mathfrak{{p}}}=\mathcal{{A}}^{\mathfrak{{p}}}_{\upxi_{\mathfrak{{p}}}}X_{\mathfrak{{p}}}
. Finally, note that the representation of
N_{K_{j}}(\mathfrak{{v}})
on
\mathfrak{{v}}_{\mathfrak{{p}}}
is the slice representation of
H_{j,\mathfrak{{w}}}
at
o
, hence it is transitive on the unit sphere in
\mathfrak{{v}}_{\mathfrak{{p}}}
. This representation is isomorphic to
N_{K_{j}}(\mathfrak{{v}})\curvearrowright\mathfrak{{v}}
by means of the projection
\mathfrak{{v}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 6.3941pt\hskip-6.3941pt\hbox{$\xrightarrow{\phantom{\sim}}$}\hskip-6.3941pt\hskip-4.76384pt\raisebox{0.64444pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\sim\,$}}\hskip-4.76384pt\hskip 6.3941pt}}\mathfrak{{v}}_{\mathfrak{{p}}}
. Indeed, the projection
(\Id_{\mathfrak{{g}}}\hskip-1.0pt-\hskip 1.5pt\uptheta)/2\colon\mathfrak{{g}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathfrak{{p}}
is
K
-equivariant, hence
\mathfrak{{v}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 6.3941pt\hskip-6.3941pt\hbox{$\xrightarrow{\phantom{\sim}}$}\hskip-6.3941pt\hskip-4.76384pt\raisebox{0.64444pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\sim\,$}}\hskip-4.76384pt\hskip 6.3941pt}}\mathfrak{{v}}_{\mathfrak{{p}}}
is
N_{K_{j}}(\mathfrak{{v}})
-equivariant.

Now we just put everything together. Take
\upxi\in\mathfrak{{v}}
and
X\in\mathfrak{{n}}^{j}
. We need to show that
\mathcal{{A}}_{\upxi}X=0
, or equivalently, that
\mathcal{{A}}^{\mathfrak{{p}}}_{\upxi_{\mathfrak{{p}}}}X_{\mathfrak{{p}}}=0
. According to the above discussion, there exists
k\in N_{K_{j}}(\mathfrak{{v}})
such that
\Ad(k)\upxi_{\mathfrak{{p}}}=(\Ad(k)\upxi)_{\mathfrak{{p}}}\in\mathfrak{{p}}_{\uplambda_{m}}
. The vector
\Ad(k)\upxi
then lies in
\mathfrak{{g}}_{\uplambda_{m}}
, hence the shape operator
\mathcal{{A}}_{\Ad(k)\upxi}
vanishes on the whole
\mathfrak{{a}}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}^{j}
—thanks to parts (
) and (
) of Proposition
6.17
. In other words, the operator
\mathcal{{A}}^{\mathfrak{{p}}}_{(\Ad(k)\upxi)_{\mathfrak{{p}}}}=\mathcal{{A}}^{\mathfrak{{p}}}_{\Ad(k)\upxi_{\mathfrak{{p}}}}
vanishes on the whole
\mathfrak{{a}}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}^{j}_{\mathfrak{{p}}}
. In particular, it vanishes on
\Ad(k)X_{\mathfrak{{p}}}
, which belongs to
\mathfrak{{a}}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}^{j}_{\mathfrak{{p}}}
because
X_{\mathfrak{{p}}}\in\mathfrak{{n}}^{j}_{\mathfrak{{p}}}
and
\Ad(k)
preserves
\mathfrak{{a}}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}^{j}_{\mathfrak{{p}}}
. But now simply notice that
0=\mathcal{{A}}^{\mathfrak{{p}}}_{\Ad(k)\upxi_{\mathfrak{{p}}}}(\Ad(k)X_{\mathfrak{{p}}})=\mathcal{{A}}^{\mathfrak{{p}}}_{\upxi_{\mathfrak{{p}}}}X_{\mathfrak{{p}}}
, as
\Ad(k)
is an element of the isotropy of the action at
o
and thus preserves the shape operators of
S
at
o
. This shows that
\mathcal{{A}}_{\upxi}X=0
, which completes the proof.
∎

This result allows us to improve drastically on Corollary
6.3
:

Corollary 6.19
.

For every
\upalpha\in\Upsigma_{j}
, we have
\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\upalpha}=\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}
. Moreover,

N_{\mathfrak{{m}}_{j}}(\mathfrak{{w}})=N_{\mathfrak{{m}}_{j}}(\mathfrak{{v}})=N_{\mathfrak{{k}}_{0}}(\mathfrak{{w}})\oplus\mathfrak{{a}}^{j}\oplus\bigoplus_{\upalpha\in\Upsigma_{j}}\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}.

In particular, this normalizer contains the isometry Lie algebra
\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{j}
of
B_{j}
.

Proof.

Pick some
\upalpha\in\Upsigma_{j}^{+}
and take any
X\in\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}
and
\upxi\in\mathfrak{{v}}
. We know from Propositions
6.17
(
) and
6.18
that
[X,\upxi]
has trivial projection in
\mathfrak{{h}}
, hence it belongs to
\mathfrak{{v}}
. This shows that
X
normalizes
\mathfrak{{v}}
and thus lies in
\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\upalpha}
. If
\upbeta\in\Upsigma_{j}^{-}
, we have
\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{\upbeta}=\uptheta(\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{-\upbeta})=\uptheta(\mathfrak{{g}}_{-\upbeta})=\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upbeta}
. The formula for the normalizers then follows directly from Corollary
6.3
. The last assertion follows from the fact that
\mathfrak{{g}}^{\prime}_{j}
is generated by
\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}
as
\upalpha
runs through
\Upsigma_{j}
.
∎

For our next step, we deal with the extreme case of
\mathfrak{{w}}
being zero. As it will turn out, this is actually the only case leading to C1-actions that cannot be obtained via other methods.

Proposition 6.20
.

Suppose that for some
1\leq k\leq m
, we have
\mathfrak{{w}}_{\uplambda_{k}}=\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{0}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
. Then
\mathfrak{{w}}=\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{0}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
. Moreover, in this situation, the singular orbit of
H_{j,\mathfrak{{w}}}
is totally geodesic, except for the case when
\Upsigma\cong\mathrm{{G}}_{2}
and
\upalpha_{j}
is the short simple root.

Note that we are not saying that
\mathfrak{{w}}=\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{0}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
always leads to a C1-action: it is always trivially admissible but may not be protohomogeneous, as we saw in the case of
\mathrm{{DEF}}
-type spaces. What we are saying is, when it is protohomogeneous, the singular orbit of the corresponding C1-action must be totally geodesic, barring one exception.

Proof of Proposition
6.20
.

We begin with the first assertion. Suppose that
\mathfrak{{w}}_{\uplambda_{k}}=\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{0}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
for some
k
as above. This means that
\mathfrak{{v}}_{\uplambda_{k}}=\mathfrak{{g}}_{\uplambda_{k}}
. Pick any
1\leq l\leq m
other than
k
. We must have
\uplambda_{l}-\uplambda_{k}\in\Upsigma_{j}
by virtue of Corollary
6.14
. But now Corollary
6.19
tells us that
\mathfrak{{g}}_{\uplambda_{l}-\uplambda_{k}}
normalizes
\mathfrak{{v}}
. Altogether, we have

\mathfrak{{g}}_{\uplambda_{l}}=[\mathfrak{{g}}_{\uplambda_{l}-\uplambda_{k}},\mathfrak{{g}}_{\uplambda_{k}}]=[\mathfrak{{g}}_{\uplambda_{l}-\uplambda_{k}},\mathfrak{{v}}_{\uplambda_{k}}]\subseteq\mathfrak{{v}}_{\uplambda_{l}},

hence
\mathfrak{{v}}_{\uplambda_{l}}=\mathfrak{{g}}_{\uplambda_{l}}
. As
l
was arbitrary, we obtain
\mathfrak{{v}}=\mathfrak{{n}}_{j}^{1},\,\mathfrak{{w}}=\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{0}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
. Now we come to the second part. Let
\updelta=\sum_{i=1}^{r}n_{i}\upalpha_{i}\in\Upsigma^{+}
be the highest root. By looking at
[
BCO16
, pp. 336–340]
, we see that
n_{i}\leq 3
whenever
\upalpha_{i}
has only one neighbor in the Dynkin diagram (which
\upalpha_{j}
does by Corollary
6.15
), and the equality is only attained when
\Upsigma\simeq\mathrm{{G}}_{2}
and
\upalpha_{i}
is the short simple root, or when
\Upsigma\simeq\mathrm{{E}}_{8}
and
\upalpha_{i}
is one particular boundary root (among the three). In regard to
\upalpha_{j}
, assume first that
n_{j}\leq 2
. Then
\Updelta_{j}^{\upnu}
is empty whenever
\upnu\geq 3
and thus
\mathfrak{{n}}_{j}=\mathfrak{{n}}_{j}^{1}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}_{j}^{2}
. Since
\mathfrak{{w}}=\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{0}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
, decomposition (
17
) now reads as
\mathfrak{{h}}=\mathfrak{{a}}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}^{j}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}_{j}^{2}
. Take a normal vector
\upxi\in\mathfrak{{v}}_{\uplambda_{k}}=\mathfrak{{g}}_{\uplambda_{k}}
for some
1\leq k\leq m
. We want to show that the shape operator
\mathcal{{A}}_{\upxi}
is identically zero. We already know that it vanishes on
\mathfrak{{a}}\oplus\mathfrak{{n}}^{j}
—thanks to Propositions
6.17
(
) and
6.18
—so we only need to show that it vanishes on
\mathfrak{{n}}_{j}^{2}
as well. Pick any
\upgamma\in\Updelta_{j}^{2}
and take some
X\in\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upgamma}
. By definition,
\uplambda_{k}=\upalpha_{j}+\sum_{i\neq j}n_{i}\upalpha_{i}
and
\upgamma=2\upalpha_{j}+\sum_{i\neq j}m_{i}\upalpha_{i}
. Therefore,
\upgamma+\uplambda_{k}
is not a root, for otherwise
\Updelta_{j}^{3}
would be nonempty. What is more,
\upgamma-\uplambda_{k}
either lies in
\Updelta_{j}^{1}
or is not a root at all. We deduce that
[\upxi,X]=0
and
[\uptheta\upxi,X]\in\mathfrak{{n}}_{j}^{1}=\mathfrak{{v}}
, and the latter implies
[\uptheta\upxi,X]^{\top}=0
. By plugging this into Proposition
6.17
(
), we get
\mathcal{A}_{\upxi}X=0
. We conclude that
\mathcal{{A}}_{\upxi}
vanishes on
\mathfrak{{n}}_{j}^{2}
and thus on the whole
\mathfrak{{h}}
. According to our discussion at the end of Subsection
2.3
, singular orbits of C1-actions are CPC, which implies that all of the shape operators of
H_{j,\mathfrak{{w}}}\cdot o
are zero and thus this orbit is totally geodesic.

Finally, let us look at the case when
n_{j}=3
. If
\Upsigma\simeq E_{8}
, then
\mathfrak{{w}}=\{0\}
is not protohomogeneous, as we witnessed in the proof of Proposition
6.16
. So we may assume
\Upsigma\simeq\mathrm{{G}}_{2}
and
\upalpha_{j}=\upalpha_{2}
is the short simple root. There are two spaces with this root system:
\mathrm{{G}}_{2}^{2}/\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{SO}(4)
and
\mathrm{{G}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})/\mathrm{{G}}_{2}
. As was shown in
[
BT13
]
, for either of them, the subspace
\mathfrak{{w}}=\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{0}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
of
\mathfrak{{n}}_{2}^{1}
is admissible and protohomogeneous, and the corresponding C1-action of
H_{2,0}
has a non-totally-geodesic singular orbit—because it does not figure in the classification in
[
BT04
]
. One can also verify that this orbit is not totally geodesic directly. Indeed, we have
\upalpha_{1}+2\upalpha_{2}\in\Updelta_{2}^{2}
and
\upalpha_{1}+3\upalpha_{2}\in\Updelta_{2}^{3}
. For any nonzero
\upxi\in\mathfrak{{v}}_{\upalpha_{2}}=\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha_{2}}
, the restriction of
\mathcal{{A}}_{\upxi}
to
\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha_{1}+3\upalpha_{2}}
is given simply by
-\frac{1}{2}\ad(\uptheta\upxi)\colon\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha_{1}+3\upalpha_{2}}\to\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha_{1}+2\upalpha_{2}}
, as follows from Proposition
6.17
(
). Since
\upalpha_{1}+3\upalpha_{2}
is the first root in its
(-\upalpha_{2})
-string, this map in nonzero by virtue of Lemma
2.4
.
∎

Since the classification of C1-actions with a totally geodesic singular orbit on irreducible symmetric spaces of noncompact type is complete, we can disregard the case
\mathfrak{{w}}=\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{0}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
. Thanks to the above proposition, from now on,
we can and will assume that
\mathfrak{{w}}_{\uplambda_{k}}\neq\hskip-0.8pt\left\{\hskip 0.29999pt{0}\hskip 0.29999pt\right\}
for every
k=1,\ldots,m
. This enables us to obtain the following result:

Lemma 6.21
.

For any
\upalpha_{i}\in\Uplambda_{j}
, there exists a root
\uplambda_{k}\in\Updelta_{j}^{1}
such that
\dim\mathfrak{{g}}_{\uplambda_{k}}\geq 2\dim\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha_{i}}
.

Proof.

Take any
\upalpha=\upalpha_{i}\in\Uplambda_{j}
. Put
l
to be the largest number among
\{1,\dots,m\}
such that
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{H^{i}}\hskip 0.5pt,{\uplambda_{l}}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt=0
, and write
\uplambda=\uplambda_{l}
for simplicity. As
\uplambda_{1}=\upalpha_{j}
and
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{H^{i}}\hskip 0.5pt,{\upalpha_{j}}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt=0
,
l
is well-defined. Moreover, according to Proposition
6.10
,
l
cannot be equal to
m
. We must have
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{H^{i}}\hskip 0.5pt,{\uplambda_{l+1}}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt=1
, which means
\uplambda_{l+1}=\uplambda+\upalpha
. At the same time,
\uplambda-\upalpha
is of the form
\upalpha_{j}-\upalpha_{i}+\sum_{k\neq i,j}n_{k}\upalpha_{k}
, hence it cannot be a root. But then
\upalpha-\uplambda
cannot be a root either. Therefore,
\upalpha
is the root of minimum height in its
\uplambda
-string, and that string also contains
\upalpha+\uplambda
. According to Lemma
2.4
, for any nonzero
X\in\mathfrak{{g}}_{\uplambda}
, the map
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\ad(X)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}}}\colon\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}\to\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha+\uplambda}
is injective. It follows from Corollary
6.14
and our assumption above that each of the two summands in
\mathfrak{{g}}_{\uplambda}=\mathfrak{{w}}_{\uplambda}\oplus\mathfrak{{v}}_{\uplambda}
is nontrivial. Take any nonzero vectors
X\in\mathfrak{{w}}_{\uplambda}
and
\upxi\in\mathfrak{{v}}_{\uplambda}
. In view of Corollary
6.19
,
\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}
normalizes both
\mathfrak{{w}}
and
\mathfrak{{v}}
, which leads to

\ad(X)\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}\subseteq\mathfrak{{w}}_{\upalpha+\uplambda}\quad\text{and}\quad\ad(\upxi)\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}\subseteq\mathfrak{{v}}_{\upalpha+\uplambda}.

We deduce that
\dim\mathfrak{{w}}_{\upalpha+\uplambda}\geq\dim\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}
and
\dim\mathfrak{{v}}_{\upalpha+\uplambda}\geq\dim\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}
, which implies
\dim\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha+\uplambda}\geq 2\dim\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha}
, so we can take
\upalpha+\uplambda=\uplambda_{l+1}
as our desired root.
∎

The power of this result lies in the fact that it will allow us to exclude many candidates for
\upalpha_{j}
based solely on root multiplicities. To begin with:

Proposition 6.22
.

Any nilpotent construction action on a symmetric space
M
of noncompact type with root system of type
\mathrm{{A}}_{r},\,r\geq 2,
either has a totally geodesic singular orbit or else can be obtained via canonical extension from a proper boundary component of
M
.

Before we proceed, let us make a quick overview. In the proof of this proposition (as well as of Propositions
6.24
and
6.25
), we will assume that
\mathfrak{{w}}
is nonzero (justified by Proposition
6.20
) and the action of
H_{j,\mathfrak{{w}}}
does not come from nontrivial canonical extension. These assumptions (either one of them or both) are required by some of the results we have established in this section (notably, by Corollaries
6.14
and
6.15
and Lemma
6.21
). We will then use those results to show that either no such
\mathfrak{{w}}
exists or the singular orbit of
H_{j,\mathfrak{{w}}}
must be totally geodesic.

Proof of Proposition
6.22
.

Consider the Dynkin diagram of
\Upsigma\simeq\mathrm{{A}}_{r}
:
\upalpha_{1}
\upalpha_{2}
\upalpha_{r-1}
\upalpha_{r}

Thanks to Corollary
6.15
, we need only deal with
j=1
or
r
. The two cases are essentially identical due to the symmetry of the Dynkin diagram, so we assume
j=1
. According to Lemma
6.21
, there must exist a root in
\Updelta_{1}^{1}
whose multiplicity is at least twice that of
\upalpha_{2}
. But this is impossible since all roots in
\Upsigma
have the same length and thus multiplicity (see, e.g.,
[
Sol24
, Cor. 6.3.2]
).
∎

Remark 6.23
.

Even though we do not need this, a similar argument can be applied to any space of rank
\geq 2
all of whose restricted roots have the same multiplicity (and any choices of
\upalpha_{j}
and nonzero
\mathfrak{{w}}
therein). This includes all spaces of types
\mathrm{{A}},\mathrm{{D}},\mathrm{{E}},
and
\mathrm{{G}}_{2}
, all spaces whose isometry Lie algebra is a split real form, as well as the noncompact duals of all compact simple Lie groups.

Using similar arguments and a little more effort, we can also dispatch the case of
\mathrm{{C}}_{r}
:

Proposition 6.24
.

Any nilpotent construction action on a symmetric space
M
of noncompact type with root system of type
\mathrm{{C}}_{r},\,r\geq 3,
either has a totally geodesic singular orbit or else can be obtained via canonical extension from a proper boundary component of
M
.

Proof.

Consider the Dynkin diagram of
\Upsigma\simeq\mathrm{{C}}_{r}
:
\upalpha_{1}
\upalpha_{2}
\upalpha_{r-2}
\upalpha_{r-1}
\upalpha_{r}

Once again, according to Corollary
6.15
, we only need to analyze the cases
j=1
and
j=r
, except this time we have to handle them separately as the Dynkin diagram has no nontrivial symmetries. First, put
j=1
. According to
[
SL24
, Prop 4.6]
, we have

\Updelta_{1}^{1}=\{\upalpha_{1}\}\cup\Bigl\{\upalpha_{1}+\sum_{i=2}^{k}\upalpha_{i},\,\upalpha_{1}+\sum_{i=2}^{r}\upalpha_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{l}\upalpha_{r-j}\,\mid\,\begin{subarray}{c}2\leq k\leq r,\\[1.0pt]
1\leq l\leq r-2\end{subarray}\Bigr\}.

Moreover, all roots in
\Updelta_{1}^{1}
have the same length and thus multiplicity. In Figure
3
we include the diagram of
\Updelta_{1}^{1}
with
r=5
for illustrative purposes.

\upalpha_{2}
\upalpha_{3}
\upalpha_{4}
\upalpha_{5}
\upalpha_{4}
\upalpha_{3}
\upalpha_{2}
\upalpha_{1}

Figure 3.
\Updelta_{j}^{1}
for
\Upsigma\cong\mathrm{{C}}_{5}
and
j=1
.

As
r\geq 3
, every
\uplambda\in\Updelta_{1}^{1}
has the same length as
\upalpha_{2}
—and hence the same multiplicity, which contradicts Lemma
6.21
. Now let us consider the case
j=r
. Using
[
SL24
, Prop. 4.2]
, we have

(19)

\Updelta_{r}^{1}=\{\upalpha_{r}\}\cup\Bigl\{\upalpha_{r}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\upalpha_{r-i},\,\upalpha_{r}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\upalpha_{r-i}+\sum_{j=1}^{l}\upalpha_{r-j}\,\mid\,\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq k\leq r-1,\\[1.0pt]
\,1\leq l\leq k\end{subarray}\Bigr\}.

Since
r\geq 3
, we see that

\upalpha_{r}+2\upalpha_{r-1}\quad\text{and}\quad\upalpha_{r}+\upalpha_{r-1}+\upalpha_{r-2}

are both roots in
\Updelta_{r}^{1}
. Moreover, they have the same height, which violates Corollary
6.14
. In Figure
4
, we draw the diagram of
\Updelta_{r}^{1}
for
r=5
with these two roots corresponding to the blue nodes. ∎

\upalpha_{4}
\upalpha_{3}
\upalpha_{2}
\upalpha_{1}

\upalpha_{3}

Figure 4.
\Updelta_{j}^{1}
for
\Upsigma\cong\mathrm{{C}}_{5}
and
j=5
.

We are left to deal with the spaces of types
\mathrm{{B}}_{r}
and
\mathrm{{BC}}_{r}
with
r\geq 2
. As we are about to see, these cases are harder, as arguing merely by looking at the roots and their multiplicities is not going to cut it.

Proposition 6.25
.

Any nilpotent construction action on a symmetric space
M
of noncompact type with root system of type
\mathrm{{B}}_{r}
or
\mathrm{{BC}}_{r},\,r\geq 2
, either has a totally geodesic singular orbit or can be obtained via canonical extension from a proper boundary component of
M
.

Proof.

We will analyze both cases simultaneously. Consider the Dynkin diagram of
\Upsigma
:
\upalpha_{1}
\upalpha_{2}
\upalpha_{r-2}
\upalpha_{r-1}
\upalpha_{r}

Note that if
\Upsigma
is of type
\mathrm{{BC}}_{r}
, then
2\upalpha_{r}
is also a root
10
10

10

For this reason, to distinguish the Dynkin diagrams of
\mathrm{{B}}_{r}
and
\mathrm{{BC}}_{r}
, the latter is sometimes drawn with a double circle for
\upalpha_{r}
:
. We are not using this notation here.
. Thanks to Corollary
6.15
, we need only deal with
j=1
or
r
. We start with the case
j=1
. From
[
SL24
, Prop. 4.5]
, we have

\Updelta_{1}^{1}=\{\upalpha_{1}\}\cup\Bigl\{\upalpha_{1}+\sum_{i=2}^{k}\upalpha_{i},\,\upalpha_{1}+\sum_{i=2}^{r}\upalpha_{i}+\sum_{j=0}^{l}\upalpha_{r-j}\,\mid\,\begin{subarray}{c}2\leq k\leq r,\\[1.0pt]
\,0\leq l\leq r-2\end{subarray}\Bigr\}.

For illustrative purposes, we include the diagram of
\Updelta_{1}^{1}
for
r=5
in Figure
5
.

when

or

and
j=1
.

Take the simple root
\upalpha_{r}\in\Uplambda_{1}
, which is the shortest in
\Uplambda
. As can be seen from
[
BCO16
, pp. 336–340]
,
\upalpha_{r}
has the largest possible multiplicity among all the roots in
\Upsigma
. In particular,
\dim\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha_{r}}\geq\dim\mathfrak{{g}}_{\uplambda}
for any
\uplambda\in\Updelta_{1}^{1}
, which contradicts Lemma
6.21
.

We are left to consider the case
j=r
. In this situation, according to
[
SL24
, Prop. 4.1]
, we have

(20)

\Updelta_{r}^{1}=\Bigl\{\sum_{i=0}^{k}\upalpha_{r-i}\,\mid\,0\leq k\leq r-1\Bigl\},

and all roots in
\Updelta_{r}^{1}
have the same length and multiplicity. In Figure
6
, we draw the diagram of
\Updelta_{r}^{1}
for
r=5
.

\upalpha_{4}

\Updelta_{j}^{1}
when
\Upsigma\cong\mathrm{{B}}_{5}
or
\mathrm{{BC}}_{5}
and
j=5
.

Recall that for every
\uplambda\in\Updelta_{r}^{1}
, both summands in the decomposition
\mathfrak{{g}}_{\uplambda}=\mathfrak{{w}}_{\uplambda}\oplus\mathfrak{{v}}_{\uplambda}
are nontrivial. In particular, we can take a normal vector
\upxi\in\mathfrak{{v}}_{\upalpha_{r}}
with
||\upxi||_{AN}=1
. We will prove that the shape operator
\mathcal{{A}}_{\upxi}
is zero. Since the orbit
S\cdot o
is CPC, this will imply that it is totally geodesic.

Referring to
[
BCO16
, pp. 336–340]
one more time, we see that every
\upalpha=\sum_{i}n_{i}\upalpha_{i}\in\Upsigma^{+}
has
n_{i}\leq 2
for all
i
. In particular,
n_{r}\leq 2
, which means
\Updelta_{r}^{\upnu}=\varnothing
for
\upnu\geq 3
and thus
\Upsigma^{+}=\Upsigma_{r}^{+}\sqcup\Updelta_{r}^{1}\sqcup\Updelta_{r}^{2}
. We want to show that
\mathcal{{A}}_{\upxi}X=0
for every
X\in\mathfrak{{h}}
. In view of (
17
) and Proposition
6.17
(
), we may assume that
X
lies in a single root space
\mathfrak{{g}}_{\uplambda},\,\uplambda\in\Upsigma^{+}
. If the
\upalpha_{r}
-string of
\uplambda
is trivial, then
\mathcal{A}_{\upxi}X=0
thanks to Proposition
6.17
(
). Otherwise, imagine that
\upbeta\in\Upsigma^{+}
is the root of minimum height in its nontrivial
\upalpha_{r}
-string. Then
\upbeta+\upalpha_{r}
is a root and hence either
\upbeta
or
\upbeta+\upalpha_{r}
lies
\Updelta_{r}^{1}
. Therefore, it suffices to consider only the
\upalpha_{r}
-strings of the roots in
\Updelta_{r}^{1}
. We will analyze the
\upalpha_{r}
-string of
\upalpha_{r}
at the end. For now, let us consider
\uplambda\in\Updelta_{r}^{1}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to8.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.84 -2.08 L 0 10.03}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\hskip-1.0pt}{\hbox{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to8.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.84 -2.08 L 0 10.03}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\hskip-1.0pt}{\hbox{\hbox to2.95pt{\vbox to5.45pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.72499pt\lower-0.22499pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.45pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.77 0 L -0.69 6.92}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\hskip 1.0pt}{\hbox{\hbox to1.9pt{\vbox to3.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.08 0 L 0 4.15}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\{\upalpha_{r}\}
. Since
\upalpha_{r}
is the shortest simple root, we have
n_{\upalpha_{r},\upalpha_{r-1}}=-2
and thus
n_{\upalpha_{r},\uplambda}=0
, as follows from (
20
). We also know that
\uplambda-\upalpha_{r}
belongs to
\Upsigma_{r}^{+}
, as guaranteed by Corollary
6.14
. Using this and
[
Kna02
, Prop. 2.48(g)]
, we arrive at the conclusion that the
\upalpha_{r}
-string of
\uplambda
consists of the roots
\uplambda-\upalpha_{r}\in\Upsigma_{r}^{+}
,
\uplambda\in\Updelta_{r}^{1}
and
\uplambda+\upalpha_{r}\in\Updelta_{r}^{2}
.

Thanks to Proposition
6.18
, we already know that
\mathcal{{A}}_{\upxi}
vanishes on
\mathfrak{{g}}_{\uplambda-\upalpha_{r}}
. Take an element
X_{\uplambda+\upalpha_{r}}\in\mathfrak{{g}}_{\uplambda+\upalpha_{r}}
. According to
[
BSL23
, Prop. 4.2(iv)]
, we can write
X_{\uplambda+\upalpha_{r}}=\ad(\upxi)^{2}X_{\uplambda-\upalpha_{r}}
for some
X_{\uplambda-\upalpha_{r}}\in\mathfrak{{g}}_{\uplambda-\upalpha_{r}}
. We calculate:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\upxi}X_{\uplambda+\upalpha_{r}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}_{\upxi}(\ad(\upxi)^{2}X_{\uplambda-\upalpha_{r}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}([\upxi,\ad(\upxi)^{2}X_{\uplambda-\upalpha_{r}}]-[\uptheta\upxi,\ad(\upxi)^{2}X_{\uplambda-\upalpha_{r}}])^{\top}

(Proposition
6.17
(
))

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}[\uptheta\upxi,[\upxi,[\upxi,X_{\uplambda-\upalpha_{r}}]]]^{\top}

(
\uplambda+2\upalpha_{r}\not\in\Upsigma
)

\displaystyle=||\upalpha_{r}||^{2}[\upxi,X_{\uplambda-\upalpha_{r}}]^{\top}

(
[
BSL23
, Lem. 2.4(iii)]
)

\displaystyle=2||\upalpha_{r}||^{2}\mathcal{A}_{\upxi}X_{\uplambda-\upalpha_{r}}=0

\displaystyle\text{(Propositions \ref{proposition:shape:orbit:an}\eqref{proposition:shape:orbit:an:c} and \ref{proposition:all:shapes:nj})}.

Finally, consider
X_{\uplambda}\in\mathfrak{{w}}_{\uplambda}
. Corollary
6.19
ensures that
\mathfrak{{g}}_{\uplambda-\upalpha_{r}}
normalizes
\mathfrak{{v}}
, which implies that
\ad(\upxi)\mathfrak{{g}}_{\uplambda-\upalpha_{r}}
is contained in
\mathfrak{{v}}_{\uplambda}
and is thus orthogonal to
X_{\uplambda}
. Using
[
BSL23
, Prop. 4.2(v)]
, we obtain
\nabla_{X}\upxi=0
and hence
\mathcal{{A}}_{\upxi}X_{\uplambda}=-(\nabla_{X}\upxi)^{\top}=0
. In view of Proposition
6.17
(
), this means that both
\ad(\upxi)
and
\ad(\uptheta\upxi)
vanish on
\mathfrak{{w}}_{\uplambda}
—we will need this observation below. The only root left in
\Upsigma^{+}
with possibly nontrivial
\upalpha_{r}
-string is
\upalpha_{r}
. If
\Upsigma
is of type
\mathrm{{B}}_{r}
, then
2\upalpha_{r}
is not a root. In this case, Proposition
6.17
(
) together with Lemma
2.3
(
) ensure that
\mathcal{{A}}_{\upxi}X_{\upalpha_{r}}=0
for every
X_{\upalpha_{r}}\in\mathfrak{{w}}_{\upalpha_{r}}
. This concludes the case of
\mathrm{{B}}_{r}
-type spaces.

Let us then assume that
\Upsigma
is of type
\mathrm{{BC}}_{r}
. We have to show that the shape operator
\mathcal{{A}}_{\upxi}
is zero on
\mathfrak{{w}}_{\upalpha_{r}}\oplus\mathfrak{{g}}_{2\upalpha_{r}}
. We begin with the first summand. Take some
X_{\upalpha_{r}}\in\mathfrak{{w}}_{\upalpha_{r}}
as well as an auxiliary nonzero vector
Z\in\mathfrak{{g}}_{-\upalpha_{r-1}}
. Notice that the
(-\upalpha_{r-1})
-string of
\uplambda_{2}=\upalpha_{r-1}+\upalpha_{r}
consists of
\uplambda_{2}
and
\upalpha_{r}
. By virtue of Lemma
2.4
, the restriction of
\ad(Z)
to
\mathfrak{{g}}_{\uplambda_{2}}
gives an injective map
\mathfrak{{g}}_{\uplambda_{2}}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{{g}}_{\upalpha_{r}}
. As we mentioned earlier, all roots in
\Updelta_{r}^{1}
have the same multiplicity, so this map is actually an isomorphism. What is more, according to Corollary
6.19
, it carries
\mathfrak{{w}}_{\uplambda_{2}}
into
\mathfrak{{w}}_{\upalpha_{r}}
and
\mathfrak{{v}}_{\uplambda_{2}}
into
\mathfrak{{v}}_{\upalpha_{r}}
. Consequently, this map has to send
\mathfrak{{w}}_{\uplambda_{2}}
isomorphically onto
\mathfrak{{w}}_{\upalpha_{r}}
. This implies that there exists a vector
Y\in\mathfrak{{w}}_{\uplambda_{2}}
such that
[Z,Y]=X_{\upalpha_{r}}
. We can now compute:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\upxi}X_{\upalpha_{r}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}([\upxi,X_{\upalpha_{r}}]-[\uptheta\upxi,X_{\upalpha_{r}}])^{\top}

(Proposition
6.17
(
))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}[\upxi,[Z,Y]]^{\top}

(Lemma
2.3
(
))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}([[\upxi,Z],Y]+[Z,[\upxi,Y]])^{\top}=0

\displaystyle\text{($\upalpha_{r}-\upalpha_{r-1}\not\in\Upsigma\;$ and $\;{\left.\kern-1.2pt\ad(\upxi)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathfrak{{w}}_{\uplambda_{2}}}}=0$)}.

Now we can show that
\mathcal{{A}}_{\upxi}
actually vanishes on the whole
\mathfrak{{w}}_{\upalpha_{r}}\oplus\mathfrak{{g}}_{2\upalpha_{r}}
. Proposition
6.17
(
) together with the above calculation imply that this subspace is invariant under
\mathcal{{A}}_{\upxi}
. As shape operators are diagonalizable, it suffices to show that the restriction of
\mathcal{{A}}_{\upxi}
to this subspace does not have nonzero eigenvalues. Suppose
X_{\upalpha_{r}}+X_{2\upalpha_{r}}\in\mathfrak{{w}}_{\upalpha_{r}}\oplus\mathfrak{{g}}_{2\upalpha_{r}}
is an eigenvector of
\mathcal{{A}}_{\upxi}
with eigenvalue
c
. Note that
\mathcal{A}_{\upxi}X_{2\upalpha_{r}}
belongs to
\mathfrak{{w}}_{\upalpha_{r}}
—by virtue of Proposition
6.17
(
) and the fact that
3\upalpha_{r}
is not a root. Hence, we have

cX_{\upalpha_{r}}+cX_{2\upalpha_{r}}=\mathcal{A}_{\upxi}(X_{\upalpha_{r}}+X_{2\upalpha_{r}})=\mathcal{A}_{\upxi}X_{2\upalpha_{r}}\in\mathfrak{{w}}_{\upalpha_{r}},

which implies either
c=0
or
X_{2\upalpha_{r}}=0
. In the latter case, we have
\mathcal{A}_{\upxi}(X_{\upalpha_{r}}+X_{2\upalpha_{r}})=\mathcal{A}_{\upxi}X_{\upalpha_{r}}=0
and thus still
c=0
. This completes the case of
\mathrm{{BC}}_{r}
. Altogether, we have shown that the shape operator
\mathcal{{A}}_{\upxi}
vanishes whenever
\upxi\in\mathfrak{{v}}_{\upalpha_{r}}
and hence the singular orbit
S
, being CPC, is totally geodesic.
∎

Now we can finally put all pieces of the puzzle together and prove the main result of this article.

Proof of the
Main theorem
.

Our setup is as follows:
M=G/K
is a symmetric space of noncompact type and
H
is a connected Lie group acting on
M
properly and isometrically. The actions in parts (
)-(
) of the
Main theorem
are indeed of cohomogeneity one: for (
)-(
), this follows directly from Theorem
3.5
, whereas for (
) from the fact that canonical extension preserves the cohomogeneity of an action. Conversely, suppose the action of
H
is of cohomogeneity one. We already know that it must be orbit-equivalent to one of the actions in Theorem
3.5
. Parts (
)-(
) thereof correspond to the same parts in the
Main theorem
, so we need only deal with (
). In other words, we need to show that every nilpotent construction action on
M
is accounted for in parts (
)-(
) of the
Main theorem
.

To begin with, the action of
H
may arise via canonical extension: suppose
B_{\Upphi}
is a boundary component of
M
and
H_{\Upphi}\curvearrowright B_{\Upphi}
is a C1-action whose canonical extension is orbit-equivalent to
H\curvearrowright M
. Since canonical extension is a transitive procedure, we can repeat this process inductively until no further canonical extension is possible. For the sake of brevity, we simply assume that the action of
H_{\Upphi}
on
B_{\Upphi}
does not arise via canonical extension from a proper boundary component of
B_{\Upphi}
. Now we apply Theorem
3.5
to
H_{\Upphi}\curvearrowright B_{\Upphi}
. This action has a singular orbit (since so did
H\curvearrowright M
), so it has to correspond to parts (
)-(
) of the theorem. First, suppose it corresponds to (
) or (
). As we assumed that
H_{\Upphi}\curvearrowright B_{\Upphi}
does not come from nontrivial canonical extension, we simply have that either it has a totally geodesic singular orbit and the boundary component
B_{\Upphi}
is irreducible, or else it is orbit-equivalent to a diagonal action and
B_{\Upphi}
is a product of two homothetic rank-one spaces. In the latter case, we can actually require strong orbit-equivalence (even by a trivial isometry), as follows from the proof of Theorem A in
[
DRDVO23
]
. Since strong orbit-equivalence is preserved by canonical extension, we see that the original action
H\curvearrowright M
is then orbit-equivalent to an action described in part (
) or (
) of the
Main theorem
, respectively.

We are left to consider the situation where the action
H_{\Upphi}\curvearrowright B_{\Upphi}
is given by part (
) of Theorem
3.5
, i.e., it is orbit-equivalent to a nilpotent construction action. Using the terminology established in Subsection
4.2
, let
\upchi\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{{N}}}}
be nilpotent construction data on
B_{\Upphi}
such that
H_{\Upphi}\curvearrowright B_{\Upphi}
is orbit-equivalent to the action of
\mathcal{{H}}(\mathcal{{G}}(\upchi))
by means of some isometry
g\in I(M)
, i.e.,
g
carries the orbits of
H_{\Upphi}
onto those of
\mathcal{{H}}(\mathcal{{G}}(\upchi))
. Since the maps
\mathcal{{G}}
and
\mathcal{{H}}
are
I(M)
-equivariant, we see that the action of
H_{\Upphi}
has the same orbits as the action of
g^{-1}\mathcal{{H}}(\mathcal{{G}}(\upchi))g=\mathcal{{H}}(\mathcal{{G}}(g^{-1}\cdot\upchi))
corresponding to the nilpotent construction data
g^{-1}\cdot\upchi
. In other words, we can simply assume that the action
H_{\Upphi}\curvearrowright B_{\Upphi}
itself is a nilpotent construction action. Going further, recall that nilpotent construction actions are decomposable (see the discussion after Theorem
3.5
). But decomposable C1-actions on reducible symmetric spaces of noncompact type always arise via nontrivial canonical extension (see
[
DRDVO23
, Lem. 4.1]
). This means that
B_{\Upphi}
has to be irreducible. In other words,
\Upphi
has to be a connected subset of the Dynkin diagram
\DD
of
M
. But then
\Upphi
is contained in a single connected component
\DD_{i}
of
\DD
, which translates to the fact that
B_{\Upphi}
has to be contained in a single de Rham factor
M_{i}
of
M
. We may also assume that the singular orbit of
H_{\Upphi}
is not totally geodesic, otherwise the action
H\curvearrowright M
is once again given by part (
) of the
Main theorem
. In particular,
B_{\Upphi}
cannot be isometric to
\mathbb{R}H^{n}
(see
[
BT13
, Th. 6.1]
).

Next, suppose
B_{\Upphi}
is isometric to
\mathbb{C}H^{n+1},\,\mathbb{H}H^{n+1}\,(n\geq 1),\,\mathbb{O}H^{2},\,\mathrm{{G}}_{2}^{2}/\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{SO}(4),
or
\mathrm{{G}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})/\mathrm{{G}}_{2}
. According to the solution of the nilpotent construction problem on these spaces in
[
BB01
,
BT07
,
DRDVRV21
,
BT13
]
, the action of
H_{\Upphi}
must belong to
\mathcal{{M}}_{\mathbb{C}H^{n+1}},\,\mathcal{{M}}_{\mathbb{H}H^{n+1}},
or
\mathcal{{M}}_{\mathbb{O}H^{2}}
, or else it must be orbit-equivalent to the action of
H_{2,0}
(see the discussion preceding the
Main theorem
to recall this notation). Using the classification of symmetric spaces of noncompact type (see
[
BCO16
, pp. 336–340]
), we can deduce that the de Rham factor
M_{i}
has to be as described in parts (
)-(
) of (
) in the
Main theorem
. For example, if
B_{\Upphi}
is isometric to
\mathbb{H}H^{n+1}
, then the Dynkin diagram
\DD_{i}
of
M_{i}
has to contain
\mathrm{{BC}}_{1}
as its subdiagram, and the multiplicities of the corresponding simple root
\upalpha
and its double
2\upalpha
have to be
4n
and
3
, respectively. From the classification, we see that
M_{i}
must then be isometric to the noncompact quaternionic Grassmannian
\Gr^{*}(k,\mathbb{H}^{2k+n})
for some
k\geq 1
. Note that the restrictions on
k
and
n
in (
)-(
) come from the facts that
\mathcal{{M}}_{\mathbb{C}H^{2}}=\varnothing
and
\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{SO}(3,\mathbb{H})/\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{U}(3)\simeq\mathbb{C}H^{3}
. It remains to notice that the canonical extension of
H_{\Upphi}\curvearrowright B_{\Upphi}
to
M
can be regarded as the product of its canonical extension to
M_{i}
with transitive actions on all the other de Rham factors (
[
DRDVO23
, Lem. 4.1, 4.2]
). We conclude that the original action
H\curvearrowright M
is described in part (
) of the
Main theorem
in this case. Consequently, we may assume that
B_{\Upphi}
has rank at least two and is not a
\mathrm{{G}}_{2}
-space. By combining Propositions
6.16
,
6.22
,
6.24
, and
6.25
, we deduce that the action
H_{\Upphi}\curvearrowright B_{\Upphi}
has no other options but to arise from nontrivial canonical extension or have a totally geodesic singular orbit—and both of these possibilities have already been excluded. This concludes the proof of the
Main theorem
. ∎
